Airy β line ensemble and its Laplace transform
Vadim Gorin, Jiaming Xu, Lingfu Zhang

Abstract
The Airy β line ensemble is a random collection of continuous curves, which should serve as a universal edge scaling limit in problems related to eigenvalues of random matrices and models of 2d statistical mechanics. This line ensemble unifies many existing universal objects including Tracy-Widom distributions, eigenvalues of the Stochastic Airy Operator, Airy 2 process from the KPZ theory. Here β > 0 \beta>0 is a real parameter governing the strength of the repulsion between the curves. We introduce and characterize the Airy β line ensemble in terms of the Laplace transform, by producing integral formulas for its joint multi-time moments. We prove two asymptotic theorems for each β > 0 \beta>0 : the trajectories of the largest eigenvalues in the Dyson Brownian Motion converge to the Airy β line ensemble; the extreme particles in the G β \beta E corners process converge to the same limit. The proofs are based on the convergence of random walk expansions for the multi-time moments of prelimit objects towards their Brownian counterparts. The expansions are produced through Dunkl differential-difference operators acting on multivariate Bessel generating functions.



1
Introduction

1.1
Overview

Following the seminal work of Dyson, Mehta, Wigner, and others in 1950s-60s and the influential papers of Tracy and Widom in the 1990s, the central question of the random matrix theory is to describe the probability distributions for the scaling limits of eigenvalues of very large random self-adjoint matrices. The original focus (see
[
Meh04
]
for the review) has been on the eigenvalues in the bulk of the spectrum, motivated by the nuclear physics, where spacings between such eigenvalues served as a model for spacing between energy levels in heavy nuclei; later the same bulk eigenvalues were discovered to be connected to the statistics of the zeros of the Riemann zeta-function (see, e.g.
[
Mon73
,
BK99
]
), thus, reinvigorating the interest to the topic. More recently, the largest eigenvalues attracted lots of attention, driven by their limiting distributions turning out to be relevant for the 2d-statistical mechanics (see, e.g.
[
Joh18
,
Gor21
]
), to interacting particle systems and growth models in the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class (see e.g.
[
Cor12
,
BG16a
]
), to asymptotic representation theory (see, e.g.,
[
Oko00
,
BOO00
,
JS16
,
Ahn23
]
), and to the statistical testing procedures (see, e.g.,
[
Joh06
]
). One remarkable discovery in the random matrix theory was the phenomenon of
universality
, which is reminiscent of the universality of the Gaussian distribution in classical probability theory: it turns out that there is a very short list of distinguished limiting probability distributions (with the sine-process and Tracy-Widom distribution being two most celebrated examples) and the asymptotic behavior for each of the hundreds of systems, ranging from different classes of random matrices and to the stochastic PDEs and objects of the number theory, is governed by one of these few distributions, see, e.g.,
[
Dei06
,
TV14
,
EY17
]
.

The main goal of this paper is to construct and investigate the most recent addition to this family of the universal limit distributions — the Airy
β
line ensemble: for each
\beta>0
, this is an infinite collection of random continuous curves
\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\geq\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\geq\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{3}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\dots
. For special choices of
\beta
and by passing to the marginals,
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
specializes to several important objects:
\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1}(0)
is the general
\beta
Tracy-Widom distribution; for
\beta=2
, the Airy
2
line ensemble
\{\mathcal{A}^{2}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
plays a central role in the KPZ universality class (see, e.g., the introduction in
[
AH23
]
and references therein), it is also the same object as the extended Airy determinantal point process;
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(0)\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
are eigenvalues of the Stochastic Airy Operator
[
ES07
,
RRV07
]
; for
\beta=1,2
the Airy
β
line ensemble is identified with the spectral edge for corners of time-dependent Wigner matrices
[
Sod15
]
. Hence, our
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
combines all these objects and gives them a unified treatment. We prove two main types of results for the Airy
β
line ensemble:

•

We characterize
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, by giving integral expressions for the moments of its (random) Laplace transforms, and deriving the continuity of the trajectories (see Theorem
1.4
).

•

We show that this object appears in two distinct edge limit transitions: for the largest particles in the general
\beta
Dyson Brownian Motion (see also
[
Lan20
,
HZ24
]
) and for the Gaussian
\beta
corners process.

Our approach to the proofs suggests numerous generalizations, and therefore predicts universality of
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
.
Towards this universality, in a companion paper
[
KX24
]
, the approach using actions of Dunkl operators similar to this text is applied to extend the one-time
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(0)\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
edge universality to certain additions of Gaussian and Laguerre
\beta
-ensembles.
In another paper
[
HZ24
]
a general framework of proving convergence to
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
is developed, and is applied to various continuous random processes.

1.2
Beta ensembles and their
2d
–extensions

The starting point for our text is the Gaussian
\beta
Ensemble. It depends on an integer
N=1,2,\dots,
and two parameters
\beta,\tau>0
and is a probability distribution on
N
–tuples of reals
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\dots>\lambda_{N}
of density proportional to:

\mathcal{P}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})\sim\prod_{i<j}|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|^{\beta}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\tau}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\lambda_{i})^{2}\right).

(1)

For
\beta=1,2,4
, the formula (
1
) records the eigenvalue distribution of
N\times N
self-adjoint Wigner matrices with i.i.d. mean
0
Gaussian real/complex/quaternionic matrix elements, respectively, see, e.g.,
[
Meh04
,
For10
]
. For general values of
\beta
, (
1
) are eigenvalues of tridiagonal matrices of Dumitriu and Edelman
[
DE02
]
. The computation of the normalization constant which makes (
1
) a probability distribution is the Mehta integral evaluation, which is a particular case of the famous Selberg integral, cf.
[
FW08
]
. Other points of view on (
1
) connect it to Calogero–Sutherland quantum many–body system and to Coulomb log-gases, cf.
[
For11
,
Ser24
]
.

A powerful strategy for studying
N\to\infty
asymptotics of the random matrix-type distributions uses (
1
) as the basic ingredient: we first investigate various limits of the Gaussian
\beta
Ensemble by one set of tools and then use another set of tools to prove the universality, i.e. to compare other models with the Gaussian
\beta
Ensemble and to show that the limits should be the same. For classical values
\beta=1,2,4
, the formulas for the limits of (
1
) both in the bulk and at the edge are quite old (see, e.g.,
[
Meh04
, Chapters 6,7,8]
or
[
For10
, Chapter 7]
and references therein); their development was based on the theory of determinantal and Pfaffian point processes. More recently they were shown to extend universally to the log-gases (of the form (
1
) with other potentials replacing
\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{2\tau}(\lambda)^{2}\right)
), to Wigner matrices with generic matrix elements, cf.
[
EY12
,
TV14
]
, and to many other random matrix models not mentioned here. The edge and bulk scaling limits of (
1
) for general
\beta>0
were found in
[
ES07
,
RRV07
]
and
[
VV09
]
, respectively, based on the tridiagonal matrix model of
[
DE02
]
. Subsequently, they were extended to log-gases with more general potential in
[
BEY14a
,
BEY14b
,
BFG15
,
KRV16
]
, to discrete log-gases in
[
GH19
]
, and to some
\beta
matrix additions in
[
KX24
]
.

The focus of this text is not on the Gaussian
\beta
Ensemble per se, but rather on its two-dimensional extensions. There are two natural ways to add a second dimension to (
1
): we can either allow
\tau
to vary, or we can allow
N
to vary. Somewhat surprisingly, when it comes to edge limits, both approaches lead to exactly the same limiting objects, as our Theorems
1.5
and
1.6
below indicate.

The dynamical point of view on (
1
) goes back to
[
Dys62
]
and interprets
\tau
as a time parameter. For that we consider an
N
-dimensional diffusion known as the Dyson Brownian Motion (see

[
Meh04
, Chapter 9]
,
[
AGZ10
, Section 4.3]
and references therein)

Definition 1.1
.

For a real
\beta>0
, the Dyson Brownian Motion (DBM) is the (unique,
strong) solution
1
1

1

For
0<\beta<1
a special care is needed, because particles start to collide, see
[
CL97
]
.
of the system of
N
stochastic differential equations

\mathrm{d}Y_{i}(\tau)=\frac{\beta}{2}\,\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{Y_{i}(\tau)-Y_{j}(\tau)}\,\mathrm{d}\tau+\mathrm{d}W_{i}(\tau),\quad i=1,2,\ldots,N,

(2)

where
W_{1},W_{2},\ldots,W_{N}
are independent standard Brownian motions.

If one starts
(
2
) from zero initial condition, then the distribution of the solution at time
\tau

is given by (
1
). For the special value
\beta=2
, the
diffusion (
2
) admits two probabilistic interpretations.
First, it can be identified with a
system of
N
independent standard Brownian motions conditioned never to collide, thereby making it a key tool to analyze KPZ universality class models (see e.g.,
[
DOV22
,
DZ21
]
). Besides, it also can be viewed as the evolution of the eigenvalues of a Hermitian
random matrix whose elements evolve as independent Brownian motions. Similarly, for
\beta=1
and
4
, (
2
) is the evolution of eigenvalues for self-adjoint symmetric and quaternionic matrices of Brownian motions, respectively. The DBM can be also linked to prominent conformally invariant objects: it is the driving function for multiple SLEs in a simultaneous parameterization; this observation can be traced back to
[
Car03
]
2
2

2

See also
[
Car04
]
for the correction of the correspondence between the parameters.
, and it was made more precise recently, see, e.g.,
[
HL21
,
WY23
,
FWY24
]
. We recall that Schramm-Loewner Evolutions (SLEs) appear as conformally invariant scaling limits of interfaces in various critical models of 2d statistical mechanics, see, e.g.,
[
TW04
]
. SLEs depend on a parameter
\kappa
, which is related to the parameter of the DBM via
\beta=8/\kappa
. In particular, the relevant values of the parameter go beyond the classical
\beta=1,2,4
, for instance, the celebrated Ising model corresponds to
\kappa=3
, i.e.
\beta=8/3
, cf.
[
CDCH+14
]
, while self-avoiding random walks should be related to
\kappa=8/3
, i.e.
\beta=3
, cf.
[
LSW04
]
.

For an alternative 2d-extension of (
1
), we define a
Gelfand–Tsetlin pattern of rank
N
with top row
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}
as an array
\{y_{i}^{n}\}_{1\leq i\leq n\leq N}
of real numbers satisfying

y^{n+1}_{i}\geq y^{n}_{i}\geq y^{n+1}_{i+1}
and
(y_{1}^{N},y_{2}^{N},\dots,y_{N}^{N})=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})
. Denote by
\mathcal{G}_{N}
the space of all Gelfand–Tsetlin patterns of rank
N
with arbitrary top rows.

Definition 1.2
.

Fix
\beta>0
.
3
3

3

Compared to
[
GS15
, Definition 2.18]
or
[
BGCG22
, Definition 2.1]
, we set
\beta=2\theta
.
The
\beta
-corners process with top row
\lambda_{1}>\dots>\lambda_{N}

is a random array

\{y^{n}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n\leq N}\in\mathcal{G}_{N}
with top row
(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})
, in which
the
N(N-1)/2
coordinates
y_{i}^{n}
,
1\leq i\leq n<N
, have the probability density

\frac{1}{Z_{N;\beta}}\cdot\prod_{n=1}^{N-1}\left[\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}|y_{j}^{n}-y_{i}^{n}|^{2-\beta}\right]\cdot\left[\prod_{a=1}^{n}\prod_{b=1}^{n+1}|y^{n}_{a}-y^{n+1}_{b}|^{\beta/2-1}\right],

(3)

where
Z_{N;\beta}
is the normalization constant:

Z_{N;\beta}=\left[\prod_{n=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(\beta/2)^{n}}{\Gamma(k\beta/2)}\right]\cdot\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{i}|^{\beta-1}.

(4)

By taking limits in the space of probability measures on
\mathcal{G}_{N}
, we can allow equalities and extend the definition to arbitrary
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}
.

Definition 1.3
.

The G
\beta
E corners process of rank
N
and variance
\tau
is an
N(N+1)/2
dimensional random array
\{y^{n}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n\leq N}\in\mathcal{G}_{N}
, such that the top row
(y_{1}^{N},y_{2}^{N},\dots,y_{N}^{N})
is distributed according to (
1
) and the conditional distribution of
\{y^{n}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n<N}
given the top-row is that of the
\beta
–corners process of Definition
1.2
.

For
\beta=1,2,4
, the distribution of G
\beta
E corners process coincides with that of eigenvalues of top-left
k\times k
corners,
k=1,\dots,N
, of the Wigner matrix with Gaussian real, or complex, or quaternionic matrix elements, respectively: the ideas of this computation go back to
[
GN50
, Section 9.3]
, with more recent treatments in
[
Bar01
,
Ner03
]
.
Definition

1.3
is consistent
4
4

4

See, e.g., discussion after Definition 1.1 in
[
GS15
]
.
over
N
: for
M<N
, the distribution of
\{y^{k}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k\leq M}
is again the G
\beta
E corners process (of rank
M
and the same variance
t
). In this way, one can also set
N=\infty
and think about infinite arrays
\{y^{n}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
; these infinite random infinite arrays are examples of extreme ergodic Gibbs measures and play a role in the classification theorems for the latter, see
[
OV96
,
AN21
,
BR24
]
. For the special value
\beta=2
, the conditional density in (
3
) is uniform, and the corresponding GUE-corners process (first studied in
[
JN06
,
OR06
]
) is universally appearing as a scaling model of many models of
2d
statistical mechanics, see
[
Gor21
, Lecture 20]
,
[
GN23
]
, and references therein; some traces of this universality also exist for general values of
\beta
, cf.
[
Cue21
]
.

1.3
Edge limits via the Airy
β
line ensemble

We are interested in the largest particles (eigenvalues) in either of the DBM or the G
\beta
E corners process, and we now introduce the limiting object governing their asymptotic behavior.

Theorem 1.4
.

For any
\beta>0
, there is a unique family of time-dependent (stationary) continuous random processes — denoted by
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
— such that they are ordered
\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1}\geq\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{2}\geq\dots
; and for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, and
\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}
, the joint moment for the Laplace transforms

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp(\mathbf{k}_{\ell}\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell})/2)\right)\right]

(5)

equals
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
, which is defined below in
Definition

2.12
5
5

5

The factor of dividing by
2
in (
5
) is to simply the expressions in
Definition

2.12
, avoiding various extra factors there. It also comes up naturally in the edge scaling limits.
.

We call such
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
the Airy
β
line ensemble, and we also refer to the individual paths as particles.
6
6

6

Following our proofs, each
\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}
is a continuous function from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
, with topology which makes each
[-\infty,a)
,
a\in\mathbb{R}
an open set.
However, it is implied by
[
Lan20
]
(
\beta\geq 1
) and
[
HZ24
]
(any
\beta>0
) that, almost surely,
\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\in\mathbb{R}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, eventually the potential presence of
-\infty
can be ignored.

The expectations in (
5
) can be rewritten as Laplace transforms of the correlation functions for the Airy
β
line ensemble, although extraction of compact expressions for correlation functions from (
5
) is a separate task to be done, cf.
[
Oko02
]
,
[
BG16b
]
for computations of such type at
\beta=2
. We plan to address this in the future work.

For
\beta=1,2
representations of (
5
) as infinite sums of integrals were previously studied in
[
Sod15
,
JS16
]
(following the earlier results on fixed time marginals, corresponding to
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}=\dots=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}=0
in (
5
) in
[
Sos99
,
Oko00
,
Oko02
]
); the answers in these articles have distinct combinatorial structures for
\beta=1
and
\beta=2
. On the other hand, our expressions for
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
are analytic functions in
\beta
.
Another approach at
\beta=2
is to characterize the Airy
2
line ensemble in terms of its correlation functions and determinantal formulas for the latter written in terms of the extended Airy kernel
[
PS02
]
; the interplay between determinantal formulas and combinatorial expressions for (
5
) was explored in
[
Oko02
]
to compute certain intersection numbers. Yet another approach to the Airy
2
line ensemble is to characterize it through the Brownian Gibbs property, see
[
CH14
,
AH23
]
. It is unknown how to extend either of the two last approaches to the general values of
\beta
. Very recently
[
EJN24
, Problem 4]
suggested an attractive and simple conjectural definition for the Airy
β
ensemble; though, at the end of Section 1 the authors report that “numerical experiments do not yet provide strong evidence for these conjectures”. It is yet to be understood whether our Theorem
1.4
agrees with the conjectures of
[
EJN24
]
.

The fixed time
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}=\dots=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}=0
marginals of (
5
) were previously computed in
[
GS18
]
leading to different expressions and using a method based on tridiagonal matrices, which does not generalize to the multi-time case. The only path to the Airy
β
line ensemble existing before this work was an approach through an infinite system of stochastic differential equations, making sense of (
2
) at
N=\infty
. The initial attempt on this method goes back to
[
OT14
]
for
\beta=1,2,4
; more recently results for all
\beta\geq 1
were presented in
[
Lan20
]
in the language of individual particles; and in the most recent (simultaneous with the current paper)
[
HZ24
]
in the language of an SDE satisfied by the Stieltjes transform. One inevitable difficulty of this method is that directly solving the resulting system of SDEs becomes a new (unsolved) hard task. On the other hand, Theorem
1.4
provides direct formulas for the Airy
β
line ensemble.

Our following two results demonstrate the appearance of the Airy
β
line ensemble as an edge scaling limit.

Theorem 1.5
.

For fixed
\beta>0
, let
\{y_{i}^{n}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
be the (infinite rank) G
\beta
E-corners process of
Definition

1.3
with variance
1
.
As
N\to\infty
, the process

(\boldsymbol{\tau},i)\mapsto 2N^{2/3}\left(\frac{y_{i}^{\lfloor N-\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3}\rfloor}}{\sqrt{2\beta(N-\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3})}}-1\right)

converges to the Airy
β
line ensemble in distribution in the topology of uniform convergence over compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
for
\boldsymbol{\tau}
–variable and jointly in finitely many
i
.

Theorem 1.6
.

For fixed
\beta>0
, let
\{Y_{i}(\tau)\}_{1\leq i\leq N,\tau>0}
be the
N
dimensional DBM of
Definition

1.1
started from zero initial condition.
As
N\to\infty
, the process

(\boldsymbol{\tau},i)\mapsto 2N^{2/3}\left(\frac{Y_{i}(2N/\beta+2\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3}/\beta)}{2\sqrt{(N+\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3})N}}-1\right)

converges to the Airy
β
line ensemble in distribution in the topology of uniform convergence over compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
for
\boldsymbol{\tau}
–variable and jointly in finitely many
i
.

Remark 1.7
.

One can glue together the corners process and the DBM in a three-dimensional object — time evolution of a
\mathcal{G}_{N}
array, see
[
GS15
]
. Most of our results extend to
monotone
2d
–sections
of these
3d
object: we can deal with the joint distribution of the marginals at points
(N_{1},\tau_{1})
,
(N_{2},\tau_{2})
,…,
(N_{k},\tau_{k})
, with
N_{1}\geq N_{2}\geq\dots\geq N_{k}
,
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\dots\leq\tau_{k}
, but not with the full distribution.

The proof of Theorems
1.5
and
1.6
is split into two parts: in Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
we prove convergence of joint moments of Laplace transforms towards the expressions of Theorem
1.4
. Then in Section
5
we deduce the distributional convergence in the uniform in compacts topology in
\boldsymbol{\tau}
.

We refer to
[
Lan20
]
and
[
HZ24
]
for alternative approaches to studying the multi-time edge limits of the DBM. On the other hand, no other approaches exist for investigating the edge limits of G
\beta
E-corners process at general
\beta>0
.

The coincidence of the edge scaling limits for the G
\beta
E corners process and for the DBM might look mysterious. At
\beta=2
such a coincidence (first results of this type go back to
[
FS03
,
Joh05
]
, where Airy
2
line ensemble was found in tiling and growth models — and see
[
NJ06
,
AG22
]
and
[
Gor21
, Lecture 20]
for connections between tilings and GUE corners process) can be explained through the Brownian Gibbs property: one naturally expects both limiting objects to satisfy it and then
[
AH23
]
shows that there is only one such object. For other values of
\beta
, one explanation is that both scaling limits should satisfy an infinite dimensional SDE, which can be viewed as either the
N\to\infty
limit of (
2
), or a Markov property of the G
\beta
E corners process (which can be seen from
Definition

1.2
).
From a general perspective, this coincidence serves as an indication of the
universality
of the Airy
β
line ensemble. The coincidence itself was previously known for
\beta=1
due to
[
Sod15
]
and for
\beta=\infty
due to
[
GK24
]
.

We expect that the Airy
β
line ensemble is also the edge limit of many other general
\beta
objects, such as: Laguerre and Jacobi corners as in
[
BP08
,
DW09
,
FN11
]
and
[
BG15
,
Sun16
]
; Macdonald processes as in
[
BC14
]
; Jack-Gibbs measures as in
[
GS15
,
DK24
]
and multi-level versions of discrete
\beta
–ensembles as in
[
GH19
]
; various non-intersecting random walks as in
[
Hua21a
,
Hua21b
]
; DBM with general potentials as in
[
LLX13
,
HL19
]
; Laguerre and Jacobi dynamics as in
[
Bru91
,
KO01
]
and
[
Dem10
]
; additions of Gaussian and Laguerre
\beta
ensembles as in
[
KX24
]
.
It would be extremely interesting to create proper tools for proving them, thus developing the universality of the Airy
β
line ensemble.
One approach is initiated in
[
HZ24
]
: it consists in first proving tightness and then checking that any subsequential limit satisfies an SDE, which also characterizes the Airy
β
line ensemble.
This strategy is being successfully applied in
[
HZ24
]
to establish the Airy
β
line ensemble limit for DBM with general potentials, and the Laguerre and Jacobi dynamics.

1.4
Methodology

The classical approach for finding the local limits of eigenvalue distributions in random matrix theory goes back to the 60s and is based on the determinantal/Pfaffian formulas for the correlation functions; it is extensively covered in textbooks, see
[
Meh04
,
For10
]
. This method was very successful for
\beta=1,2,4
, but it failed to extend to generic
\beta>0
. An alternative proposed in
[
DE02
,
ES07
]
was to use tridiagonal matrix models for all
\beta>0
and this had led to computations of the scaling limits of
\beta
-ensembles in the bulk and in the edge through stochastic objects such as Brownian carousel
[
VV09
]
and Stochastic Airy Operator
[
RRV07
]
. Adding time (as in Definition
1.1
) or matrix size
N
(as in Definition
1.2
) to these results turned out to be extremely difficult: the tridiagonalization procedure of
[
DE02
]
does not agree with these two-dimensional extensions (as discussed, e.g. after (9) in
[
Voi23
]
), while alternative matrix models (cf.
[
ABG12
,
AG13
,
HP17
]
) do not pave a way for the computations of local limits. The most recent attempt to add time to the tridiagonalization procedure is
[
EJN24
]
, yet computing the edge scaling limit remains a hard open problem in that paper as well.

A very different approach for computing edge limits was pioneered in
[
Sos99
]
(see also
[
FK81
,
BY88
,
BdMS95
,
SS98a
,
SS98b
]
): it suggests investigating the traces of very high powers of matrices (equivalently, eigenvalue power sums of large degrees). In subsequent years, these ideas were adapted to various classes of random matrices, e.g. in
[
Péc09
,
Sod14
,
GS18
]
, to Plancherel measure for symmetric groups in
[
Oko00
,
JS16
]
, to tensor products of representations of unitary groups in
[
Ahn23
]
. In order to use this method for the Airy
β
line ensemble, the main challenge was to find a combinatorial expression for the expectations of (products of) power sums of eigenvalues, which simultaneously:

•

Captures the information on multi-level/time distributions, as in Definitions
1.1
,
1.2
;

•

Is amenable to large
N
, large power limits, necessary to probe the edge.

It took us more than ten years and several attempts to figure out a path forward. We previously tried: an approach to expected power sums through Macdonald difference operators of
[
BG15
]
(obstacle: the combinatorial sums do not seem to be absolutely convergent in the large power limit), an approach to expected power sums through Negut’s difference operators
[
GZ18
]
(obstacle: large powers required contour integrals of growing dimensions), and approach through powers of tridiagonal matrices
[
GS18
]
(obstacle: unclear how to add multilevel/time structures). More recently, we investigated in
[
BGCG22
,
Xu23
]
the Laws of Large Numbers for empirical distributions of the eigenvalues of
\beta
–ensembles through the
Dunkl differential-difference operators
of
[
Dun89
]
. The key observation underlying the proofs in the present article is that the expressions for the expectations of the product of the power sums obtained through the Dunkl operators fit the bill and satisfy both desired properties. Compared to the fixed degree power sums calculation in
[
BGCG22
,
Xu23
]
, the degrees of power sums are growing with
N
(more precisely, of order
O(N^{\frac{2}{3}})
) in order to capture the edge behavior, which makes taking asymptotic of their expectation much more involved. After significant technical effort, we manage to use these operators to produce combinatorial expressions involving sums over decorated positive random walks for the prelimiting objects and then discover that the expressions essentially survive in the large
N
limit: random walks are asymptotically replaced by Brownian motions, while “decorations” remain similar, and eventually we arrive at a series expansion in terms of “number of decorations”. To our best knowledge, such combinatorial expression is novel, and gives explicit formulas even new for the one-time marginal
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(0)\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
. Simultaneously, similar developments and expressions were achieved in
[
KX24
]
in the study of limits of expectations of eigenvalue power sums for additions of Gaussian and Laguerre
\beta
-ensembles.
