Notations.

For any real numbers
a<b
, we denote
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket=[a,b]\cap\mathbb{Z}
.
We will also use Pochhammer symbols
(x)_{n}=x(x+1)\cdots(x+n-1)
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
For a function
f
whose domain of definition contains
x\in\mathbb{R}
and a left (resp. right) neighborhood of
x
, we use
f(x-)
(resp.
f(x+)
) to denote the left limit of
f
at
x
, if it exists.
For two real numbers
x
and
y
, we sometimes denote
x\wedge y=\min(x,y)
, to make formulas shorter. If
y=\varnothing
, then
\min(x,y)=\max(x,y)=x
. For a set
A
, we let
|A|
denote the number of elements in it.

Throughout the rest of this paper, we fix
\beta>0
and treat it as a constant.

2
Formulas for joint moments

In this section, we present formulas for
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
of Theorem
1.4
. We will later prove the convergence of moments of G
\beta
E corners process and DBM towards these quantities in
Theorems

4.1
and
4.2
by demonstrating that the discrete versions of the constructions of this section converge in a diffusive scaling limit to the continuous versions introduced here.

2.1
Blocks

Our expression involves a combinatorial structure, which we call
blocks
and illustrate in Figure
1
.
Blocks consist of several objects: a block process, a virtual block process, and a block height. Informally, block process and virtual block process are piecewise-constant constraints on a function, which is being conditioned to stay above them (later this function will become a Brownian bridge). The block height represents the values of this function in the most important points.

We next give formal definitions sequentially.

Definition 2.1
.

For an interval
[a,b]
, we call a function
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
a
block function
, if it is right-continuous on
[a,b)
, and
f(a)=f(b)=f(b-)=0
, and the support of
f
is connected, and the image
f([a,b])
is a finite set.
We denote by
\mathfrak{B}([a,b])
the space of all block functions
f
on
[a,b]
.

Clearly, every block function is a finite and positive linear combination of indicators of intervals.

We next define block processes, as families of block functions with certain constraints.

Definition 2.2
.

Take
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\vec{\mathbf{k}}=(\mathbf{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{k}_{m})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
, and denote
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{k}_{\ell^{\prime}}
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
.
A
block process with times
\vec{\mathbf{k}}

is a family
\vec{\mathbf{p}}=\{\mathbf{p}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m+\mathbf{u}}
, where
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and each
\mathbf{p}_{j}:[0,\mathbf{Q}_{m}]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a function satisfying the following:

•

for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
\mathbf{p}_{\ell}\in\mathfrak{B}([0,\mathbf{Q}_{m}])
, and
\mathbf{p}_{\ell}=0
on
[\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1},\mathbf{Q}_{m}]
;

•

for each
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
,
\mathbf{p}_{j}\in\mathfrak{B}([0,\mathbf{Q}_{m}])
and
\mathbf{p}_{j}
is not identical zero.

•

for each
j\in\llbracket 1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
,
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
\mathbf{p}_{j}
is left-continuous at
x=\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}
unless
j=\ell
.

•

regularity condition spelled in Definition
2.3
below.
7
7

7

Here is some intuition for the definition of a block process. It corresponds to the degrees of polynomials in Dunkl operators expansion, as shown later in
Section

4
. The values of
\mathbf{p}_{\ell}
at points
[0,\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}]
for
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
relate to the degrees of variables
x_{i}
with indices matching one of the Dunkl operators
\mathfrak{D}_{i}^{N}
(to be introduced in
Section

3
) we are expanding, and
\mathbf{p}_{j}
for
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
are the degrees of the other variables. In particular, the variable corresponding to the first operator would have zero degree after
\mathbf{Q}_{0}=0
, so
\mathbf{p}_{1}
is identical zero.

In particular, the definition implies that
\mathbf{p}_{1}
is always identical zero. Given a block process and
\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, we also denote

\mathbf{p}^{\ell}=\sum_{j=\ell+1}^{m+\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{p}_{j}.

(6)

The block functions are encoded by the jumps at all the discontinuity points, for which we also set up notations. For a block process
\vec{\mathbf{p}}
as defined above, and
j\in\llbracket 1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
,
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, we let

\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}=\{x\in(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}):\mathbf{p}_{j}(x-)\neq\mathbf{p}_{j}(x)\},\qquad\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}=|\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}|\qquad\text{ and}

(7)

\boldsymbol{\delta}=\sum_{j=1}^{m+\mathbf{u}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell},\quad\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m+\mathbf{u}}\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}.

(8)

Definition 2.3
.

For a block process
\vec{\mathbf{p}}
as defined above, we say it is
regular
, if all
\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}
are mutually disjoint
8
8

8

From definition of
\mathbf{\Delta}
it also follows that the set
\mathbf{\Delta}
is disjoint with
\{0,\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2},\dots,\mathbf{Q}_{m}\}
.
, and
\min\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}<\min\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}_{j^{\prime},\ell}
for all
m+1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq m+\mathbf{u}
.

The condition on
\min\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}
is introduced for convenience of enumerations: it breaks the symmetry between
\mathbf{p}_{j}
for
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
. We note that these requirements can be replaced by any other ordering. Throughout the text, by a block process, we always mean a regular one.

Another ingredient we need is virtual block process.

Definition 2.4
.

For a block process
\vec{\mathbf{p}}
, a compatible
virtual block process
is a vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}=(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{m})\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\varnothing\})^{m}
satisfying the following conditions: for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,

•

if
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\neq\varnothing
, then
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}<\min\bigl(\bigcup_{j=1}^{m+\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\}\bigr)
;

•

if
\ell=1
, or
\ell>1
and
\mathbf{p}_{\ell}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}-)=0
, then
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}=\varnothing
.

The virtual block process can be treated as a part of the block process from the previous definitions, which will be treated in a slightly different way in the following constructions.

The next ingredient is “block height”. This object originates from considering a Brownian bridge, conditioned to stay above the function
\mathbf{p}^{0}
coming from the block process and conditioned on certain additional inequalities coming from the virtual block process. We are interested in the values of this conditional Brownian bridge at the various points of time appearing in the previous definitions, cf. Figure
1
, which are all encoded in the following definition.

Definition 2.5
.

For a block process
\vec{\mathbf{p}}
and compatible virtual block process
\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}
, a compatible
block height
is a function
\mathbf{H}:\mathbf{\Delta}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\mid\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket\}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\mid\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\,\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, such that

•

\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})=\mathbf{p}^{0}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}-)
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
\mathbf{H}(0)=0
;

•

\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell})=\mathbf{p}^{0}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}-)
for each
\ell
with
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\neq\varnothing
;

•

for all
x\in\mathbf{\Delta}
, we have
\mathbf{H}(x)\geq\max\bigl(\mathbf{p}^{0}(x),\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)\bigr)
;

•

if
j\in\llbracket 1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
and
x\in\mathbf{\Delta}
is a discontinuity point for
\mathbf{p}_{j}
, then the jump
\mathbf{p}_{j}(x)-\mathbf{p}_{j}(x-)
and the difference
\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)-\mathbf{p}_{j}(x)
have the same sign;

•

if
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}=\varnothing
for
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, then
\mathbf{H}\bigl(\min\bigl(\bigcup_{j=1}^{m+\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\}\bigr)\bigr)\geq\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1})
.

In particular, if
\mathbf{p}_{j}(x-)>0
and
\mathbf{p}_{j}(x)=0
for
x\in\mathbf{\Delta}
, then we must have
\mathbf{H}(x)=\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)
, because
\mathbf{H}(x)\geq\max\bigl(\mathbf{p}^{0}(x),\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)\bigr)
on one hand, and
\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)\leq\mathbf{p}_{j}(x)
on the other hand.

0
\mathbf{Q}_{1}
\mathbf{Q}_{2}
\mathbf{Q}_{3}
\mathbf{p}_{1}

0
\mathbf{Q}_{1}
\mathbf{Q}_{2}
\mathbf{Q}_{3}
\mathbf{p}_{2}

0
\mathbf{Q}_{1}
\mathbf{Q}_{2}
\mathbf{Q}_{3}
\mathbf{p}_{3}

0
\mathbf{Q}_{1}
\mathbf{Q}_{2}
\mathbf{Q}_{3}
\mathbf{p}_{4}

\mathbf{Q}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{2}
\mathbf{Q}_{2}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{3}
0
\mathbf{Q}_{1}
\mathbf{Q}_{2}
\mathbf{Q}_{3}
\mathbf{p}^{0}=\mathbf{p}_{1}+\mathbf{p}_{2}+\mathbf{p}_{3}+\mathbf{p}_{4}
\mathbf{H}
\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}
Brownian bridge

Figure 1
:
An illustration of blocks, with
m=3
,
\mathbf{u}=1
,
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{1}=\varnothing
.

All the above objects combine together into “blocks”.

Definition 2.6
.

“Blocks” is a triplet
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
: block process, virtual block process, and compatible block height.
For each
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
, we let
\mathscr{K}=\mathscr{K}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
be the space of all blocks with times
\vec{\mathbf{k}}
.

2.2
Measure on blocks

We next fix
m
,
\vec{\mathbf{k}}
and equip
\mathscr{K}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
with a
\sigma
–finite measure. We recall the notations (
7
), (
8
).

Definition 2.7
.

The measure
\mathrm{d}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
on
\mathscr{K}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
views it as a disjoint union of subspaces with fixed values of
\mathbf{u}=0,1,\dots
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}=0,1,\dots
. For the fixed
(\mathbf{u},\boldsymbol{\delta})
the space is further partitioned according to the values of
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}
.
We equip each part with the product of Lebesgue measures over all parameters of blocks, except that whenever only a single value for a parameter is involved, then we assign weight
1
for this value and for
\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\varnothing\}
in the definition of virtual blocks,
\varnothing
comes with the Dirac delta measure of weight
1
, while
\mathbb{R}_{+}
comes with the Lebesgue measure.

Let us decode the definition in more detail. We start with fixing the values of non-negative integers
\mathbf{u}
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}
,
j=1,\dots,m+\mathbf{u}
,
\ell=1,\dots,m
. The measure
\mathrm{d}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
is separately defined for each choice of these values.
9
9

9

Therefore, the integral over this measure can be split into an infinite sum of integrals, where the summation goes over all possible values of
\mathbf{u}
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}
.
The space of all block processes with given
\mathbf{u}
,
\{\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}\}
can be parameterized with finitely many real numbers. For each
j\in\llbracket 1,\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
there are two options: either
\sum_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}=0
implying that
\mathbf{p}_{j}
is identical zero (and then there are no parameters to choose for it), or we should choose
\sum_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}+\sum_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}
reals to specify
\mathbf{p}_{j}
— the first sum counts the parameters encoding the positions of all discontinuity points for
\mathbf{p}_{j}
and the second sum counts the parameters encoding the values of the jumps at these points. For each
j\in\llbracket\mathbf{u}+1,\mathbf{u}+m\rrbracket
, the first
\mathbf{p}_{j}=0
case is prohibited by our definitions, while in the second case we should choose
\sum_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}+\sum_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}-1
reals to specify
\mathbf{p}_{j}
— we subtracted
1
because the last jump of
\mathbf{p}_{j}
,
j>m
, is predetermined by the condition that
\mathbf{p}_{j}
is left-continuous at
\mathbf{Q}_{m}
. We combine all parameters together in a vector of length
r
and equip the coordinates of this vector with the Lebesgue measure; if
r=0
(which is possible only when
\mathbf{u}=\boldsymbol{\delta}=0
), then there is a unique choice of the blocks structure and we equip it with weight
1
.

Next, when we specify the virtual block process, we need to choose an
m
–dimensional vector
\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}=(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{m})\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\varnothing\})^{m}
. The coordinates of this vector are equipped with Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
part and weight
1
(i.e. the Dirac delta measure of such weight) on
\varnothing
part.

Finally, we specify the block height, for which we need to choose
\boldsymbol{\delta}-\mathbf{u}
real parameters: in Definition
2.5
there is freedom in choosing the values of
\mathbf{H}
at the discontinuity points from the set
\mathbf{\Delta}
, and as remarked immediately after Definition
2.5
, at the last discontinuity point the inequalities uniquely determine the value
\mathbf{H}(x)=\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)
. (That remark does not apply at other points:
\mathbf{p}_{j}
does not take value
0
except to the left from the first discontinuity for all
j
and to the right of the last discontinuity for
j>m
.) We again equip the vector of these
\boldsymbol{\delta}-\mathbf{u}
real parameters with the Lebesgue measure.

Example 2.8
.

Take
m=1
,
\mathbf{u}=0
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}=0
. In this situation there is a unique blocks structure:
\mathbf{p}_{1}=0
,
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{1}=\varnothing
. This block structure comes with weight
1
.

In Figure
1
we have
m=3
,
\mathbf{u}=1
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1,1}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1,2}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1,3}=0
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{2,1}=1
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{2,2}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{2,3}=0
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{3,1}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{3,2}=1
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{3,3}=0
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{4,1}=0
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{4,2}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{4,3}=1
, leading to
\boldsymbol{\delta}=5
. For the block process, we choose two parameters for
\mathbf{p}_{2}
, four parameters for
\mathbf{p}_{3}
, and three parameters for
\mathbf{p}_{4}
. For the virtual block process, we have
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{1}=\varnothing
and choose two parameters
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{3}
. For the block height, we choose four parameters (they are blue dots strictly above
\mathbf{p}^{0}
in the bottom panel of Figure
1
).

2.3
Weight of blocks via Brownian bridges

The value of
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is an integral of a function
\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}:\mathscr{K}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]\to\mathbb{R}
.
Given the blocks
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
, this function can be roughly defined as follows.
Take a Brownian bridge on
[0,\mathbf{Q}_{m}]
, and consider the following events based on it (see the bottom panel of
Figure

1
): the trajectory is

(a)

equal to
\mathbf{H}
on the set where
\mathbf{H}
is defined

(b)

\geq\mathbf{p}^{0}
on
[0,\mathbf{Q}_{m}]
,

(c)

\geq\mathbf{p}^{0}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}-)
on
[\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}]
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
with
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\neq\varnothing
,

(d)

\geq\mathbf{p}^{0}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}-)
on
[\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1},\min\bigl(\bigcup_{j=1}^{m+\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\}\bigr)
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
with
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}=\varnothing
.

Then
\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]
is defined as the product of:

•

the ‘probability density’ of the intersection of the above events; and

•

the expectation of the exponentiated and multiplied by
\beta^{-1}
area between the Brownian bridge and
\mathbf{p}^{0}
, conditional on all of the above events; and

•

\pm 1
, depending on whether some jumps of
\mathbf{p}_{0}
are positive or negative.

We next give a detailed definition.
For this, we remind the reader of the following classical stochastic processes. For
W:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
being a standard Wiener process,

•

for an interval
[a,b]
, and
g,h\in\mathbb{R}
,
x\mapsto B(x)=W(x-a)+g+\frac{x-a}{b-a}(-W(b-a)+h-g)
is a Brownian bridge on
[a,b]
, with
B(a)=g
and
B(b)=h
;

•

for
W
conditional on
W>-\epsilon
on
[0,\epsilon^{-1}]
, its
\epsilon\to 0+
limit is the Bessel
3
process;

•

for
a>0
, the Brownian excursion on
[0,a]
is the
\epsilon\to 0+
limit of
W
on
[0,a]
, conditional on
W>-\epsilon
on
[0,a]
and
W(a)<\epsilon
.

For more details on these stochastic processes, see e.g., the textbooks
[
BS15
,
MP10
]
.

We need the following functions built out of the above three processes.

Definition 2.9
.

For
x>0
, and
h,g\geq 0
, we define

\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\left(\exp\left(-\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{(g+h)^{2}}{2x}\right)\right),

and

\mathbf{I}(x;h,g)=\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{x}B(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)\right],

where
B:[0,x]\to\mathbb{R}
is a Brownian bridge with
B(0)=g
and
B(x)=h
, conditional on
B\geq 0
. We also define

\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)=\frac{2h}{\sqrt{2\pi x^{3}}}\exp\left(-\frac{h^{2}}{2x}\right),\qquad\mathbf{I}_{0}(x;h)=\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{x}B_{3}(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)\right],

where
B_{3}:[0,x]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a Bessel
3
process, conditional on
B_{3}(x)=h
.
Finally,

\mathbf{F}_{0,0}(x)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi x^{3}}},\qquad\mathbf{I}_{0,0}(x)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi x^{3}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{x}B_{e}(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)\right],

where
B_{e}:[0,x]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a Brownian excursion.

Note that
\mathbf{I}_{0}(x;h)=\lim_{g\to 0}g^{-1}\mathbf{I}(x;h,g)
, and
\mathbf{I}_{0,0}(x)=\lim_{h\to 0}h^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{0}(x;h)
.

Let us fix
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
, recall that
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{k}_{\ell}
, and also fix the blocks structure
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
. Consider the set of points on which
\mathbf{H}
is defined —
\mathbf{\Delta}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\mid\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket\}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}+v_{\ell}\mid\ell\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket,\,v_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}
— and let us denote them
(0=x_{0}<x_{1}<x_{2}<\dots<x_{n})
. We further let
\Xi
be the set of
n-1
adjacent points:
\Xi=\{(x_{0},x_{1}),(x_{1},x_{2}),\dots,(x_{n-1},x_{n})\}
. We subdivide
\Xi
into four subsets
\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2},\Xi_{3},\Xi_{4}
, depending on the types of the constraints Definition
2.5
imposed on
\mathbf{H}
in these points. For that, we introduce
A=\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)
if
x=Q_{\ell}
, and otherwise
A=\mathbf{p}^{0}(x)=\mathbf{p}^{0}(y-)
;
A
has the meaning of a lower bound for the Brownian motion in
[x,y]
. We compare possible values of
\mathbf{H}(x)
and
\mathbf{H}(y)
(dictated by Definition
2.5
) with
A
:
(x,y)\in\Xi_{i}
if

\Xi_{1}:

\mathbf{H}(x)=\mathbf{H}(y)=A
. This happens when
x=\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}
for some
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, and either
y=\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
, or
y=\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}
, or
y=\sup\{y^{\prime}:\mathbf{p}_{j}(y^{\prime})>0\}
for some
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
;

\Xi_{2}:

\mathbf{H}(x)=A
,
\mathbf{H}(y)>A
. This happens when
x=\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}
for some
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, and
y\in\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}
with either
j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
or
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
and
y\neq\sup\{y^{\prime}:\mathbf{p}_{j}(y^{\prime})>0\}
in the latter case;

\Xi_{3}:

\mathbf{H}(x)>A
,
\mathbf{H}(y)=A
. This happens when
x\not\in\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m-1}
, and either
y=\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
for some
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, or
y=\sup\{y^{\prime}:\mathbf{p}_{j}(y^{\prime})>0\}
for some
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
;

\Xi_{4}:

\mathbf{H}(x)>A
,
\mathbf{H}(y)>A
. This happens when
x\not\in\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m-1}
, and
y\in\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}
with either
j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
or
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
and
y\neq\sup\{y^{\prime}:\mathbf{p}_{j}(y^{\prime})>0\}
in the latter case.

Schematically, these four cases correspond to four panels in
Figure

2
.

x
y
\Xi_{1}

x
y
\Xi_{2}

x
y
\Xi_{3}

x
y
\Xi_{4}

Figure 2
:
An illustration of the four cases in
\Xi
.

Example 2.10
.

The bottom panel of Figure
1
has ten segments. Enumerating them from left to right:
\Xi_{1}
contains segments
4
and
8
;
\Xi_{2}
contains segment
1
;
\Xi_{3}
contains segments
3
,
7
,
9
,
10
;
\Xi_{4}
contains segments
2
,
5
,
6
.

Using the above notations, we can now define the weight function for blocks.

Definition 2.11
.

Using the above
\Xi
,
\Xi_{1}
,
\Xi_{2}
,
\Xi_{3}
,
\Xi_{4}
notations, we define

\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]=2^{-\boldsymbol{\delta}-|\{\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}|}\prod_{(x,y)\in\Xi}\mathbf{I}_{\beta}[x,y],

(9)

where

\mathbf{I}_{\beta}[x,y]=\begin{cases}\exp\big(\beta^{-1}(y-x)(\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x))\big)\mathbf{I}_{0,0}(y-x),&(x,y)\in\Xi_{1},\\
\exp\big(\beta^{-1}(y-x)(\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x))\big)\mathbf{I}_{0}(y-x;\mathbf{H}(y)-\mathbf{H}(x)),&(x,y)\in\Xi_{2},\\
(-1)^{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{p}^{0}(x)<\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)]}\mathbf{I}_{0}(y-x;\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{H}(y)),&(x,y)\in\Xi_{3},\\
(-1)^{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{p}^{0}(x)<\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)]}\mathbf{I}(y-x;\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x),\mathbf{H}(y)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(y_{-})),&(x,y)\in\Xi_{4}.\end{cases}

Heuristically, the factor
\exp(\beta^{-1}(y-x)(\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x)))
in the first two cases accounts for the (exponentiated) area between
\mathbf{p}^{0}
and the red dashed horizontal line in Figure
1
corresponding to virtual blocks. This factor combines with
\mathbf{I}_{0,0}
or
\mathbf{I}_{0}
to produce the full area between the Brownian bridge and
\mathbf{p}^{0}
. The power of
2
in (
9
) does not have any particular meaning, it will come out of the rescaling of random walks to Brownian motion in the Section
4
.

Here is the expression for the mixed moments of the Laplace transform, as in Theorem
1.4
.

Definition 2.12
.

Take any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, and
\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}
, and denote
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{k}_{\ell^{\prime}}
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.
We let

\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathscr{K}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]}\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]\mathrm{d}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}),

where the principal value integral is taken in the following sense.
For any small enough
\epsilon>0
, we let
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}=\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
denote the space of all blocks
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
, such that for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
\mathbf{p}^{0}
is constant in
(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}+\epsilon)
, and either
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}>\epsilon
, or
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}=\varnothing
.
We integrate over
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}
, then send
\epsilon\to 0+
.

We note that it is essential to take a principal value integral here, since a direct integral over all blocks in
\mathscr{K}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
would not be absolute convergent, because
\mathbf{I}_{0,0}
and
\mathbf{I}_{0}(\cdot;h)
(for any
h>0
) are not integrable as functions of
x
.
In fact, a priori, it is also unclear whether the principal value integral is well-defined (i.e., whether the
\epsilon\to 0+
limit exists; moreover, why the integral over
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}
is well-defined). These will be justified late, see
Proposition

4.42
and
Proposition

4.43
.

3
Joint moments through Dunkl operators

In this section, we introduce our main technical tool for analyzing edge limits of both corners processes and DBM, which is the
Dunkl operators.

We deal with functions in variables
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\dots
and let
\sigma_{ij}
denote the operator swapping
x_{i}
with
x_{j}
:

[\sigma_{ij}f](x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=f(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})_{x_{i}\leftrightarrow x_{j}}.

We further set for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket

\mathfrak{D}_{i}^{N}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,\\
j\neq i\end{smallmatrix}}\frac{1-\sigma_{ij}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}.

Lemma 3.1
.

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the operators
\mathfrak{D}_{1}^{N}
,
\mathfrak{D}_{2}^{N}
, …,
\mathfrak{D}_{N}^{N}
commute.

Proof.

See,
[
Dun89
]
, or
[
KJ97
]
, or
[
EM10
]
.
∎

Next, define

\mathcal{P}_{k}^{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\mathfrak{D}_{i}^{N})^{k}.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\{y^{n}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k\leq N}\in\mathcal{G}_{N}
be the G
\beta
E corners process of variance
\tau
. Fix
m=1,2,\dots
and take
2m
positive integers
N_{m}\leq N_{m-1}\leq\dots\leq N_{1}\leq N
and
k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{m}
. We have

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\ell}}\bigl(y^{N_{\ell}}_{i}\bigr)^{k_{\ell}}\right)\right]=\mathcal{P}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m}}\cdots\mathcal{P}_{k_{2}}^{N_{2}}\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right)\right]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}.

(10)

Remark 3.3
.

While the operators
\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N}
and
\mathcal{P}_{k_{2}}^{N}
commute by
Lemma

3.1
, the operators
\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}
and
P_{k_{2}}^{N_{2}}
do not for
N_{1}\neq N_{2}
. Hence, the order of application of the operators in the right-hand side is important in (
10
).

Theorem 3.4
.

Fix
N
and let
(Y_{1}(\tau)\geq Y_{2}(\tau)\geq\dots\geq Y_{N}(\tau))
denote the DBM started from zero initial condition. For each
m=1,2,\dots
and each
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\dots\leq\tau_{m}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(Y_{i}(\tau_{\ell})\Bigr)^{k_{\ell}}\right)\right]=\mathcal{P}_{k_{m}}^{N}\Biggl[\exp\left(\frac{\tau_{m}-\tau_{m-1}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right)\mathcal{P}_{k_{m-1}}^{N}\Biggl[\\
\cdots\mathcal{P}_{k_{2}}^{N}\Biggl[\exp\left(\frac{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right)\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N}\Biggl[\exp\left(\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right)\Biggr]\dots\Biggr]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0},

(11)

where in the product we alternate applications of
\mathcal{P}_{k_{\ell}}^{N}
and multiplications by
\exp(\cdot)
.

3.1
Multivariate Bessel functions

In the rest of this section, we prove
Theorems

3.2
and
3.4
.

Definition 3.5
.

For any reals
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}
, the
multivariate Bessel function

\mathcal{B}_{(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N};\beta)
is a function of
N
complex variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
defined as

\mathcal{B}_{(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\,\beta)=\mathbb{E}_{\{y^{k}_{i}\}}\!\left[\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}x_{k}\cdot\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}^{k}-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}y_{j}^{k-1}\right)\right)\right],

(12)

where the expectation is with respect to
\beta
-corners process with top row
(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})
from Definition
1.2
.

This definition (which can be found, e.g., in
[
GK02
]
) implies that

\mathcal{B}_{(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})}(0,\dots,0;\beta)=1.

(13)

Alternatively, the multivariate Bessel function can be defined as the semiclassical limit of Jack symmetric polynomials (see, e.g.,
[
OO97
, Section 4]
):

\mathcal{B}_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\beta)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{J_{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\lambda_{1}\rfloor,\dots,\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\lambda_{N}\rfloor}(1+\varepsilon x_{1},\dots,1+\varepsilon x_{N};\beta/2)}{J_{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\lambda_{1}\rfloor,\dots,\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\lambda_{N}\rfloor}(1,\dots,1;\beta/2)}.

(14)

If we take (
14
) as a definition, then (
12
) is obtained as a limit of the combinatorial formula for Jack polynomials.

[
Opd93
]
shows that
\mathcal{B}_{(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\beta)
admits an analytic continuation on the
2N+1
variables
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{N},\beta
, to an open subset of
\mathbb{C}^{2N+1}
containing
\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{N},x_{1},\cdots,x_{N},\beta)\in\mathbb{C}^{2N+1}\mid\mathrm{Re}(\beta)\geq 0\}
. In particular, for a fixed
\beta>0
,
\mathcal{B}_{(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\beta)
is an entire function on the variables
\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{N},x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}
.

Another important property is that the
\mathcal{B}_{(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\,\beta)
is
symmetric
in its arguments
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
. In the particular case
\beta=2
, there is an explicit determinantal formula, which makes all these properties especially transparent:

B_{(a_{1},\dots,a_{N})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\,2)=1!\cdot 2!\cdots(N-1)!\cdot\frac{\det\bigl[e^{a_{i}x_{j}}\bigr]_{i,j=1}^{N}}{\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})(a_{i}-a_{j})}.

(15)

We interested in Bessel functions because of their link to the Dunkl operators:

Theorem 3.6

(
[
Opd93
]
)
.

For each
k,N=1,2,\dots,
and each
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{N}
, we have

\mathcal{P}_{k}^{N}\mathcal{B}_{(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\beta)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\lambda_{i}^{k}}\right)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\beta).

(16)

Let us point to several reviews covering various aspects of the Dunkl theory and its generalizations:
[
Ank17
]
,
[
CM02
]
,
[
EM10
]
,
[
KJ97
]
.

3.2
Proof of Theorem
3.2

The proof is based on the following integral identity. In the terminology of
[
BGCG22
]
this is a computation of the Bessel Generating Function (BGF) of the Gaussian
\beta
Ensemble. In terminology of
[
Ank17
]
this is a computation of its (symmetric) Dunkl transform.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\dots>\lambda_{N}
be distributed as the G
\beta
E of (
1
) with parameters
N
and
\tau
. For any complex numbers
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}}\bigl[\mathcal{B}_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\,\beta)\bigr]=\exp\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right).

(17)

Proof.

At
N=1
, (
17
) is a well-known computation of the Laplace/Fourier transform of the Gaussian density. The general case and many references are discussed in
[
BGCG22
, Lemma 4.5]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

We start from the identity (
17
) in
N_{1}
variables and apply
\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}
to both sides using the eigenrelation (
16
). We get

\mathbb{E}_{y_{1}^{N_{1}},\dots,y_{N_{1}}^{N_{1}}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\left(y_{i}^{N_{1}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{y_{1}^{N_{1}},\dots,y_{N_{1}}^{N_{1}}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N_{1}};\,\beta)\right]=\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}\exp\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right).

(18)

Next, we plug
x_{N_{2}+1}=x_{N_{2}+2}=\dots=x_{N_{1}}=0
into (
18
) and use the following identity: if
\{y_{i}^{j}\}_{1\leq i\leq j\leq N}
is the
\beta
–corners process of Definition
1.2
with arbitrary top row, then for each
M\leq N
, we have

\mathcal{B}_{y_{1}^{N},y_{2}^{N}\dots,y_{N}^{N}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{M},0^{N-M};\,\beta)=\mathbb{E}_{y_{1}^{M},y_{2}^{M},\dots,y_{M}^{M}}\left[\mathcal{B}_{y_{1}^{M},y_{2}^{M},\dots,y_{M}^{M}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{M};\,\beta)\right].

(19)

The identity (
19
) is readily seen from (
12
). Hence, the result of plugging zeros into (
18
) is

\mathbb{E}_{y_{1}^{N_{1}},\dots,y_{N_{1}}^{N_{1}};\,\,\,y_{1}^{N_{2}},\dots,y_{N_{2}}^{N_{2}}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\left(y_{i}^{N_{1}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{y_{1}^{N_{2}},\dots,y_{N_{2}}^{N_{2}}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N_{2}};\,\beta)\right]\\
=\left[\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}\exp\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right)\right]_{x_{N_{2}+1}=x_{N_{2}+2}=\dots=x_{N_{1}}=0}.

(20)

Applying
\mathcal{P}_{k_{2}}^{N_{2}}
, we get

\mathbb{E}_{y_{1}^{N_{1}},\dots,y_{N_{1}}^{N_{1}};\,\,\,y_{1}^{N_{2}},\dots,y_{N_{2}}^{N_{2}}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\left(y_{i}^{N_{1}}\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N_{2}}\left(y_{i}^{N_{2}}\right)^{k_{2}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{y_{1}^{N_{2}},\dots,y_{N_{2}}^{N_{2}}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N_{2}};\,\beta)\right]\\
=\mathcal{P}_{k_{2}}^{N_{2}}\left[\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}\exp\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right)\right]_{x_{N_{2}+1}=x_{N_{2}+2}=\dots=x_{N_{1}}=0}.

(21)

We further plug
x_{N_{3}+1}=x_{N_{3}+2}=\dots=x_{N_{2}}=0
, then apply
\mathcal{P}_{k_{3}}^{N_{3}}
, and continue recursively. Finally, after we have applied
\mathcal{P}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m}}
, we set
x_{1}=\dots=x_{N_{m}}=0
, use the normalization condition (
13
) and arrive at (
10
).
∎

3.3
Proof of Theorem
3.4

The proof is based on the representation of transition probabilities of the DBM in terms of the Bessel functions.

Lemma 3.8
.

Fix
N
and suppose that the DBM of (
2
) is started at time
0
from a deterministic configuration
(Y_{1}(0)\geq Y_{2}(0)\geq\dots Y_{N}(0))
. Then at time
\tau>0
, for any
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{Y_{1}(\tau),Y_{2}(\tau),\dots,Y_{N}(\tau)}\left[\mathcal{B}_{Y_{1}(\tau),\dots,Y_{N}(\tau)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\,\beta)\right]\\
=\mathcal{B}_{Y_{1}(0),\dots,Y_{N}(0)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\,\beta)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right).

(22)

Remark 3.9
.

If
(Y_{1}(0),\dots,Y_{N}(0))=(0,\dots,0)
, then the law of
Y_{1}(\tau),Y_{2}(\tau),\dots,Y_{N}(\tau)
is the G
\beta
E of (
1
), and (
22
) turns into (
17
).

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

We use a formula for the transition density of DBM. It reads for
z_{1}\geq\dots\geq z_{N}
and
y_{1}\geq\dots\geq z_{N}
:

P_{\tau}\bigl((z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\to(y_{1},\dots,y_{N});\,\beta\bigr)=\tau^{-\frac{N}{2}+\beta\frac{N(N-1)}{4}}\frac{N!}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma\left(1+\tfrac{\beta}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(1+j\tfrac{\beta}{2}\right)}\\
\times\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{z_{i}^{2}+y_{i}^{2}}{2\tau}\right)\,\prod_{i<j}(y_{i}-y_{j})^{\beta}\,\mathcal{B}_{z_{1},\dots,z_{N}}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\tau},\dots,\frac{y_{N}}{\tau}\right)\mathrm{d}y_{1}\mathrm{d}y_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}y_{N}.

(23)

The last formula can be taken as a definition of DBM: for the interplay between various points of view on DBM, see
[
BF97
]
,
[
Rös98
]
,
[
AKM12
, Section 2]
,
[
Voi23
]
and references therein.
We remark that for
N=1
, (
23
) is a familiar Gaussian density, which matches the transition probability of the standard Brownian motion:

P_{\tau}\bigl(z\to y;\,\beta\bigr)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\tau)^{1/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{z^{2}+y^{2}}{2\tau}\right)\exp\left(\frac{zy}{\tau}\right)\mathrm{d}y

(24)

Equivalence of (
24
) with
N=1
version of (
22
) is a standard computation of direct (or inverse) Fourier transform of the Gaussian density. Similarly, (
23
) is equivalent to (
22
) by the inversion formula for the Dunkl transform, see, e.g.
[
Ank17
, Section 3.3]
and references therein.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.4
.

We start from the identity (
17
) for
\tau=\tau_{1}
variables and apply
\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N}
to both sides using the eigenrelation (
16
). We get

\mathbb{E}_{Y_{1}(\tau_{1}),\dots,Y_{1}(\tau_{1})}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(Y_{i}(\tau_{1})\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{Y_{1}(\tau_{1}),\dots,Y_{1}(\tau_{1})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\,\beta)\right]=\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N}\exp\left(\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right).

(25)

Next, we apply the identity (
22
) with
\tau=\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}
, transforming (
25
) into:

\mathbb{E}_{Y_{1}(\tau_{1}),\dots,Y_{1}(\tau_{1});\,\,Y_{1}(\tau_{2}),\dots,Y_{1}(\tau_{2})}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(Y_{i}(\tau_{1})\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{Y_{1}(\tau_{2}),\dots,Y_{1}(\tau_{2})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\,\beta)\right]\\
=\exp\left(\frac{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right)\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N}\exp\left(\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right).

(26)

Applying
\mathcal{P}_{k_{2}}^{N}
to both sides, we get:

\mathbb{E}_{Y_{1}(\tau_{1}),\dots,Y_{1}(\tau_{1});\,\,Y_{1}(\tau_{2}),\dots,Y_{1}(\tau_{2})}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(Y_{i}(\tau_{1})\right)^{k_{1}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(Y_{i}(\tau_{2})\right)^{k_{2}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{Y_{1}(\tau_{2}),\dots,Y_{1}(\tau_{2})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};\,\beta)\right]\\
=\mathcal{P}_{k_{2}}^{N}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right)\mathcal{P}_{k_{1}}^{N}\exp\left(\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i})^{2}\right)\right].

(27)

Further iterating this procedure and finally plugging
x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0
after applying
\mathcal{P}_{k_{m}}^{N}
, we arrive at (
11
).
∎

4
Scaling limit of general moments

4.1
Asymptotic statements

In this section we prove the convergence of high moments, for both the G
\beta
E corners process and the DBM.
For the G
\beta
E corners process, we take the variance
\tau=2N/\beta
, so that the top level particles would fill in the interval
[-2N,2N]
as
N\to\infty
.
For the DBM, we take slices around the time
2N/\beta
. In our notations, whenever possible, we use bold letters for the quantities related to the limiting objects and non-bold fonts for their prelimit counterparts.

Theorem 4.1
.

Choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, and
\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}
, any real
C_{1}>0
, and a large enough
N\in\mathbb{N}
. We take the rank
N
G
\beta
E corners process
\{y^{n}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n\leq N}
, with variance
\tau=2N/\beta
; and
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, and integers
N\geq N_{1}\geq\cdots\geq N_{m}
, such that

|k_{\ell}-\mathbf{k}_{\ell}N^{2/3}|<C_{1},\qquad|N-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}N^{2/3}-N_{\ell}|<C_{1},\qquad\text{for each }\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket.

(28)

Then as
N\to\infty
, uniformly over the (
N
–dependent) choices of
\vec{k}
and
\vec{N}
satisfying (
28
):

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\ell}}\left(\frac{y^{N_{\ell}}_{i}}{2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N}}\right)^{k_{\ell}}+\left(\frac{y^{N_{\ell}}_{i}}{2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N}}\right)^{k_{\ell}+1}\right)\right]\to\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}).

Note that here the variance is taken to be different from
Theorem

1.5
:
one needs to replace
y_{i}^{n}
by
\sqrt{2N/\beta}y_{i}^{n}
to match the scaling there.

Theorem 4.2
.

Choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, and
\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}
, any real
C_{1}>0
, and a large enough
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
We take the
N
dimensional DBM
\{Y_{i}(\tau)\}_{1\leq i\leq N,\tau>0}
started from zero initial condition, and
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
,
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\dots\leq\tau_{m}
such that

|k_{\ell}-\mathbf{k}_{\ell}N^{2/3}|<C_{1},\qquad\left|\frac{2}{\beta}N+\frac{2}{\beta}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}N^{2/3}-\tau_{\ell}\right|<C_{1},\qquad\text{for each }\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket.

(29)

Then as
N\to\infty
, uniformly over the (
N
–dependent) choices of
\vec{k}
and
\vec{\tau}
satisfying (
29
):

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{Y_{i}(\tau_{\ell})}{\sqrt{2\beta\tau_{\ell}N}}\right)^{k_{\ell}}+\left(\frac{Y_{i}(\tau_{\ell})}{\sqrt{2\beta\tau_{\ell}N}}\right)^{k_{\ell}+1}\right)\right]\to\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}).

For the ease of our analysis, we slightly reformulate the Dunkl operators, as follows.
For
\tau>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
we consider the following operators on polynomials of
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
(in the following formula
x_{i}
means the operator of multiplication by
x_{i}
):

\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N,\tau}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+\tau x_{i}+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,\\
j\neq i\end{smallmatrix}}\frac{1-\sigma_{ij}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}.

(30)

\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N,\tau})^{k},\qquad k=1,2,\dots.

(31)

The right-hand side of (
10
) now equals the degree zero term of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m},\tau}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1},\tau}
applied to the function
1
;
similarly, the right-hand side of (
11
) equals the degree zero term of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N,\tau_{m}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N,\tau_{1}}
applied to the function
1
.

By
Theorems

3.2
and
3.4
,
we see that
Theorems

4.1
and
4.2
are implied by the following two statements, respectively.

Proposition 4.3
.

Take
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, and
\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}
, and
C_{1}>0
.
For any large enough
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we take
\vec{k}
and
N\geq N_{1}\geq\cdots\geq N_{m}
satisfying (
28
).
Then as
N\to\infty
,

2^{-m}\left(\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m},2N/\beta}}{(2\sqrt{N_{m}N})^{k_{m}}}+\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}+1}^{N_{m},2N/\beta}}{(2\sqrt{N_{m}N})^{k_{m}+1}}\right)\cdots\left(\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1},2N/\beta}}{(2\sqrt{N_{1}N})^{k_{1}}}+\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}+1}^{N_{1},2N/\beta}}{(2\sqrt{N_{1}N})^{k_{1}+1}}\right)[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}

(32)

converges to
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Take
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, and
\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}
, and
C_{1}>0
.
For any large enough
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we take
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
,
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\dots\leq\tau_{m}
satisfying (
29
).
Then as
N\to\infty
,

2^{-m}\left(\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N,\tau_{m}}}{(2\beta\tau_{m}N)^{k_{m}/2}}+\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}+1}^{N,\tau_{m}}}{(2\beta\tau_{m}N)^{(k_{m}+1)/2}}\right)\cdots\left(\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N,\tau_{1}}}{(2\beta\tau_{1}N)^{k_{1}/2}}+\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}+1}^{N,\tau_{1}}}{(2\beta\tau_{1}N)^{(k_{1}+1)/2}}\right)[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}

converges to
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
.

4.2
Strategy of the proof

Sections
4.3
–
4.9
are devoted to proving
Proposition

4.3
, and our approach is to expand the product of the operators in terms of random walks and then study diffusive scaling limit for them, in which walks turn into Brownian motions. Section
4.10
explains how the same arguments can be adapted to the Dyson Brownian Motion to get
Proposition

4.4
.

As a starting point, we write the product of operators in Proposition
4.3
as a linear combination of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
. In Section
4.3
we look at how each
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{\ell}}^{N_{\ell}}
acts on monomials, and convert this information into a combinatorial expansion for a general operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
. The expansion is written in terms of the objects that we call “walks”, as defined in Section
4.3.2
. Each walk is a finite collection of discrete paths and we refer to Figures
3
–
6
for graphical illustrations. Generically, these paths have either
\pm 1
steps or horizontal plateaus where they stay constant. However, at special points there are much larger jumps.

Our task is to send
N\to\infty
in the sum over this collection of paths. We will eventually see that, generically, each path becomes a Brownian bridge (or maybe a Brownian excursion, if there are some positivity constraints), however, jumps play the central role in the analysis. The main idea is that all terms can be treated as finite perturbations of walks without jumps. In other words, the terms in the sum can be grouped by the number of jumps and the subsum of the terms with more than
\delta
jumps becomes negligible as
\delta\to\infty
, uniformly in
N
. Therefore (in the
N\to\infty
limit) it suffices to consider the paths with finitely many jumps. Careful encoding of the information contained in these jumps eventually gives rise to the combinatorial structure of “blocks” which we have already introduced in Section
2
.

There is an important obstacle in directly developing such arguments. A naive sum over all walks fails to be
absolutely
convergent in the
N\to\infty
limit. In more detail, each walk
\vec{r}
comes with a signed weight
w
, and while we are eventually interested in
\sum_{\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})
, the sum of absolute values
\sum_{\vec{r}}|w(\vec{r})|
grows faster as
N\to\infty
. We call this a blow-up issue and illustrate it in the examples in Section
4.3.3
. Because of this feature, we need to keep track of certain cancelations as
N\to\infty
; this is also the reason why the limiting expression
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is represented in Definition
2.12
as a conditionally convergent improper integral.

The first step of the asymptotic analysis of
\sum_{\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})
is to better understand the combinatorics of walks and how it leads to different asymptotic behaviors of
w(\vec{r})
and subsums corresponding to walks of different types. A combinatorial classification of walks is developed in Section
4.4
, and afterwards in Section
4.5
we present a general upper-bound for a contribution of walks of a given type to sum
\sum_{\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})
. This bound is repeatedly used in subsequent subsections to produce various tail bounds and to understand which walks survive in
N\to\infty
limit and whose contributions are asymptotically negligible. The proof of the upper-bound of Section
4.5
relies on (somewhat standard, but quite tedious) counts for the random walks with various restrictions, which are presented in Section
A.1
of the appendix.

In Sections
4.6
and
4.7
we explore various cancelations between walks of different combinatorial types in
\sum_{\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})
. The key result is Proposition
4.25
: it introduces a subset
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
,
\epsilon>0
, of all walks, which satisfies two key features:

•

The subsum over
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
is absolutely convergent, i.e. the asymptotics scale of
\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}|w(\vec{r})|
is the same as for the full signed sum
\sum_{\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})
.

•

As
\epsilon\to 0+
the walks outside
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
have negligible total contribution to
\sum_{\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})
.

Here
\epsilon
has a role of a regularization parameter, and it is the same
\epsilon
as the one in
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}
of Definition
2.12
. The constraint on the walks in
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
is that on certain
\epsilon
–dependent intervals some paths are required to stay constant, while others are required to stay above certain lower bounds.

In Section
4.8
we assign to each walk
\vec{r}
in
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
combinatorial data, which is a discrete version of the blocks structure of Section
2
. Proposition
4.38
shows that the subsum of
\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}w(\vec{r})
corresponding to the walks whose data is close to a particular blocks structure is well-approximated by
\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]
, which is precisely the integrand in Definition
2.12
.
The proof is based on
N\to\infty
approximation of Bernoulli random walks by Brownian motions; again somewhat standard, but quite technical properties of such approximations are presented in the appendix, see Sections
A.2
and
A.3
.

Section
4.9
is the culmination of the proof of Proposition
4.3
: we show that discrete sum
\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}w(\vec{r})
approximates as
N\to\infty
the integral over
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}
in Definition
2.12
and further send
\epsilon\to 0
to complete the proof. On our way we also establish in Propositions
4.42
and
4.43
that
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
from
Definition

2.12
is well-defined as a conditionally convergent improper integral.

For the Dyson Brownian Motion framework, i.e., for Proposition
4.4
, the proof follows exactly the same steps, and we only outline in Section
4.10
the necessary modifications.

Conventions.
For the convenience of notations, from now on we write
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N_{\ell}}=\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N_{\ell},2N/\beta}
and
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{\ell}}=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{\ell},2N/\beta}
. Throughout the rest of this section, we fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and use
C
and
c
to denote large and small constants that may depend on
m
, while the specific values can change from line to line.
We will also use
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
to denote constants that are used locally within each statement (i.e., they are not changing inside a particular proof, but
C_{1}
inside one lemma is allowed to be different from
C_{1}
inside another lemma).
In particular,
C,c
may also depend on
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
, unless otherwise noted.

4.3
Setup and discrete expansion

4.3.1
Terms in expansions

We start analysis of the expression in Proposition
4.3
. The product
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}
can be expanded as the sum of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
,
over all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})\in\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket
. We now expand
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
.
For a monomial
x_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{d_{N}}
(where each
d_{1},\dots,d_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
), and any
j\neq i
, the operator
\frac{1-\sigma_{ij}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}
acting on it leads to

x_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{d_{N}}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{d_{i}-d_{j}}x_{i}^{-\gamma}x_{j}^{\gamma-1},

(33)

if
d_{i}>d_{j}
; and

-x_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots x_{N}^{d_{N}}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{d_{j}-d_{i}}x_{i}^{\gamma-1}x_{j}^{-\gamma}

(34)

if
d_{i}<d_{j}
; and zero if
d_{i}=d_{j}
.

We introduce the following notation: for
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and monomial
f
of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
, we use
\deg_{i}(f)
to denote the degree of
x_{i}
in
f
. We recall
\tau=\frac{2}{\beta}N
, set
Q_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}k_{\ell^{\prime}}
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
,
and expand
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
:

Definition 4.5
.

A
term in expansion
of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
is a sequence
\{O(t),P(t)\}_{t=1}^{Q_{m}}
, where each
O(t)\in\{+,-,\top\}
denotes the operation at each step, and each
P(t)
is a monomial
10
10

10

In this paper, a monomial refers to a product of powers of variables with nonnegative integer exponents, and a non-zero constant. In particular, any non-zero constant is a monomial.
of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
. For each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, and
t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
, one of the following is required to hold for
P(t)
(assuming that
P(0)=1
for the ease of notations):

1:

O(t)=+
, and
P(t)=\frac{2Nx_{i_{\ell}}P(t-1)}{\beta}
;

2:

O(t)=-
, and
P(t)=\big(\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))+\tfrac{\beta}{2}R(t-1)\big)\frac{P(t-1)}{x_{i_{\ell}}}
, where
R(t-1)
is the number of
j\in\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket
with
\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))>\deg_{j}(P(t-1))
;

(We note that in this case, necessarily
\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))>0
, since otherwise
R(t-1)
would also be zero, and
P(t)
equals zero, which is not a monomial.)

3(a):

O(t)=\top
, and
P(t)=x_{i_{\ell}}^{-\gamma}x_{j}^{\gamma-1}\frac{\beta P(t-1)}{2}
for some
j\in\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket
with
\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))\geq\deg_{j}(P(t-1))+2
, and some
\gamma\in\llbracket 2,\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))-\deg_{j}(P(t-1))\rrbracket
;

3(b):

O(t)=\top
, and
P(t)=-x_{i_{\ell}}^{\gamma-1}x_{j}^{-\gamma}\frac{\beta P(t-1)}{2}
for some
j\in\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket
with
\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))\leq\deg_{j}(P(t-1))-1
, and some
\gamma\in\llbracket 1,\deg_{j}(P(t-1))-\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))\rrbracket
.

In addition, we require that
\deg_{i}(P(Q_{m}))=0
for each
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, i.e.,
P(Q_{m})
is a constant.

We note that the four cases in the above definition corresponds to different parts of the operator
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{\ell}}^{N_{\ell}}=\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{\ell}}^{N_{\ell},2N/\beta}
:

1:

\tau x_{i_{\ell}}=\frac{2}{\beta}Nx_{i_{\ell}}
;

2:

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{\ell}}}
, as well as the
\frac{\beta}{2}x_{i_{\ell}}^{-1}
(i.e.,
\gamma=1
) terms in the expansion of
\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\frac{1-\sigma_{i_{\ell}j}}{x_{i_{\ell}}-x_{j}}
in (
33
), for each
j\in\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket
with
\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))>\deg_{j}(P(t-1))
;

3(a):

the
\frac{\beta}{2}x_{i_{\ell}}^{-\gamma}x_{j}^{\gamma-1}
with
\gamma\geq 2
terms in the expansion of
\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\frac{1-\sigma_{i_{\ell}j}}{x_{i_{\ell}}-x_{j}}
in (
33
), for each
j\in\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket
with
\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))\geq\deg_{j}(P(t-1))+2
;

3(b):

the
\frac{\beta}{2}x_{i_{\ell}}^{\gamma-1}x_{j}^{-\gamma}
terms in the expansion of
\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\frac{1-\sigma_{i_{\ell}j}}{x_{i_{\ell}}-x_{j}}
in (
34
), for each
j\in\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket
with
\deg_{i_{\ell}}(P(t-1))\leq\deg_{j}(P(t-1))-1
.

Example 4.6
.

The operator
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{5}
applied to
x_{1}^{5}x_{2}^{2}x_{4}
leads to the sum of the following monomials:

1:

\frac{2Nx_{1}^{5}x_{2}^{3}x_{4}}{\beta}
,

2:

(2+3\beta/2)x_{1}^{5}x_{2}x_{4}
,

3(a):

x_{2}^{-2}x_{3}\cdot\frac{\beta}{2}x_{1}^{5}x_{2}^{2}x_{4}
and
x_{2}^{-2}x_{5}\cdot\frac{\beta}{2}x_{1}^{5}x_{2}^{2}x_{4}
,

3(b):

-x_{1}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\beta}{2}x_{1}^{5}x_{2}^{2}x_{4}
,
-x_{1}^{-2}x_{2}\cdot\frac{\beta}{2}x_{1}^{5}x_{2}^{2}x_{4}
, and
-x_{1}^{-3}x_{2}^{2}\cdot\frac{\beta}{2}x_{1}^{5}x_{2}^{2}x_{4}
.

It is straightforward to see that
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
applied to the function
1
is precisely the sum of
P(Q_{m})
over all the terms in expansion.
Therefore the sum of
P(Q_{m})
over all the terms in expansion would give the zero degree term of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
applied to
1
.

4.3.2
Discrete walk representation

We next observe that a term
\{O(t),P(t)\}_{t=1}^{Q_{m}}
in expansion of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
can be encoded by the degrees of the monomials
P(t)
.
More precisely, we denote
r_{i}(t)=\deg_{i}(P(t))
, for each
t\in\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket
and
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
.
We call such
\vec{r}=\{r_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
a
walk
of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
.

Definition 4.7
.

A
walk
of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
consists of
\vec{r}=\{r_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
, with each
r_{j}:\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, satisfying the following conditions.
Denote
\mathscr{H}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}r_{j}(t)
.

1.

|\mathscr{H}(t)-\mathscr{H}(t-1)|=1
for each
t\in\llbracket 1,Q_{m}\rrbracket
,
\mathscr{H}(t)\geq 0
, and
\mathscr{H}(0)=\mathscr{H}(Q_{m})=0
.

2.

For each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
, we must have one of the two cases:

•

Either,
r_{i_{\ell}}(t)=r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\pm 1
and
r_{j}(t)=r_{j}(t-1)
for all
j\neq i_{\ell}
;

•

Or there exists one and only one index
11
11

11

This corresponds to the third option, i.e.,
O(t)=\top
, in
Definition

4.5
.

j\neq i_{\ell}
, such that
r_{j}(t)\neq r_{j}(t-1)
.
Besides, in this case, necessarily
\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1
, and
j\leq N_{\ell}
, and the differences
r_{j}(t)-r_{j}(t-1)
and
r_{j}(t)-r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)+1/2
are of opposite signs.

In other words, the last condition says that when
r_{j}(t)>r_{j}(t-1)
, i.e.,
r_{j}
increases, we have
r_{j}(t)<r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)
, corresponding to 3(a) in
Definition

4.5
; and when
r_{j}(t)<r_{j}(t-1)
, i.e.,
r_{j}
decreases, we have
r_{j}(t)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)
, corresponding to 3(b) in
Definition

4.5
. In particular, if
r_{j}(t)=0
, then we must have
r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)=0
.

We use
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{B}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}]
to denote the set of all walks of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
.

We remark that in the continuous limit
\mathscr{H}(t)
turns into the trajectories of (positive) Brownian bridges, as in the bottom panel of Figure
1
, while the remaining data (block and virtual block processes) encodes how
\mathscr{H}(t)
is split into degrees of various variables.

There is a bijection between terms in expansion and walks. In addition, each walk uniquely determines the value of
P(Q_{m})
— the latter is a zero degree monomial and, hence, a constant.

Definition 4.8
.

For
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}
, we define its weight
w(\vec{r})
to be the constant
P(Q_{m})
in the corresponding term in expansion.
Equivalently, multiplying the factors from cases 1, 2, 3(a), 3(b) in Definition
4.5
:

\displaystyle w(\vec{r})=\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\Biggl[

\displaystyle(-1)^{\bigl|\{t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket:\,\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1,\,r_{i_{\ell}}(t)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\}\bigr|}

(35)

\displaystyle\times(\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}\,N_{\ell}^{(\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2-\bigl|\{t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket:\,\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1,\,r_{i_{\ell}}(t)\neq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)-1\}\bigr|}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket:\\
\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1,\\
r_{i_{\ell}}(t)=r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)}{\beta N_{\ell}}-\frac{|\{j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:r_{j}(t-1)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\}|}{N_{\ell}}\right)\Biggr].

Comparing with Definition
4.5
: the first case there contributes to the power of
N
in the second line of (
35
); the second case contributes to the product in the last line of (
35
); the case 3(b) contributes to the power of
-1
in the first line of (
35
). In addition the factors
\frac{\beta}{2}
and
\frac{2}{\beta}
from all four cases cancel out, because the walk starts and ends at
0
. Furthermore, the factors of
N_{\ell}
in the second line of (
35
) precisely cancel with similar factors in the denominators in the third line. The exponent
N_{\ell}^{(\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2}
is responsible for the appearance in the
N\to\infty
limit of
\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)
in Definition
2.12
.

We can now summarize the expansion of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}
:

Lemma 4.9
.

We have

\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})\in\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket}\,\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}]}w(\vec{r}).

(36)

We note that this sum can be viewed as a discrete analogue of the integral in
Definition

2.12
, and the rest of the proof is to show that the discrete sum converges to the principal value integral as
N\to\infty
.

4.3.3
Scaling analysis and blow-up terms

For the sum (
36
),
we expect it to be of order
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{k_{\ell}}
, since under our setup the extreme particles of the corners process at row
N_{\ell}
(
y_{i}^{N_{\ell}}
in Theorem
4.1
) are approximately equal to
2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N}
.
We next heuristically explain the scale of the sum, through examples of certain types of walks. In doing so we illustrate that it would be crucial to exploit cancellation between the weights, which can be either positive or negative.

Walks without jumps.

Let us consider all walks
\vec{r}
for which
O(t)\in\{+,-\}
for all
t
. Equivalently, for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
j\neq i_{\ell}
,
r_{j}
is constant on
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
.
Imagine that we apply
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{\ell}}^{N_{\ell}}
using (
30
). Then the walks without jumps correspond to taking
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{\ell}}}+\frac{2}{\beta}Nx_{i_{\ell}}+S\frac{\beta}{2}x_{i_{\ell}}^{-1}
, with the last term coming from
\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\frac{1-\sigma_{i_{\ell}j}}{x_{i_{\ell}}-x_{j}}
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket

where the degree of
x_{j}
is smaller than the degree of
x_{i_{\ell}}
, and
S
counts the number of such cases (
S
changes as a function of
t
and satisfies
N_{\ell}-m\leq S\leq N_{\ell}-1
). See
Figure

3
for some illustrations of such walks.

0
Q_{1}

0
Q_{1}
Q_{2}
Q_{3}

Figure 3
:

Illustrations of walks without jumps (i.e., all
O(t)
being
\pm
).

Top panel: a walk of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{3}^{N})^{k_{1}}
without jumps: the curve represents
\mathscr{H}=r_{3}
, and all its steps have slopes
\pm 1
.
(This walk corresponds to a term in
Definition

2.12
with
m=1
, and all
\mathbf{p}_{j}=0
.)

Bottom panel: a walk
\vec{r}
of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{5}^{N})^{k_{3}}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{N})^{k_{2}}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{5}^{N})^{k_{1}}
without jumps: the green and red curves are
r_{5}
and
r_{2}
respectively, and the black curve is
\mathscr{H}=r_{2}+r_{5}
.
All the steps of the green curve in
\llbracket 0,Q_{1}\rrbracket\cup\llbracket Q_{2},Q_{3}\rrbracket
have slopes
\pm 1
, and all the steps of the red curve in
\llbracket Q_{1},Q_{2}\rrbracket
have slopes
\pm 1
. (The contribution of such walk vanishes as
N\to\infty
due to cancellations in
Section

4.7
below.)

In this case, the non-constant part of each
r_{j}
is a path of length
\sum_{\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:i_{\ell}=j}k_{\ell}
, starting from and ending at
0
, taking
\pm 1
at each step, and staying non-negative.
Thus the number of such
\vec{r}
is a product of Catalan numbers
\frac{1}{n+1}{{2n}\choose n}
. Plugging the asymptotic expansion of the binomial coefficients, the number of
\vec{r}
is of order
2^{Q_{m}}N^{-\eta}
, because each
k_{\ell}
is of order
N^{2/3}
, and where
\eta
is the number of different numbers appearing in
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
.

For a walk with each
O(t)\in\{+,-\}
, its weight roughly
12
12

12

Comparing with
Definition

4.8
, we omit
\frac{|\{j\in\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket:r_{j}(t-1)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\}|}{N_{\ell}}
, which is of order
N^{-1}
and not necessarily zero, but does not change our rough bounds.
equals

\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}\prod_{t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket:\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1}\left(1+\frac{2r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)}{\beta N_{\ell}}\right),

which is of order
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}
.
Finally, by multiplying this with
2^{Q_{m}}N^{-\eta}
, and summing over
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}N_{\ell}
choices for
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
, we get the desired order
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{k_{\ell}}
.

We remark that if we take the
N\to\infty
limit, but fix
\vec{k}
, the sum of these terms would give (after rescaling) the products of the moments of the semi-circle law, which are Catalan numbers.
On the other hand, if
\vec{k}
also
\to\infty
in the scaling limit, i.e., under the setting of
Proposition

4.3
, these terms would give the integral over all
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
with
\mathbf{\Delta}=\emptyset
, in defining
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
in
Definition

2.12
. In particular, for
m=1
this corresponds to the first case in Example
2.8
.

We proceed to walks (equivalently, terms in expansion) with some
\top
(i.e., jumps) in
\{O(t)\}_{t=1}^{Q_{m}}
.
For some of such walks, the sum of the weights, even when taking absolute values, is still of the desired order. For some other walks with
\top
, we may encounter a ‘blow-up’ when summing over the absolute values of the weights; i.e., the sum of absolute values is of a much larger order.
The difference between these two types of walks (with jumps) is from the behavior to the right of each
Q_{\ell}
: in the ‘blow-up’ case the total degree
\mathscr{H}(t)
is
\geq\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell})
for
t
in a small interval to the right from
Q_{\ell}
, for some
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
(once this interval is large enough, the blow-up disappears). The precise formulation will be given shortly in
Section

4.4
below.

The walks with a blow-up correspond to blocks with non-trivial virtual block processes (i.e.,
\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}
with at least one
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\neq\varnothing
), in Definition
2.4
; other walks correspond to blocks with each virtual block coordinate
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}=\varnothing
.

We next give some examples of walks with jumps, to illustrate the phenomena, and to explain how we resolve the blow-up issue using cancellations.

Walks with jumps, but no blow-up.

Example 4.10
.

Consider the expansion of the operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{N})^{k}
, and its walks
\vec{r}
of the following form illustrated in
Figure

4
:
There exists a
j_{*}\in\llbracket 2,N\rrbracket
, such that
r_{j}=0
for each
j\in\llbracket 2,N\rrbracket\setminus\{j_{*}\}
.
For
r_{1}
, it is integer-valued and non-negative on
\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
. For some
s,s^{\prime}\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket
,
s<s^{\prime}
, we have

•

For each
t\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\setminus\{s,s^{\prime}\}
, we have
|r_{1}(t)-r_{1}(t-1)|=1
.

•

r_{j_{*}}=0
on
\llbracket 0,s-1\rrbracket\cup\llbracket s^{\prime},k\rrbracket
, and
r_{j_{*}}
is constant and positive on
\llbracket s,s^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
.
Moreover,
r_{1}(s)+r_{j_{*}}(s)=r_{1}(s-1)+r_{j_{*}}(s-1)-1
and
r_{1}(s^{\prime})+r_{j_{*}}(s^{\prime})=r_{1}(s^{\prime}-1)+r_{j_{*}}(s^{\prime}-1)-1
.

Any such
\vec{r}
is uniquely determined by the following information: the sum
\mathscr{H}=r_{1}+r_{j_{*}}
(which is a path on
\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
, starting and ending at
0
and taking
\pm 1
at each step, with
\mathscr{H}(k)>0
), and the values of
s
and
r_{j_{*}}(s)
.
The number
s^{\prime}
is uniquely determined as the smallest number in
\llbracket s+1,k\rrbracket
with
\mathscr{H}(s^{\prime})<r_{j_{*}}(s)
.
Therefore, the number of such
\vec{r}
is of the order of
2^{k}=2^{k}k^{-3/2}\cdot k\cdot k^{1/2}
, where
2^{k}k^{-3/2}
is from counting
\mathscr{H}
(Catalan numbers), and
k
and
k^{1/2}
are from the number of choices of
s
and
r_{j_{*}}(s)
, respectively.

On the other hand, for all such
\vec{r}
, we have that
w(\vec{r})
is negative, with
|w(\vec{r})|
in the order of
N^{k-2}
.
Then by summing over all such
\vec{r}
one gets order
(2N)^{k}N^{-2}
. The variables
x_{1}
and
x_{j_{*}}
could have been any two other variables, and summing over all choices them, we finally arrive at the order of magnitude
(2N)^{k}
, matching what was announced at the start of Section
4.3.3
.

Example 4.11
.

Consider the expansion of the operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{N})^{k}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{N})^{k}
, and its walks
\vec{r}
of the following form illustrated in
Figure

5
:
For each
j\in\llbracket 3,N\rrbracket
, we have
r_{j}=0
.
For
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, they are integer-valued and non-negative on
\llbracket 0,2k\rrbracket
. For some
s\in\llbracket k+1,2k\rrbracket
, we have

•

For each
t\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, we have
|r_{1}(t)-r_{1}(t-1)|=1
, and
r_{1}
is constant and positive on
\llbracket k,s-1\rrbracket
, and
r_{1}=0
on
\llbracket s,2k\rrbracket
.

•

r_{2}=0
on
\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
. For each
t\in\llbracket k+1,s-1\rrbracket
, or
t\in\llbracket s+1,2k\rrbracket
, we have
|r_{2}(t)-r_{2}(t-1)|=1
. Also
r_{2}(s)=r_{1}(s-1)-1
.

Any such
\vec{r}
is uniquely determined by the sum
\mathscr{H}=r_{1}+r_{2}
, which is a path on
\llbracket 0,2k\rrbracket
, starting and ending at
0
and taking
\pm 1
at each step, with
\mathscr{H}(k)>0
.
The number
s
is uniquely determined as the smallest number in
\llbracket k+1,2k\rrbracket
with
\mathscr{H}(s)<\mathscr{H}(k)
.
Therefore, the number of such
\vec{r}
is of the order of
2^{2k}k^{-3/2}
, or
(equivalently) of the order of
2^{2k}N^{-1}
.

On the other hand, for all such
\vec{r}
, we have that
w(\vec{r})
is negative, with
|w(\vec{r})|
in the order of
N^{2k-1}
.
Then by summing over all such
\vec{r}
one gets order
(2N)^{2k}N^{-2}
. The variables
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
could have been any two other variables, and summing over all choices them, we finally arrive at the order of magnitude
(2N)^{2k}
, which matches
\prod_{\ell=1}^{2}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{k_{\ell}}
announced at the start of Section
4.3.3
.

Blow up with jumps.

We now turn to the ‘blow-up’ issue, which can happen already when there is just one
\top
.

Example 4.12
.

We again consider of the operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{N})^{k}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{N})^{k}
, and its walks
\vec{r}
of the following form illustrated in the top panel of
Figure

6
.
For each
j\in\llbracket 3,N\rrbracket
, we have
r_{j}=0
.
For
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, they are integer-valued and non-negative on
\llbracket 0,2k\rrbracket
.
For some
s\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket
we have

•

r_{2}=0
on
\llbracket 0,s-1\rrbracket
, and
r_{2}
is constant and positive on
\llbracket s,k\rrbracket
. For each
t\in\llbracket k+1,2k\rrbracket
, we have
|r_{2}(t)-r_{2}(t-1)|=1
. Also
r_{2}(2k)=0
.

•

For each
t\in\llbracket 1,s-1\rrbracket
or
t\in\llbracket s+1,k\rrbracket
, we have
|r_{1}(t)-r_{1}(t-1)|=1
. We also have
r_{1}(s)+r_{2}(s)=r_{1}(s-1)+r_{2}(s-1)-1
, and
r_{1}=0
on
\llbracket k,2k\rrbracket
.

For any such
\vec{r}
with given
s
, it is uniquely determined by the sum
\mathscr{H}=r_{1}+r_{2}
, which is a path on
\llbracket 0,2k\rrbracket
, starting and ending at
0
and taking
\pm 1
at each step, being non-negative, and staying
\geq\mathscr{H}(k)
on
\llbracket s,k\rrbracket
.
Therefore, the number of such
\vec{r}
(with given
s
) is in the order of
2^{2k}k^{-3/2}(k-s+1)^{-1/2}
. The factor
2^{2k}k^{-3/2}
comes from Catalan number, counting the number of all possible
\mathscr{H}
; and
(k-s+1)^{-1/2}
corresponds to the extra requirement of
\geq\mathscr{H}(k)
on
\llbracket s,k\rrbracket
— one could think about imposing this requirement on a uniformly random positive random walk bridge and then computing probability using the reflection principle. By summing over all possible choices for
s
, one gets order
2^{2k}k^{-1}
, which can be rewritten as order
2^{2k}N^{-2/3}
.

Definition
4.8
yields that for any such
\vec{r}
the weight
w(\vec{r})
is negative and
|w(\vec{r})|
is of the order of
N^{2k-1}
.
Then by summing the weights over all such
\vec{r}
one gets
(2N)^{2k}N^{-5/3}
. We also need to multiply by
N^{2}
, in order to account for all the possible choices of two variables which could replace
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
in our arguments. Hence, we eventually get a negative contribution of the order
(2N)^{2k}N^{1/3}
, which is much larger than the desired scale
(2N)^{2k}
, which we saw in the previous examples and announced at the start of Section
4.3.3
.

Such blow-up issue in summing over
w(\vec{r})
is resolved by exploiting cancellations.
In particular,
\vec{r}
of the above example are paired with the following walks whose weights have opposite signs.

(

), and all other

, and the virtual block
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{2}\neq\varnothing
.)

Example 4.13
.

For the same operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}^{N})^{k}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{N})^{k}
, consider walks
\vec{r}
of the following form illustrated in the bottom panel of
Figure

6
.
There exists a
j_{*}\in\llbracket 3,N\rrbracket
, such that for each
j\in\llbracket 3,N\rrbracket\setminus\{j_{*}\}
, we have
r_{j}=0
.
For
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
, and
r_{j_{*}}
, they are integer-valued and non-negative on
\llbracket 0,2k\rrbracket
.
For some
s\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket
and
s^{\prime}\in\llbracket k+1,2k\rrbracket
(necessarily
s^{\prime}-k
should be odd), we have

•

r_{j_{*}}=0
on
\llbracket 0,s-1\rrbracket\cup\llbracket s^{\prime},2k\rrbracket
, and
r_{j_{*}}
is constant and positive on
\llbracket s,s^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
.

•

r_{2}=0
on
\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
. For each
t\in\llbracket k+1,s^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
or
t\in\llbracket s^{\prime},2k\rrbracket
, we have
|r_{2}(t)-r_{2}(t-1)|=1
. Also
r_{j_{*}}(s^{\prime})+r_{2}(s^{\prime})=r_{j_{*}}(s^{\prime}-1)+r_{2}(s^{\prime}-1)-1
and
r_{2}(2k)=0
.

•

For each
t\in\llbracket 1,s-1\rrbracket
or
t\in\llbracket s+1,k\rrbracket
, we have
|r_{1}(t)-r_{1}(t-1)|=1
. Also
r_{1}(s)+r_{j_{*}}(s)=r_{1}(s-1)+r_{j_{*}}(s-1)-1
, and
r_{1}=0
on
\llbracket k,2k\rrbracket
.

We note that for any such
\vec{r}
with given
s
, it is uniquely determined by the sum
\mathscr{H}=r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{j_{*}}
, which is a path on
\llbracket 0,2k\rrbracket
, starting and ending at
0
and taking
\pm 1
at each step, and staying non-negative, and staying
\geq\mathscr{H}(k)
on
\llbracket s,k\rrbracket
.
The number
s^{\prime}
is the smallest integer in
\llbracket k+1,2k\rrbracket
with
\mathscr{H}(s^{\prime})<\mathscr{H}(k)
.

Let
r^{*}_{1}=r_{1}
,
r^{*}_{2}=r_{2}+r_{j_{*}}
, and
r^{*}_{j}=0
for each
j\in\llbracket 3,N\rrbracket
.
The map
\vec{r}\mapsto\vec{r}^{*}
is onto, from the walks in this example to those in the previous example.
Each walk in the previous example has precisely
N-2
pre-images under this map.
Also,
w(\vec{r})>0
and
w(\vec{r}^{*})<0
, and one can check that
|(N-2)w(\vec{r})+w(\vec{r}^{*})|
is of order at most
N^{2k-1}\cdot(s^{\prime}-k)k^{1/2}N^{-1}
. The factor
N^{2k-1}
is from the second line of (
35
), which is roughly the same for
(N-2)w(\vec{r})
and
w(\vec{r}^{*})
; and the factor
(s^{\prime}-k)k^{1/2}N^{-1}
is from the third line of (
35
), which (for
(N-2)w(\vec{r})+w(\vec{r}^{*})
) is bounded by
N^{-1}\sum_{t=k+1}^{s^{\prime}-1}\mathscr{H}(t)
, whose asymptotics is estimated by assuming that random walks converge to Brownian objects under diffusive scaling.

Note that for given
s
and
s^{\prime}
, the number of
\vec{r}^{*}
is of order at most
2^{2k}k^{-3/2}(k-s+1)^{-1/2}(s^{\prime}-k)^{-3/2}
. Here (as in the previous example)
2^{2k}k^{-3/2}
comes from the Catalan number, and the factors
(k-s+1)^{-1/2}
and
(s^{\prime}-k)^{-3/2}
account for the requirements of
\geq\mathscr{H}(k)
on
\llbracket s,k\rrbracket
, and
\geq\mathscr{H}(k)=\mathscr{H}(s^{\prime}-1)
on
\llbracket k,s^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
, respectively, which we estimated as probabilities for uniformly random positive random walk bridges.

Multiplying
N^{2k-1}\cdot(s^{\prime}-k)k^{1/2}N^{-1}
with
2^{2k}k^{-3/2}(k-s+1)^{-1/2}(s^{\prime}-k)^{-3/2}
from the previous two paragraphs
and summing over
s
and
s^{\prime}
, one gets order
(2N)^{2k}N^{-2}
. Again, summing over all possible choices of a pair of variables which can replace
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, we get the desired order
(2N)^{2k}
, announced at the beginning of Section
4.3.3
.

The situation in Example
4.12
corresponds as
N\to\infty
to having
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{2}>0
for virtual block process in Definition
2.4
. On the other hand, the situation in Example
4.13
corresponds to
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{2}=\varnothing
case. The cancellation of such terms is also responsible for the necessity of considering the principle value integral in Definition
2.12
.

4.4
Classification of beginnings and endings

For a walk
\vec{r}
of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
, we set up the following notations:

•

For each
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, if
i_{\ell}\neq j
, then we let
\Delta_{j,\ell}
to be the set of all
{t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket}
, such that
r_{j}(t-1)\neq r_{j}(t)
; otherwise we let
\Delta_{j,\ell}=\emptyset
.
Let
\delta_{j,\ell}=|\Delta_{j,\ell}|
,
\delta_{j}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\delta_{j,\ell}
,
\delta=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\delta_{j}
,
and
\Delta_{j}=\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\Delta_{j,\ell}
,
\Delta=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}\Delta_{j}
.

Note that
\Delta=\{t\in\llbracket 1,Q_{m}\rrbracket:O(t)=\top\}
for
O
from the corresponding term in expansion, and that this is a discrete version of
\mathbf{\Delta}
in (
7
), (
8
).

•

Let
J
be the collection of all
j
which appear in
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
. These are the indices of the main variables. For each
j\in J
, we let
a_{j}
(resp.
b_{j}
) be the smallest (resp. largest)
\ell
such that
j=i_{\ell}
.

•

Let
U
be the collection of all
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\setminus J
, with
\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\delta_{j,\ell}>0
. These are the indices of auxiliary variables; eventually they give rise to
\mathbf{p}_{\ell}
with
\ell>m
in Definition
2.2
of the block process.

We rewrite the expression in
Definition

4.8
in the new notations as

\begin{split}w(\vec{r})=\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}&(-1)^{\bigl|\{t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket:\,\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1,\,r_{i_{\ell}}(t)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\}\bigr|}\\
&\times(\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N_{\ell}^{(\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2-\bigl|\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\bigr|}\\
&\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket\setminus\Delta\\
\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)}{\beta N_{\ell}}-\frac{\bigl|\{j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:r_{j}(t-1)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\}\bigr|}{N_{\ell}}\right).\end{split}

(37)

We next introduce a notation, which is related to virtual blocks of Definition
2.4
and plays an important role in the structure of cancellations.

Definition 4.14
.

For each
j\in J
, we let
\vartheta_{j}=\min\{t\in\llbracket 1,\tfrac{1}{4}(Q_{a_{j}}-Q_{a_{j}-1})\rrbracket:\mathscr{H}(Q_{a_{j}-1}+t)<\mathscr{H}(Q_{a_{j}-1})\}
. If the last set is empty, then we let
\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
.

For each
j\in J
, we let
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\min\{t\in\llbracket 1,(Q_{a_{j}}-Q_{a_{j}-1})/4\rrbracket:Q_{a_{j}-1}+t\in\Delta\}
. If the last set is empty, then
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\varnothing
.

Note that if
r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})=0
, then
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}\leq\vartheta_{j}
. The constant
\tfrac{1}{4}
in the definition of
\vartheta_{j}
is not important and can be replaced by any other positive number smaller than
\tfrac{1}{2}
.

Next, we take a walk
\vec{r}
and consider the corresponding set
J
. We would like to classify points of
J
in two ways. For the first way, summarized in Table
1
, we look into the behavior of
\vec{r}(t)
for
t
immediately to the right from

, and partition
J
into eight sets

,

,

,

,

,

(see

). For the second way, we look into the behavior of each
r_{j}(t)
at the points
t
after which
r_{j}
becomes zero and partition
J
into three sets
J_{\mathrm{A}}
,
J_{\mathrm{B}}
,
J_{\mathrm{C}}
(see

).
