An illustration of the various beginning types.

Type I:

Any
j\in J
is of type I, if
r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})>0
and
\vartheta_{j}<\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
or
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\varnothing
(and
\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing
).

Type II:

Any
j\in J
is of type II, if
\sum_{\ell=1}^{a_{j}-1}\delta_{j,\ell}=0
(thereby
r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})=0
), and
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing
, and there exists
j^{\prime}\in U
, such that
r_{j^{\prime}}(t)
is a positive constant for all
t\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1},Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}-1\rrbracket
, but
r_{j^{\prime}}(t)=0
for each
t\geq Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}
.

For type I or II, we also call
j
a
blow-up
index.

Type III:

Any
j\in J
is of type III, if
\sum_{\ell=1}^{a_{j}-1}\delta_{j,\ell}=0
(thereby
r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})=0
), and
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing
, and there exists
j^{\prime}\in J
, such that
r_{j^{\prime}}(t)
is a positive constant for all
t\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1},Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}-1\rrbracket
, but
r_{j^{\prime}}(t)=0
for each
t\geq Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}
.

We see that for each
j
of type II or III, the corresponding
j^{\prime}
is unique.

Type IV:

Any
j\in J
is of type IV, if
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}<\vartheta_{j}
or
\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
(and
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}\neq\varnothing
).

Type V:

Any
j\in J
is of type V, if
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
.

Any
j\in J
that is not of type I to V is of type VI (miscellaneous).
More precisely, for each
j
of type VI,
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing
, and one of the following happens:

Type VI.1:

\sum_{\ell=1}^{a_{j}-1}\delta_{j,\ell}=0
(thereby
r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})=0
); and
r_{j^{\prime}}(t)>0
for some
t\geq Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}
, where
j^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
is the unique index with
r_{j^{\prime}}(Q_{a_{j}-1})>r_{j^{\prime}}(Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j})
.

Type VI.2:

r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})>0
.

Type VI.3:

\sum_{\ell=1}^{a_{j}-1}\delta_{j,\ell}>0
, but still
r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})=0
.

or

:
A summary of the classification at the beginning.

The guiding principle underlying this classification is the following: for Types I and II, there are cancellations between them that we shall exploit in order to resolve the blow-up issue. For Types III, IV, V, each would make a significant contribution to the sum, which survives in the limit
N\to\infty
. The sum of
w(\vec{r})
with
\vec{r}
having any index of type VI would decay to zero as
N\to\infty
.

Coming back to Table
1
, let us comment why the bottom left entry is “None”: indeed
\vartheta_{j}<\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
together with
r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})=0
would imply
r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j})=-1
, which is impossible.

:
An illustration of the various ending types.

Here is the second classification: Any
j\in J
is of

Type A:

if
\sum_{\ell=b_{j}+1}^{m}\delta_{j,\ell}=0
. Equivalently, the variable
x_{j}
no longer appears in the monomials after the last times it has been a main variable;

Type B:

if there is a
j^{\prime}\in J_{\mathrm{III}}
, such that
\dot{\vartheta}_{j^{\prime}}=\vartheta_{j^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing
, and
\sum_{\ell=1}^{a_{j^{\prime}}-1}\delta_{j^{\prime},\ell}=0
; and
r_{j}(t)
being a positive constant for all
t\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j^{\prime}}-1},Q_{a_{j^{\prime}}-1}+\vartheta_{j^{\prime}}-1\rrbracket
, and
r_{j}(t)=0
for each
t\geq Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j^{\prime}}
;

Type C:

in all other cases.

Note that Type B definition implicitly includes
a_{j^{\prime}}-1\geq b_{j}
. Further examining the definition, one sees a bijection between Type B and Type III: one type turns into another under
j\leftrightarrow j^{\prime}
correspondence, which implies
|J_{\mathrm{III}}|=|J_{\mathrm{B}}|
. Types A and B would make a significant contribution to the sum, while the sum of
w(\vec{r})
with
\vec{r}
having any index of type C would eventually decay to zero as
N\to\infty
.

4.5
A general bound on walk weights

We next give a general upper bound on the sum of
|w(\vec{r})|
, with fixed index types.
This bound will be repeatedly used for various purposes.
In
Proposition

4.15
we only consider those
\vec{r}
with
J_{\mathrm{II}}=\emptyset
.
Later in this subsection,
Corollary

4.18
extends the analysis to the case of non-empty
J_{\mathrm{II}}
.

As already stated, we always fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
In addition, we fix the following data: indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}
(recall that the set of walks
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{B}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}]
was defined in Definition
4.7
); disjoint sets
J,U\subset\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
from Section
4.4
; the partitioning of
J
into
J_{\mathrm{I}}
,
J_{\mathrm{II}}
,
J_{\mathrm{III}}
,
J_{\mathrm{IV}}
,
J_{\mathrm{V}}
,
J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}
,
J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}
,
J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
and into
J_{\mathrm{A}}
,
J_{\mathrm{B}}
,
J_{\mathrm{C}}
. We assume that this partition is such that
J_{\mathrm{II}}=\emptyset
. Furthermore, we fix the numbers
\vartheta_{j}
for all
j\in J
and also
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
for
13
13

13

Generally speaking, we want to fix as many parameters, as possible on this step. However, fixing
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
,
j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}
, is harder than for other
j
, because fixing such
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
might lead to two cases which we would have to treat differently: it could correspond either to the last jump time, or not — this notion will appear in Step 1 of the proof. Instead, we decided to avoid discussing additional cases and do not fix this parameter.

j\in J\setminus J_{\mathrm{I}}
. Finally, we fix the total number of jumps, which is
\delta
.

For any
h\geq 1
, we define
\mathscr{B}^{h}\subset\mathscr{B}
to be the set of all walks agreeing with all of the above data and such that
(h-1)N^{1/3}<\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)<2hN^{1/3}
.

Proposition 4.15
.

For any
C_{1}>0
, there exist
C_{2}=C_{2}(C_{1})>0
and
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{1})>0
, such that if
|N-N_{\ell}|<C_{1}N^{2/3}
for all
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
then for each
h\geq 1
, we have

\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}^{h}}|w(\vec{r})|<\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|U|-|J|+\frac{1}{3}|J_{\mathrm{I}}|-\frac{1}{3}\bigl(|J_{\mathrm{IV}}|+|\{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}:\,\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing\}|+|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|+\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\}+|J_{\mathrm{C}}|\bigr)}\\
\times C_{2}^{\delta+1}\frac{h^{\delta-2|U|}}{(\delta-2|U|)!}\exp(-C_{3}h^{2})\big((|U|+1)\log(|U|+2)\big)^{|U|}\big(h/(|U|+1)\big)^{C_{3}|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}\prod_{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}.

(38)

Remark 4.16
.

There is some freedom in choosing a form for the upper bound (
38
). The most important parts are the precise power of
N
in the first line, as well as
\frac{1}{(\delta-2|U|)!}
and
\exp(-C_{3}h^{2})
factors which lead to the fast decay as
\delta
or
h
grow.

Proposition 4.17
.

Subsums of (
38
) admit an improved upper bound:

(i)

There is an extra factor
14
14

14

Depending on
t
, the factor might have been either
N^{-1/3}
or
N^{-2/3}
; as in the previous footnote, this depends on whether
t
is a last jump time.
N^{-1/3}
bound works uniformly as the larger of the two.
of
N^{-1/3}
in the right-hand side of (

),
if one sums over the subset of
\mathscr{B}^{h}
containing walks with a fixed jump position: we required
t\in\Delta
for a chosen and fixed
t\in\llbracket 1,Q_{m}\rrbracket
, that is not in
\bigcup_{j\in J}\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1},Q_{a_{j}}\rrbracket\cup\llbracket(Q_{b_{j}-1}+Q_{b_{j}})/2,Q_{b_{j}}\rrbracket
.

(ii)

There is an extra factor of
(y-x)^{-1/2}
in the right-hand side of (
38
),
if one sums over the subset of
\mathscr{B}^{h}
containing walks such that
\mathscr{H}(t)\geq\mathscr{H}(x)
for

. Here for

and

and

.

Proof of Proposition
4.15
.

In this proof
C,c>0
may depend on
C_{1}
, and we take
C_{2}
(resp.
C_{3}
) to be large (resp. small) enough depending on all
C,c
.

For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{A}}
, we denote
\hat{\vartheta}_{j}=\min\{t\in\llbracket 0,(Q_{b_{j}}-Q_{b_{j}-1})/4\rrbracket:Q_{b_{j}}-t\in\Delta,r_{j}(Q_{b_{j}}-t-1)>r_{j}(Q_{b_{j}}-t)\}\}
; if the set is empty, then
\hat{\vartheta}_{j}=\varnothing
. In addition to the data in definition of
\mathscr{B}^{h}
, we fix all
\hat{\vartheta}_{j}
for all
j\in J_{\mathrm{A}}
and all
\delta_{j}
for all
j\in J
. The summation over all possible values for these two parameters is performed at the very end of the proof. We refer to all the data in the definition of
\mathscr{B}^{h}
as well as the last two parameters as
type data
.

We consider arbitrary
\vec{r}
with fixed type data and
observe from (
37
) and each
|N-N_{\ell}|<C_{1}N^{2/3}
that

|w(\vec{r})|<CN^{-\delta}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}\exp(Ch).

(39)

In the rest of the proof we count the total number of the walks with fixed type data. Together with (
39
), this count would imply (
38
).

Here is the plan of the rest of the proof: We first refine the data, by additionally fixing “jump time data”, which contains most of the set
\Delta
, as well
\Delta_{j}
for
j\in J
, but not
\Delta_{j}
for
j\in U
. We then count the number of walks with the refined data fixed. The way we count is by dividing the whole interval
\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket
into segments, and constructing the walk backwards in time from
Q_{m}
down to
0
, segment by segment. Namely, for a segment
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, we count the number of possible values of
\vec{r}(t)
for
t
in this segment, with given
\vec{r}(t^{\prime})
for
t^{\prime}\in\llbracket w,Q_{m}\rrbracket
: this part is subdivided into two steps — choosing
\mathscr{H}(t)
and choosing the heights of the jumps. Finally, we multiply the counts over each segment, and then sum over (unfixed parts of) type data and jump time data.

Step 1: jump time data.

We make the following definitions:

•

For each
j\in U\cup J_{\mathrm{B}}\cup J_{\mathrm{C}}
, we let
l_{j}=\max\Delta_{j}
, i.e. the last jump time.

•

For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
, we let
l_{j}^{\prime}=\max\Delta_{j}\cap\llbracket 1,Q_{a_{j}-1}\rrbracket
. (See the bottom-right panel of
Figure

7
.)

•

We write
\vec{l}=\{l_{j}\}_{j\in U\cup J_{\mathrm{B}}\cup J_{\mathrm{C}}}\cup\{l_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}}
.

•

For each
j\in U
, or
j\in J
with
\sum_{\ell=1}^{a_{j}-1}\delta_{j,\ell}>0
, we let
f_{j}=\min\Delta_{j}
. For
j\in J
with
\sum_{\ell=1}^{a_{j}-1}\delta_{j,\ell}=0
, we let
f_{j}=\varnothing
.

We define
jump time data
for a walk
\vec{r}
, to consist of the following four parts:

1.

The set
\Delta\setminus\vec{l}
, and its intersection with
\Delta_{j}
for each
j\in J
.

2.

f_{j}
for each
j\in U
.

3.

The relative order on the elements in
\vec{l}
: this set can be ordered in two ways — by the values of
l_{j}
,
l^{\prime}_{j}
or by the values of indices
j
— the permutation between these two orderings is what we mean by the relative order.

Given the information above, we consider a collection
\mathfrak{Q}
of disjoint intervals contained in
\llbracket 1,Q_{m}\rrbracket
.
Namely,
\mathfrak{Q}
contains all maximal by inclusion connected intervals
\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
, satisfying the following: it is contained in
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
for some
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
;
it does not contain any number in
\Delta\setminus\vec{l}
;
for each
j\in J
, it does not contain
Q_{a_{j-1}}+\min(\vartheta_{j},\lfloor(Q_{a_{j}}-Q_{a_{j}-1})/4\rfloor)
and does not contain
Q_{a_{j-1}}+\min(\vartheta_{j},\lfloor(Q_{a_{j}}-Q_{a_{j}-1})/4\rfloor)+1
;
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{A}}
, it does not contain
Q_{b_{j}}-\min(\hat{\vartheta}_{j},\lfloor(Q_{b_{j}}-Q_{b_{j}-1})/4\rfloor)
and does not contain
Q_{b_{j}}-\min(\hat{\vartheta}_{j},\lfloor(Q_{b_{j}}-Q_{b_{j}-1})/4\rfloor)+1
. (See the top panel of
Figure

9
.)

The last part of jump time data is the following:

4.

To which interval in
\mathfrak{Q}
does each
l_{j}
and
l^{\prime}_{j}
belong.

.

.

jump time data. Note that the sets

and

,

, and the union

and

are given up to an interval; on the other hand,

is determined, as it coincides with
Q_{2}+\vartheta_{8}=Q_{a_{8}-1}+\vartheta_{8}
.

The collection
\mathfrak{Q}
of intervals can be obtained by breaking
\llbracket 1,Q_{m}\rrbracket
at all the plotted points, as well as
\lfloor Q_{1}/4\rfloor
,
Q_{1}-\lfloor Q_{1}/4\rfloor
, and
Q_{4}-\lfloor(Q_{4}-Q_{3})/4\rfloor
. (Each breaking point either belongs to the interval to its left, or neither.)

Bottom panel:
the procedure of counting the number of
\vec{r}
, with given jump time data (from
Q_{2}
to
Q_{4}
). The interval
\llbracket 0,Q_{4}\rrbracket
is split into segments, which are processed in backwards order. In each segment, we first determine the sum
\mathscr{H}
, and next the jump heights (thus determine
\vec{r}
).

For any given type data, the number of jump time data is bounded by

\frac{(CN^{2/3})^{\delta-|U|-|J_{\mathrm{B}}|-|J_{\mathrm{C}}|-|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|}}{(\delta-|U|-|J_{\mathrm{B}}|-|J_{\mathrm{C}}|-|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|)!}\cdot\frac{(\delta-|U|-|J_{\mathrm{B}}|-|J_{\mathrm{C}}|-|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|)!\delta^{2|J|}}{(\delta-2|U|-\sum_{j\in J}\delta_{j})!\prod_{j\in J}\delta_{j}!}\cdot(\delta+|U|+5m)^{|U|+2m}\\
\times\left(\frac{\delta}{N^{2/3}}\right)^{|\{j\in J_{\mathrm{A}}:\hat{\vartheta}_{j}\neq\varnothing\}|+|J_{\mathrm{IV}}|+|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|+\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\}-|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|}.

(40)

This bound is obtained as follows.
First, the number of possibilities of the set
\Delta\setminus\vec{l}
is
{Q_{m}\choose\delta-|U|-|J_{\mathrm{B}}|-|J_{\mathrm{C}}|-|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|}
, which is bounded by the first factor. Then given the set
\Delta\setminus\vec{l}
, it would be divided into several sets:
\Delta_{j}\setminus\vec{l}
for each
j\in J
, and
\{f_{j}\}
for each
j\in U
, and the remaining numbers (which are
\bigcup_{j\in U}\Delta_{j}\setminus\{f_{j}\}
).
The number of such partition is
\frac{(\delta-|U|-|J_{\mathrm{B}}|-|J_{\mathrm{C}}|-|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|)!}{(1!)^{|U|}|\bigcup_{j\in U}\Delta_{j}\setminus\{f_{j},l_{j}\}|!\prod_{j\in J}|\Delta_{j}\setminus\vec{l}|!}
, which is bounded by the second factor.
For the third and last parts of jump time data, one considers a collection of
|\mathfrak{Q}|
intervals (with
|\mathfrak{Q}|\leq\delta+3m
), and take a multiset of cardinality
|\vec{l}|\leq|U|+2m
from them, and determine the relative order on the elements in
\vec{l}
.
This number of these information is
(|\mathfrak{Q}|)_{|\vec{l}|}
. This is bounded by the third factor.

The last factor comes from that the set
\Delta\setminus\vec{l}
is mandated to contain certain numbers:

•

Q_{b_{j}}-\hat{\vartheta}_{j}
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{A}}
with
\hat{\vartheta}_{j}\neq\varnothing
;
Q_{a_{j}-1}+\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}
,

•

Q_{a_{j}-1}+\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}
with the corresponding
j^{\prime}
(i.e., the unique
j^{\prime}
with
r_{j^{\prime}}(Q_{a_{j}-1})>r_{j^{\prime}}(Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j})
) not in
J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
(otherwise it might be that
Q_{a_{j}-1}+\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=l_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}
).

By rearranging the factors and using
\delta^{2|J|}(\delta+|U|+5m)^{|U|+2m}<C^{\delta}(|U|+1)^{|U|}
, we upper bound (
40
) by

N^{\frac{2}{3}(\delta-|U|-|\{j\in J_{\mathrm{A}}:\hat{\vartheta}_{j}\neq\varnothing\}|-|J_{\mathrm{B}}|-|J_{\mathrm{C}}|-|J_{\mathrm{IV}}|-|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|-\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\})}\frac{C^{\delta}(|U|+1)^{|U|}}{(\delta-2|U|-\sum_{j\in J}\delta_{j})!\prod_{j\in J}\delta_{j}!}.

(41)

Step 2: construction of segments.

We next bound the number of walks, with given type data and jump time data.
Our strategy is to divide
\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket
into several segments, and count
\vec{r}
on them sequentially.
More precisely, we let
D
be the set consisting of the following numbers:

\{Q_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m}
,
\{Q_{a_{j}-1}+\min(\vartheta_{j},\lfloor(Q_{a_{j}}-Q_{a_{j}-1})/4\rfloor)\}_{j\in J}
,
\{Q_{b_{j}}-\min(\hat{\vartheta}_{j},\lfloor(Q_{b_{j}}-Q_{b_{j}-1})/4\rfloor)\}_{j\in J_{\mathrm{A}}}
.

The set
D
splits
\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket
into
|D|-1\leq 3m
discrete intervals, each of the form of
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
with
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket\cap D=\{v,w\}
.
We inductively count the number of choices for the walk on each
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, backwards in time, i.e., starting from the rightmost interval.
For each segment
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
we split the count into two steps.
We first estimate the number of possible
\mathscr{H}(t)
,
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, given
\vec{r}(w)
. Then we argue conditionally on
\mathscr{H}(t)
,
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, thinking about the latter as being uniformly random and estimate the number of possible
\vec{r}(t)
,
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, with given
\vec{r}(w)
and
\mathscr{H}
. Since jump time data is given and it includes jump times, the main task in the second step is to estimate the number of possible ‘jump heights’.

Step 3: Choosing
\mathscr{H}(t)
in a fixed segment.

\mathscr{H}(t)
,
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
is a non-negative function with increments
\pm 1
, with given
\mathscr{H}(w)
, and satisfies one of the following:

1.

\mathscr{H}(t)\geq\mathscr{H}(w)
for all
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, if
v=Q_{b_{j}}-\hat{\vartheta}_{j}\wedge\lfloor(Q_{b_{j}}-Q_{b_{j}-1})/4\rfloor
and
w=Q_{b_{j}}
for some
j\in J_{\mathrm{A}}
.

2.

\mathscr{H}(t)\geq\mathscr{H}(v)
for all
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, if
v=Q_{a_{j}-1}
and
w=Q_{a_{j}-1}+\lfloor(Q_{a_{j}}-Q_{a_{j}-1})/4\rfloor
for some
j\in J
with
\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
.

3.

\mathscr{H}(t)\geq\mathscr{H}(v)
for all
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
and
\mathscr{H}(v)=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in J\cup U:j^{\prime}\neq j,f_{j^{\prime}}\not\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w\rrbracket}r_{j^{\prime}}(w)
, if
v=Q_{a_{j}-1}
and
w=Q_{a_{j}-1}+\lfloor(Q_{a_{j}}-Q_{a_{j}-1})/4\rfloor
for some
j\in J
with
\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
and additionally
f_{j}=\varnothing
or
j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
, and
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w\rrbracket\cap\Delta\subset F
, where

F=\left(\bigcup_{j^{\prime}\in J:f_{j^{\prime}}\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w\rrbracket}\Delta_{j^{\prime}}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{j^{\prime}\in U:f_{j^{\prime}}\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w\rrbracket}\{f_{j^{\prime}},l_{j^{\prime}}\}\right).

(42)

This is because, for each
j^{\prime}\in J\cup U
with
j^{\prime}\neq j
and
f_{j^{\prime}}\not\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w\rrbracket
,
r_{j^{\prime}}
must be constant on
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
; while
r_{j^{\prime}}(v)=0
for all other
j^{\prime}
.

4.

\mathscr{H}(t)\geq\mathscr{H}(v)=\mathscr{H}(w)+1
for all
t\in\llbracket v,w-1\rrbracket
, if
v=Q_{a_{j}-1}
and
w=Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}
for some
j\in J
with
\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing
.

5.

\mathscr{H}(v)=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in J\cup U:j^{\prime}\neq j,f_{j^{\prime}}\not\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w\rrbracket}r_{j^{\prime}}(w)-1
, if
v=Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}
for some
j\in J
with
\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing
, and
f_{j}=\varnothing
or
j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
, and
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w\rrbracket\cap\Delta\subset F
, where
F
is the same as above.

This is because in this case, for each
j^{\prime}\in J\cup U
with
j^{\prime}\neq j
and
f_{j^{\prime}}\not\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w\rrbracket
,
r_{j^{\prime}}
must be constant on
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1},w\rrbracket
; while
r_{j^{\prime}}(Q_{a_{j}-1})=0
for all other
j^{\prime}
.
Therefore
\mathscr{H}(Q_{a_{j}-1})
is determined by
\vec{r}(w)
. On the other hand there is
\mathscr{H}(Q_{a_{j}-1})=\mathscr{H}(v)+1
.

6.

No further constraints: the remaining cases.

The number of possible values for
\mathscr{H}(t)
,
t\in\llbracket v,w-1\rrbracket
, is then (up to a constant factor) bounded by

\begin{split}&2^{w-v}(\hat{\vartheta}_{j}^{-1/2}+N^{-1/3}),\quad 2^{w-v}N^{-1/3},\quad 2^{w-v}N^{-2/3},\\
&2^{w-v}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2},\quad 2^{w-v}N^{-1/3},\quad 2^{w-v},\end{split}

(43)

in these cases, respectively.
These bounds are by
Lemma

A.1
(stated and proved in
Section

A.1
), which counts the number of Bernoulli walks under various constraints. More precisely, we use (iii), (iii), (iv), (v), (ii), (i) there, in these 6 cases respectively, with
M=0
.
Note that by directly applying
Lemma

A.1
, the bounds would be
2^{w-v}
multiplying a power of
(w-v)^{-1}
.
It is convenient to keep
w-v
in the power of
2
, but replace it with an order of magnitude in the polynomial factors; this is how we get (
43
).

Step 4: choosing jump heights in a fixed segment.

We count the number of possible
\vec{r}(t)
,
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, given
\mathscr{H}(t)
,
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
.
Consider

K=\llbracket v+1,w\rrbracket\cap\left(\Delta\setminus\vec{l}\right).

The main task would be to determine
\vec{r}(s-1)
and
\vec{r}(s)
for each
s\in K
, and
\vec{r}(v)
;
and from there,
\vec{r}
(as well as the set
\llbracket v+1,w\rrbracket\cap\vec{l}
) would be uniquely determined, as we will see shortly.

s
s^{\prime}
r_{9}(s)
r_{3}(s)
r_{8}(s)
r_{1}(s)
r_{4}(s)
r_{1}(s^{\prime})
r_{4}(s^{\prime})

Figure 10
:
An illustration of determining
\vec{r}
in an interval
\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket
satisfying
\llbracket s+1,s^{\prime}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\subset\vec{l}
, with given
\vec{r}(s^{\prime})
and
\mathscr{H}
on this interval.
Here it is known that
\llbracket s+1,s^{\prime}\rrbracket\cap\Delta=\{l_{3},l_{8},l_{9}\}
, with
l_{8}<l_{3}<l_{9}
. Necessarily
r_{1}(s)=r_{1}(s^{\prime})
and
r_{4}(s)=r_{4}(s^{\prime})
, while

r_{3}(s)
,
r_{8}(s)
, and
r_{9}(s)
can be any positive integers satisfying
r_{9}(s)\geq 3
,
r_{3}(s)+r_{8}(s)+r_{9}(s)\leq 8
.

Then given these numbers
\vec{r}(s)
,
l_{8}
is the smallest
t
with
\mathscr{H}(t)<r_{1}(s)+r_{3}(s)+r_{4}(s)+r_{8}(s)+r_{9}(s)
,
l_{3}
is the smallest
t
with
\mathscr{H}(t)<r_{1}(s)+r_{3}(s)+r_{4}(s)+r_{9}(s)
, and
l_{9}
is the smallest
t
with
\mathscr{H}(t)<r_{1}(s)+r_{4}(s)+r_{9}(s)
.

Recall that, from jump time data, it may already be known that
w\in\vec{l}
, when
w=Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}
for some
j\in J
.
We let
w^{\prime}=w-1
in this case
15
15

15

Note that in this case,
\vec{r}(w^{\prime})=\vec{r}(w-1)
is determined by
\mathscr{H}
and
\vec{r}(w)
.
,
and
w^{\prime}=w
otherwise.

Take any
s\in K\cup\{v\}
, let
s^{\prime}
denote the smallest number in
\llbracket s,w^{\prime}\rrbracket
with
s^{\prime}+1\in K
, or
s^{\prime}=w^{\prime}
if no such number exists (i.e.,
s=\max K\cup\{v\}
).
We now determine
\vec{r}
on
\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket
given
\vec{r}(s^{\prime})
.

Denote
u[s]=|\Delta\cap\llbracket s+1,s^{\prime}\rrbracket|
.
Note that
\Delta\cap\llbracket s+1,s^{\prime}\rrbracket\subset\vec{l}
. It remains to determine
r_{j}(s)
for
j
with
l_{j}
or
l_{j}^{\prime}
in
\Delta\cap\llbracket s+1,s^{\prime}\rrbracket
16
16

16

Note that the collection of such
j
, and the ordering of these
l_{j}
and
l_{j}^{\prime}
are already determined by jump time data
; from there
\vec{r}
on
\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket
would be determined (see
Figure

10
).
A constraint is that their sum is at most
\max_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}-\sum_{j\neq i_{\ell}}r_{j}(w^{\prime})
while (a particular) one of them is at least
\min_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}-\sum_{j\neq i_{\ell}}r_{j}(w^{\prime})
, where
\ell
is the number with
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket\subset\llbracket Q_{\ell-1},Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
.
Besides, since
\mathscr{H}
on
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
is given, sometimes this already determines the sum of some entries of
\vec{r}(s)
.
More precisely, there are three cases:

(a)

v\in\{Q_{a_{j}-1},Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\}
for some
j\in J
with
f_{j}=\varnothing
or
j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
, and

\Delta\cap\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,s\rrbracket\subset F
while
\Delta\cap\llbracket s+1,s^{\prime}\rrbracket\not\subset F
(for
F
defined in (
42
)).

In this case necessarily
u[s]\geq 1
, and
\sum_{j^{\prime}\in J\cup U:f_{j^{\prime}}\not\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,s\rrbracket}r_{j^{\prime}}(s)=\mathscr{H}(Q_{a_{j}-1})
. Thereby, the number of possible choices of
\vec{r}(s)
would be bounded by
\frac{(\max_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}-\min_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}+1)^{u[s]-1}}{(u[s]-1)!}
.

(b)

In the remaining case, the number of possible choices of
\vec{r}(s)
would be bounded by
\frac{(\max_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}-\min_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}+1)^{u[s]}}{(u[s])!}
.

We now take any
s\in K
, and determine
\vec{r}(s-1)
given
\vec{r}(s)
. There are also three cases:

(d)

s=f_{j}
for some
j\in J\cup U
. In this case
\vec{r}(s-1)
is determined by
\vec{r}(s)
.

(e)

v\in\{Q_{a_{j}-1},Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\}
for some
j\in J
with
f_{j}=\varnothing
or
j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
, and

\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,s-1\rrbracket\cap\Delta\subset F
, while
s\not\in F
. In this case, necessarily
\sum_{j^{\prime}\not\in J\cup U:f_{j^{\prime}}\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,s-1\rrbracket}r_{j^{\prime}}(s-1)=\mathscr{H}(Q_{a_{j}-1})
, and to ensure this there are at most
|J|+|U|
possible choices of
\vec{r}(s-1)
.

(f)

In the remaining case, the number of possible choices of
\vec{r}(s-1)
would be at most
4hN^{1/3}
.
This is because for every
j\in J\cup U
, assuming that
s\in\Delta_{j}
, there are at most
2r_{j}(s)
possible choices of
r_{j}(s-1)
; and
\sum_{j\in J\cup U}2r_{j}(s)<4hN^{1/3}
.

We note that the bound of
ChN^{1/3}
in the last case (f) is the main reason for us to exclude the information of the set
\Delta_{j}
(for
j\in U
) from jump time data, and to construct
\mathscr{H}
and
\vec{r}
backwards.
More precisely, specifying each
\Delta_{j}
ahead of time would lead to an extra factor of roughly
|U|^{\delta}
; while if we consider the number of possible
\vec{r}(s)
with given
\vec{r}(s-1)
, it would only be bounded by
2|U|hN^{1/3}
, also leading to an extra factor of
|U|^{\delta}
when taking the product over all
s
.

As another remark, in case (a) and case (e) we see that there are constraints on the sum of
r_{j^{\prime}}(s)
or
r_{j^{\prime}}(s-1)
for some
j^{\prime}
.
These complement case 3 and case 5 in counting the number of
\mathscr{H}
, in Step 3.

Putting the above cases together, we have that the number of
\vec{r}
on
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, given
\mathscr{H}
on the same interval, is bounded by

\prod_{s\in K\cup\{v\}}\frac{(\max_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}-\min_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}+1)^{u_{-}[s]}}{(u_{-}[s])!}\cdot(|J|+|U|)(4hN^{1/3})^{|K\setminus\{f_{j}\}_{j\in J\cup U}|-u_{-}},

(44)

where each
u_{-}[s]=u[s]-1
or
u[s]
, depending on whether
s
is of case (a) or (b); and
u_{-}=0
or
1
, depending on the existence of any
s
of case (e).

We apply
Lemma

A.3
(stated and proved in
Section

A.1
) in order to upper bound (
44
). Note that we have counted the number of possible choices for
\mathscr{H}
on the previous step, and now we treat (
44
) as a random variable with respect to the uniform measure on all these choices. This random variable and this measure are the content of
Lemma

A.3
. Note that all six cases from (
43
) of Step 3 are covered by the lemma applied to
\mathscr{H}
or its shifts by a constant.
More precisely, we always take
X=w-v
or
w-v-1
.
Then in case 1 and 2, we take
G=0
, and sum over
H
;
in case 3 we take
G=0
and a fixed
H
;
in case 4 we take
H=G=0
;
in case 5 we take fixed
H
and
G
;
in case 6 we fix
G
and sum over all
H
.
The number
L
is chosen to fit the constraint
\max\mathscr{H}<2hN^{1/3}
.

We conclude that, given type data and jump time data, the number of
\vec{r}
on
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
(with given
\vec{r}(w)
) is at most the number of choices for
\mathscr{H}(t)
(upper bounded on the previous step), multiplied by (with the convention that
0^{0}=1
)

C^{u(v)}\log(u(v)+1)^{u(v)}(u(v)^{-u(v)/2}+u(v)^{-u(v)}h^{u(v)})N^{u(v)/3}\cdot(|J|+|U|)(4hN^{1/3})^{u^{\prime}(v)},

(45)

where

u(v)=\sum_{s\in K\cup\{v\}}u[s]=|\vec{l}\cap\llbracket v+1,w^{\prime}\rrbracket|,

and

u^{\prime}(v)=|K\setminus\{f_{j}\}_{j\in J\cup U}|=|(\Delta\setminus\{f_{j}\}_{j\in J\cup U})\cap\llbracket v+1,w^{\prime}\rrbracket|-u(v).

And there is an extra factor of
N^{-1/3}
, under the following condition:

(ae)

v\in\{Q_{a_{j}-1},Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\}
for some
j\in J
with
f_{j}=\varnothing
or
j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
, and
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w\rrbracket\cap\Delta\setminus F
is not empty, with its smallest number in
\llbracket v+1,w^{\prime}\rrbracket
. This holds if one of (a) and (e) happens for one
s
.

Step 4’: choosing jump heights when the increment is large.
In the next step we also need a version of the above count with an additional requirement that
\max_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}-\min_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}>(h-1)N^{1/3}/(3m)
. We claim that in this situation the same bounds (
43
) and (
45
) hold with an extra overall factor of
C\exp(-ch^{2})
.
Indeed, to see that we consider two situations. If the boundary values of
\mathscr{H}
satisfy
|\mathscr{H}(w)-\mathscr{H}(v)|>(h-1)N^{1/3}/(6m)
in cases 1–3, 5, 6 of Step 3, then there would be an extra factor of
C\exp(-ch^{2})
on Step 3, by taking
M=(h-1)N^{1/3}/(6m)
in
Lemma

A.1
.
(In particular, in case 3 where
Lemma

A.1
(iv) is applied, we have
H=|\mathscr{H}(w)-\mathscr{H}(v)|>M
; and in case 5 where
Lemma

A.1
(ii) is applied, we have
|G-H|=|\mathscr{H}(w)-\mathscr{H}(v)|>M
.)
Otherwise, if
|\mathscr{H}(w)-\mathscr{H}(v)|\leq(h-1)N^{1/3}/(6m)
, then we apply (
96
) in
Lemma

A.3
(with
K=(h-1)N^{1/3}/(12m)
) and get an extra factor of
C\exp(-ch^{2})
in the expectation in Step 4.

Step 5: product of contributions of segments.

We can now bound the number of possible choices for
\vec{r}(t)
,
t\in\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket
(with given type data and jump time data), by multiplying (
43
), and (
45
), over all
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
.

We first take the product of (
45
). Note that the sum of all
u(v)
is at least
|U|
and at most
|U|+m
. The former implies
u(v)\geq\frac{1}{3m}|U|
for at least one segment
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
.
For this
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, then we have

u(v)^{-u(v)/2}+u(v)^{-u(v)}h^{u(v)}\leq(3m/|U|)^{|U|/(6m)}+\Big[(3mh/|U|)^{|U|/(3m)}\ \text{or}\ (3mh/|U|)^{|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}\Big];

(46)

where for the first term we use the fact that
x\mapsto x^{-\frac{x}{2}}
is decreasing and upper bound it at
x=\frac{1}{3m}|U|
, and the two cases for the second term correspond to the regime
h<\frac{1}{3m}|U|
(so
\frac{h}{u(v)}\leq\frac{3mh}{|U|}<1
and we minimize the exponent) or
h\geq\frac{1}{3m}|U|
(so
\max(\frac{h}{u(v)},1)\leq\frac{3mh}{|U|}
and we maximize the exponent at
|U|+m
).

For other
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
’s, we use the fact that the function
x\mapsto x^{-x}h^{x}
,
x>0
, achieves its maximum at
x=\frac{h}{e}
: it is increasing when
x<h/e
, and decreasing when
x>h/e
. So instead we have

u(v)^{-u(v)/2}+u(v)^{-u(v)}h^{u(v)}\leq 1+e^{h/e},

(47)

and the product of all such
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
is at most
(1+e^{h/e})^{3m}<C^{h}
.
Combining (
46
) and (
47
), we upper bound
u(v)
by
|U|+C
and slightly adjust the expression to avoid 0 in
\log(\cdot)
and denominator, and get

\prod_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}\log(u(v)+1)^{u(v)}(u(v)^{-u(v)/2}+u(v)^{-u(v)}h^{u(v)})<C^{|U|+h}\log(|U|+2)^{|U|}(h/(|U|+1))^{c|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}},

where
c
is a positive constant between
\frac{1}{6m}
and
1
.
Therefore, the product of (
45
) over
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
is bounded from above by

C^{\delta+h}\log(|U|+2)^{|U|}(h/(|U|+1))^{c|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}h^{\delta-2|U|}N^{(\delta-|U|-|\{j\in J:f_{j}\neq\varnothing\}|-|\{j\in J:Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\in\vec{l}\}|)/3},

(48)

where we use that
\sum_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}u^{\prime}(v)\leq\delta-2|U|
, and

\sum_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}u(v)+u^{\prime}(v)=\sum_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}|(\Delta\setminus\{f_{j}\}_{j\in J\cup U})\cap\llbracket v+1,w^{\prime}\rrbracket|\\
=\delta-|U|-|\{j\in J:f_{j}\neq\varnothing\}|-|\{j\in J:Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\in\vec{l}\}|.

For
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
under (ae), there is an extra factor of
N^{-1/3}
.
The number of
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
under (ae) is the same as the number of
j\in J
, satisfying the following two conditions:

•

f_{j}=\varnothing
or
j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
, and

•

Let
w_{j}=Q_{a_{j}-1}+\lfloor(Q_{a_{j}}-Q_{a_{j}-1})/4\rfloor
if
\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
, and let
w_{j}
be the smallest number in the set
D
(from Step 2) larger than
Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}
if
\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing
. Let
F_{j}
be the set
F
of (
42
) for
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w_{j}\rrbracket
.

We ask for
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w_{j}\rrbracket\cap(\Delta\setminus F_{j})
to be non-empty;
besides, when
Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\in\vec{l}
,
the smallest number of the set
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w_{j}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\setminus F_{j}
should not be
Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}
.

(This follows from (ae), and that
w^{\prime}=Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}-1
for the interval
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\rrbracket
.)

Then we get a factor at most

N^{-|\{j\in J:f_{j}=\varnothing\text{ or }j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}},\;\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w_{j}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\not\subset F_{j},\;Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\not\in\vec{l}\}|/3}.

(49)

Multiplying (
48
) and (
49
), we get

C^{\delta+h}\log(|U|+2)^{|U|}(h/(|U|+1))^{c|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}h^{\delta-2|U|}\\
\times N^{(\delta-|U|-|\{j\in J:f_{j}\neq\varnothing\}|-|\{j\in J:f_{j}=\varnothing\text{ or }j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}},\;\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w_{j}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\not\subset F_{j}\}|)/3}.

(50)

We next take the product of (
43
) over
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, and get

C2^{Q_{m}}N^{-|\{j\in J:f_{j}=\varnothing\text{ or }j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}},\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,w_{j}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\subset F_{j}\}|/3}\\
\times\exp(-ch^{2})\prod_{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}\prod_{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing}N^{-1/3}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{A}},\hat{\vartheta}_{j}\neq\varnothing}\hat{\vartheta}_{j}^{-1/2}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{A}},\hat{\vartheta}_{j}=\varnothing}N^{-1/3}.

(51)

Here the factor of
C\exp(-ch^{2})
is from Step 4’: because
\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)>(h-1)N^{1/3}
, we should necessary have
\max_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}-\min_{\llbracket v,w\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}>(h-1)N^{1/3}/(3m)
for at least one
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
.

In summary, by multiplying (
50
) and (
51
), the number of possible
\vec{r}
(with given type data and jump time data) is bounded by

2^{Q_{m}}C^{\delta+1}N^{-\frac{1}{3}|\{j\in J:f_{j}=\varnothing\text{ or }j\in J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}\}|}N^{\frac{1}{3}(\delta-|U|-|\{j\in J:f_{j}\neq\varnothing\}|)}h^{\delta-2|U|}\log(|U|+2)^{|U|}(h/(|U|+1))^{c|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}\\
\times\exp(-ch^{2})\prod_{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}\prod_{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing}N^{-1/3}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{A}},\hat{\vartheta}_{j}\neq\varnothing}\hat{\vartheta}_{j}^{-1/2}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{A}},\hat{\vartheta}_{j}=\varnothing}N^{-1/3}.

(52)

Step 6: Final summation.
By multiplying (
41
) and (
52
), and summing over
\delta_{j}
for
j\in J
and
\hat{\vartheta}_{j}
for
j\in J_{\mathrm{A}}
, we get

2^{Q_{m}}N^{\delta-|U|-\frac{1}{3}(|J|+|\{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing\}|+|J_{\mathrm{A}}|+|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|)-\frac{2}{3}(|J_{\mathrm{B}}|+|J_{\mathrm{C}}|+|J_{\mathrm{IV}}|+|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|+\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\})}\\
\times C^{\delta+1}\frac{h^{\delta-2|U|}}{(\delta-2|U|)!}\exp(-ch^{2})((|U|+1)\log(|U|+2))^{|U|}(h/(|U|+1))^{c|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}\prod_{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}.

Using that
|J_{\mathrm{I}}|+\cdots+|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|=|J_{\mathrm{A}}|+|J_{\mathrm{B}}|+|J_{\mathrm{C}}|=|J|
, and that
|J_{\mathrm{III}}|=|J_{\mathrm{B}}|
, and
|\{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing\}|=|\{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}:\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing\}|+|J_{\mathrm{V}}|
, we can rewrite the exponent of
N
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.17
.

We explain how to adapt the previous proof to get the improvements, under the extra requirements.

Improvement (i):
Two scenarios can happen if a fixed
t
is contained in
\Delta
: it is contained in
\Delta\setminus\vec{l}
, or
\vec{l}
.
In the first case, there would be an extra factor of
\delta N^{-2/3}
in (
40
).
This would lead to an extra factor of
N^{-2/3}
in the final bound.

In the second case,
in Step 4, in addition to (a) and (b), there is another case (b’) where the fixed number
t\in\llbracket s+1,s^{\prime}\rrbracket
. In this case (b’), necessarily
u[s]\geq 1
, and the number of possible choices of
\vec{r}(s)
would be at most
C\frac{(\max_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}-\min_{\llbracket s,s^{\prime}\rrbracket}\mathscr{H}+1)^{u[s]-1}}{(u[s]-1)!}
.
Then in (
44
), we would have
u_{-}[s]=u[s]-1
under (b’).
We note that for any
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
, at most one of (a), (b’), (e) can happen, and for at most one
s
.
Then (
45
), there would be an extra factor of
N^{-1/3}
when
t\in\llbracket v+1,w^{\prime}\rrbracket
.
This would lead to an extra factor of
N^{-1/3}
in the final bound.

Improvement (ii):

In Step 1, jump time data would contain the following extra information: for each
l_{j}
or
l^{\prime}_{j}
, whether it is contained in
\llbracket 1,y\rrbracket
, or
\rrbracket y+1,Q_{m}\rrbracket
.
In Step 2, the set
D
would contain
y
.
In Step 3, when
\llbracket v,w\rrbracket=\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
, the function
\mathscr{H}(t)
for
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
would be required to have
\mathscr{H}(t)\geq\mathscr{H}(v)
for all
t\in\llbracket v,w\rrbracket
.
Then the number of such
\mathscr{H}
would be bounded by
C2^{w-v}(y-x)^{-1/2}
, which has an extra factor of order
(y-x)^{-1/2}
compared to the last case in Step 3, corresponding to the probability for a Bernoulli random walk with
y-x
steps to be minimized at
x
.
This would lead to an extra factor of
(y-x)^{-1/2}
in (
51
), and thereby in (
52
) and the final bound.
∎

In
Proposition

4.15
we only consider walks where
J_{\mathrm{II}}=\emptyset
.
We can also deduce a bound without this assumption, which will also be useful.

Corollary 4.18
.

Under the same setup as
Proposition

4.15
except for that
J_{\mathrm{II}}
is allowed to be non-empty, the estimate becomes

\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}^{h}}|w(\vec{r})|<\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|U|-|J|+\frac{1}{3}|J_{\mathrm{I}}|+\frac{1}{3}|J_{\mathrm{II}}|-\frac{1}{3}\bigl(|J_{\mathrm{IV}}|+|\{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}:\,\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing\}|+|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|+\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\}+|J_{\mathrm{C}}|\bigr)}\\
\times C_{2}^{\delta+1}\frac{h^{\delta-2|U|}}{(\delta-2|U|)!}\exp(-C_{3}h^{2})\big((|U|+1)\log(|U|+2)\big)^{|U|}\big(h/(|U|+1)\big)^{C_{3}|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}\prod_{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}.

To prove this, we note that each walk can be associated with another walk without type II indices, as follows.

Definition 4.19
.

For
\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}
, we construct another
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}
without type II indices, as follows.
For each type II index
j\in J_{\mathrm{II}}
, and the corresponding
j^{\prime}
, we let
r_{j}=s_{j}+s_{j^{\prime}}
and
r_{j^{\prime}}=0
.
For the remaining
j\in J\cup U
we let
r_{j}=s_{j}
.
We use
\mathcal{Q}
to denote the map
\vec{s}\mapsto\vec{r}
.
If
J_{\mathrm{II}}=\emptyset
, then
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}
.

Then we can reduce
Corollary

4.18
to
Proposition

4.15
.

Proof of
Corollary

4.18
.

From (
37
) we see that

|w(\vec{r})|<CN^{-|J_{\mathrm{II}}|}\exp(Ch)|w(\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r}))|,

for any
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}^{h}
. Here the factor
N^{-|J_{\mathrm{II}}|}
comes from the second line of (
37
), since
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})
has
|J_{\mathrm{II}}|
fewer jumps than
\vec{r}
; while the factor
\exp(Ch)
is from the third line of (
37
).

We shall apply
Proposition

4.15
to all
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})
.
Note that
J_{\mathrm{II}}
and
U
are fixed in
\mathscr{B}^{h}
, so at most
|U|^{|J_{\mathrm{II}}|}\leq|U|^{m}
different walks
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}^{h}
would have the same image under
\mathcal{Q}
.
On the other hand, in
Proposition

4.15
, the sets
J,U
and the partition of
J
are fixed.
However, for different
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}^{h}
, the sets
J,U
and the partition of
J
for
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})
are not necessarily the same; in fact, there are at most
|U|^{|J_{\mathrm{II}}|}\leq|U|^{m}
possibilities, and we need to apply
Proposition

4.15
to each of them.
Then the upper bound follows, as we replace
|J_{\mathrm{I}}|
by
|J_{\mathrm{I}}|+|J_{\mathrm{II}}|
and
|U|
by
|U|-|J_{\mathrm{II}}|
in the right-hand side of (
38
), and multiply the bound by
N^{-|J_{\mathrm{II}}|}\exp(Ch)
and
|U|^{m}\cdot|U|^{m}=|U|^{2m}
.
The factors
\exp(ch)
and
|U|^{2m}
can be respectively absorbed into
\exp(-C_{3}h^{2})
and
C_{2}^{\delta+1}
, by taking larger
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
.
∎

4.6
Cancellations and the first regularization

In this section we apply the general bound of
Proposition

4.15
to produce two uniform upper bounds for the subsums of the sum of weights (
36
).

Proposition 4.20
.

For a fixed
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}
, recalling
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{B}[i_{1},\dots,i_{m}]
from
Section

4.3.2
, we have

\left|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}}w(\vec{r})\right|<C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|J|}.

The proposition implies that the expression in
Proposition

4.3
is of the desired order
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}
, since the number of possible
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
with given
|J|
is of order
N^{|J|}
.

We recall that the announced limit
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is an improper integral, involving
\varepsilon\to 0
transition. Before producing this integral, we also introduce a bound which would imply its convergence, as well as uniform convergence of the sums representing its discrete counterpart.

Definition 4.21
.

Take any
\epsilon
with
\hat{C}^{-1}N^{-2/3}<\epsilon<\hat{C}
for a universal constant
\hat{C}>0
; any
C,c
can depend on
\hat{C}
. We let
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}=\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}]
be the set of all walks
\vec{r}
of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
, with the following requirements:

•

J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}=J_{\mathrm{C}}=\emptyset
.

•

For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}\cup J_{\mathrm{II}}\cup J_{\mathrm{IV}}
,
\vartheta_{j}\wedge\dot{\vartheta}_{j}>\epsilon N^{2/3}
.

Proposition 4.22
.

We have

\left|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}}w(\vec{r})\right|<C\sqrt{\epsilon}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|J|}.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proofs of
Proposition

4.20
, and
Proposition

4.22
.

As already alluded to, there is a blow-up issue, which we now resolve by exploiting cancellations between type I and type II.

For this, we use the map
\mathcal{Q}
from
Definition

4.19
.
Note that a walk
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}
if and only if
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}
.
For
\vec{r}
satisfying
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}
, i.e., without type II indices,
the pre-image
\{\vec{s}:\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}\}
contains order
N^{|J_{\mathrm{I}}|}
many walks, since for each
j
that is of type I in
\vec{r}
, it can be made type II, with order
N
many choices of corresponding
j^{\prime}
.
(Note that this differs from the setting in
Corollary

4.18
, where
U
and
J_{\mathrm{II}}
are fixed.)

Now from (
37
), the sum of
w(\vec{s})
over all the order
N^{|J_{\mathrm{I}}|}
many walks can be bounded as follows.

Lemma 4.23
.

We have

\left|\sum_{\vec{s}:\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}w(\vec{s})\right|<C\prod_{j\in J_{I}}\left(|U|N^{-1}+N^{-1}\vartheta_{j}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)\right)|w(\vec{r})|.

(53)

Proof.

We claim that

\left|\sum_{\vec{s}:\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}w(\vec{s})\right|<\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}}\left(C(|U|+1)N^{-1}+1-(1+CN^{-1}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))^{-\vartheta_{j}}\right)|w(\vec{r})|.

(54)

Indeed, here the factors in the bound are from the following reason.
For each type I index
j
of
\vec{r}
, if
j
is type II for some
\vec{s}
, then in the
\ell=a_{j}
factor in (
37
),
w(\vec{r})
and
w(\vec{s})
differ in the following ways:

•

in the first line of (
37
),
w(\vec{s})
has an extra factor of
-1
;

•

in the second line of (
37
),
w(\vec{s})
has an extra factor of
N_{a_{j}}^{-1}
;

•

in the third line of (
37
),
s_{j}(t-1)=r_{j}(t-1)-r_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})
, for
t\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\rrbracket
. (And
s_{j}(t-1)=r_{j}(t-1)
for any other
t
.)

The first two above give a factor of
-N_{a_{j}}^{-1}
, while the last one gives a factor smaller than
1
, but at least
(1+CN^{-1}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))^{-\vartheta_{j}}
.
On the other hand, to make
j
of type II in
\vec{s}
, the corresponding
j^{\prime}
has at least
N_{a_{j}}-|U|-m
and at most
N_{a_{j}}
many choices.
The product of this number of choices and the factors above, plus
1
, gives

-(1-(|U|+m)N_{a_{j}}^{-1})(1+CN^{-1}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))^{-\vartheta_{j}}+1,

whose absolute value is bounded by
C(|U|+1)N^{-1}+\left(1-(1+CN^{-1}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))^{-\vartheta_{j}}\right)
.

Hence, we have proven (
54
). In order to deduce (
53
) from it, we notice that since since
CN^{-1}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)>0
, and
\vartheta_{j}>0
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}
, we have

(1+CN^{-1}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))^{-\vartheta_{j}}\geq 1-CN^{-1}\vartheta_{j}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t).\qed

On the other hand,
Proposition

4.15
and
Proposition

4.17
give the following.

Let
\mathscr{B}^{{h}}_{+}
be the collection of all
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}
, satisfying
\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)>{h}N^{1/3}
and
J_{\mathrm{II}}=\emptyset
, and the following fixed:
J_{\mathrm{I}}
,
J_{\mathrm{III}}
,
J_{\mathrm{IV}}
,
J_{\mathrm{V}}
,
J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}
,
J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}
,
J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
, and
\vartheta_{j}
for each
j\in J
,
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
for each
j\in J\setminus J_{\mathrm{I}}
.

Corollary 4.24
.

For any
h^{\prime}\geq 0
, we have

\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}^{{h^{\prime}}}_{+}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}}\frac{|U|+\vartheta_{j}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)}{N}|w(\vec{r})|<C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|J|-\frac{1}{3}(|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|+\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\}+|J_{\mathrm{C}}|)}\\
\times\exp(-c{h^{\prime}}^{2})\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}}\frac{\vartheta_{j}^{-1/2}}{N^{1/3}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}},\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\frac{\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}}{N^{1/3}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}},\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing}\frac{1}{N^{2/3}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}.

(55)

There is an extra factor of
N^{-1/3}
or
(y-x)^{-1/2}
in the right-hand side, under the condition (i) or (ii), respectively, specified in
Proposition

4.17
.

Proof.

In the upper bound (
38
), by summing over
\delta\geq 2|U|
, we have

\sum_{\delta=2|U|}^{\infty}C^{\delta}\frac{h^{\delta-2|U|}}{(\delta-2|U|)!}=C^{2|U|}\exp(Ch).

Next, for walks from
\mathscr{B}^{h}
of Proposition
4.15
, we upper bound
|U|+\vartheta_{j}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)
in the left-hand side of (
55
) with
(|U|+1)\vartheta_{j}(2h+1)N^{1/3}
. Then by summing over
U
(noting the number of possible
U
with given
|U|
is at most
{N\choose|U|}\leq\frac{N^{|U|}}{|U|!}
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{U}(|U|+1)^{|J_{\mathrm{I}}|}N^{-|U|}C^{2|U|}\big((|U|+1)\log(|U|+2)\big)^{|U|}\big(h/(|U|+1)\big)^{c|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{|U|=0}^{\infty}\frac{C^{2|U|}}{|U|!}(|U|+1)^{m}\big((|U|+1)\log(|U|+2)\big)^{|U|}\big(h/(|U|+1)\big)^{c|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}

\displaystyle<C\exp(Ch\log(h+1)).

We arrive at:

C\exp(-c{h}^{2}+Ch\log(h+1))\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|J|-\frac{1}{3}(|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|+\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\}+|J_{\mathrm{C}}|)}\\
\times\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}}\frac{\vartheta_{j}^{-1/2}(2h+1)}{N^{1/3}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}},\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\frac{\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}}{N^{1/3}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}},\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing}\frac{1}{N^{2/3}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}.

Summing over dyadic growing
h=h^{\prime},2h^{\prime},4h^{\prime},8h^{\prime},\dots
, the conclusion of the corollary follows.
Under the conditions (i) or (ii) specified in
Proposition

4.17
, the extra factors remain unchanged throughout the proof.
∎

By combining
Lemma

4.23
and
Corollary

4.24
, we get
Proposition

4.20
and
Proposition

4.22
.

Proof of
Proposition

4.20
.

We first sum over
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}^{0}_{+}
in
Lemma

4.23
, and apply
Corollary

4.24
(using that
|U|<CN^{2/3}
), and get

C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|J|-\frac{1}{3}(|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|+\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\}+|J_{\mathrm{C}}|)}\\
\times\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}}\frac{\vartheta_{j}^{-1/2}}{N^{1/3}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}},\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\frac{\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}}{N^{1/3}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}},\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing}\frac{1}{N^{2/3}}\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}.

(56)

We claim that by summing over
\vartheta_{j}
for each
j\in J
,
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
for each
j\in J\setminus J_{\mathrm{I}}
, each factor in the second line sums to order
1
.

Indeed, for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}
, we would sum
\vartheta_{j}^{-1/2}
for
\vartheta_{j}
from
1
and up to a number of order
N^{2/3}
, leading to a factor of order
N^{1/3}
.
For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}
, we would sum
\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}
for
\vartheta_{j}
from
1
and up to a number of order
N^{2/3}
, giving a sum of order
1
(note that here
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\vartheta_{j}
).
For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{V}}
there is always
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
, so nothing to sum.
For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}
, if
\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
, the number of possible
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
is of order
N^{2/3}
, thus we get a factor of order
N^{2/3}
.
If
\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing
, we first sum
\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}
for
\vartheta_{j}
from
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
up to a number of order
N^{2/3}
, and get a factor of order
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}^{-1/2}
; then we sum this for
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
from
1
and up to a number of order
N^{2/3}
, getting a factor of order
N^{1/3}
.

Finally we sum over all possibly ways of partitioning
J
into the various type sets
J_{\mathrm{I}},\ldots,
and
J_{\mathrm{A}},\ldots
: we simply bound
N^{-|J|-\frac{1}{3}(|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|+\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\}+|J_{\mathrm{C}}|)}\leq N^{-|J|}
, and note that the number of partitions is of constant order.
Thus the bound follows.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.22
.

Similarly to
Proposition

4.20
, we start from (
56
). The difference is that this time we should sum it only over the parameters with (1)
\min\{\vartheta_{j}:j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}\}\leq\epsilon N^{2/3}
or
\min\{\dot{\vartheta}_{j}:j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}\}\leq\epsilon N^{2/3}
, or (2)
J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}\cup J_{\mathrm{C}}
is not empty.

For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}
, the sum of
\frac{\vartheta_{j}^{-1/2}}{N^{1/3}}
for
\vartheta_{j}
from
1
up to
\epsilon N^{2/3}
is of order
\sqrt{\epsilon}
.
For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}
, the sum of
\frac{\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}}{N^{1/3}}
for
\vartheta_{j}>\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
, plus
\frac{1}{N^{2/3}}
(for
\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
) is bounded by
\frac{C\dot{\vartheta}_{j}^{-1/2}}{N^{1/3}}+\frac{1}{N^{2/3}}
; and a further sum of
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}
from
1
up to
\epsilon N^{2/3}
is of order
\sqrt{\epsilon}
.
Thus the second line of (
56
) sum up to order
\sqrt{\epsilon}
.

For the sum over those walks where
J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}\cup J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}\cup J_{\mathrm{C}}
is non-empty, the factor
N^{-\frac{1}{3}(|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|+\max\{|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|,|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|\}+|J_{\mathrm{C}}|)}
is at most
N^{-1/3}<\hat{C}^{-1/2}\sqrt{\epsilon}
, since we take
\epsilon>\hat{C}^{-1}N^{-2/3}
.

In summary, in either case we get an extra factor of order at most
\sqrt{\epsilon}
, so the conclusion follows.
∎

4.7
Elimination of pair types and the second regularization

Type III and type B indices are in pairs.
Our next regularization is to show that these two types can be ‘eliminated’, via a procedure similar to the cancellations between type I and type II.
The goal of this subsection is to write the action of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}
as a sum of
w(\vec{r})
, over
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}
without type III indices or type B indices.

Again we take
\epsilon
to be any number with
\hat{C}^{-1}N^{-2/3}<\epsilon<\hat{C}
, where
\hat{C}>0
is a universal constant. Any
C,c
below can depend on
\hat{C}
.

We let
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
denote the set of all walks
\vec{r}
of a particular operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{m}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}

(i.e., in the notations of
Section

4.3.2
for
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{B}[i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}]
, we set
i_{\ell}=\ell
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
)
, such that
J_{\mathrm{B}}=J_{\mathrm{C}}=\emptyset
,
J_{\mathrm{III}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}=\emptyset
, and
\vartheta_{\ell}\wedge\dot{\vartheta}_{\ell}>\epsilon N^{2/3}
for each
\ell\in J_{\mathrm{I}}\cup J_{\mathrm{II}}\cup J_{\mathrm{IV}}
.
Note that for such a walk, there is
J=\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, and
a_{\ell}=b_{\ell}=\ell
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.

Proposition 4.25
.

We have

\left|N^{m}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}w(\vec{r})-\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}\right|<C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}(\sqrt{\epsilon}+N^{-1/12}).

We note that here in the sum we have the restriction from
\epsilon
, i.e.,
\vartheta_{\ell}\wedge\dot{\vartheta}_{\ell}>\epsilon N^{2/3}
. When sending
N\to\infty
later in this section, this constraint is necessary to ensure absolute convergence, i.e.,
\limsup_{N\to\infty}N^{m}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{-k_{\ell}}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}|w(\vec{r})|<\infty
(see
Proposition

4.42
).

In the rest of this subsection we prove this proposition through a series of intermediate statements.

We shall consider walks of different operators.
Thus to be clear, we fix one operator
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}=(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
. All the above set up notations, such as
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}
, refer to those associated with it (unless otherwise noted).

We define a partial order
\prec
on
J
, such that for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}
and the corresponding
j^{\prime}\in J_{\mathrm{B}}
, we have
j^{\prime}\prec j
.
Under this partial order,
J
is a disjoint union of linearly-ordered chains.
By a ’minimal’ element of a partially ordered set, we refer to any element such that no other element is smaller than it. In particular, any singleton element (which is not comparable to any other element) is minimal.

Definition 4.26
.

For
\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}
, we construct a walk
\vec{r}
of another operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}^{\prime}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
without types III or B indices, as follows.
For each
j\in J
that is minimal under
\prec
, we let
r_{j}=\sum_{j^{\prime}:j\preceq j^{\prime}}s_{j^{\prime}}
, and
i_{\ell}^{\prime}=j
for each
\ell
with
j\preceq i_{\ell}
; and let
r_{j}=0
for each non-minimal
j
.
We use
\mathcal{M}
to denote the map
\vec{s}\mapsto\vec{r}
, and let
\mathbf{m}(\vec{s})
be the number of non-minimal
j\in J
.
Note that
\mathbf{m}(\vec{s})
equals the number of type III (or type B) indices of
\vec{s}
.

Note that
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{Q}
(from
Definition

4.19
) commute.

Lemma 4.27
.

We have

\left|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}}\left[w(\vec{r})-(-N)^{-\mathbf{m}(\vec{r})}w(\mathcal{M}(\vec{r}))\right]\right|<C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|J|-1/3}.

Proof.

Similarly to
Lemma

4.23
, for
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}
without type II indices, we have

\left|\sum_{\vec{s}:\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}\left[w(\vec{s})-(-N)^{-\mathbf{m}(\vec{r})}w(\mathcal{M}(\vec{s}))\right]\right|<C\left(N^{-1/3}+\exp(CN^{-1/3}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))N^{-2/3}\sum_{j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}}\vartheta_{j}\right)\\
\times\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}}\left(|U|N^{-1}+N^{-1}\vartheta_{j}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)\right)|w(\vec{r})|.

(57)

Here the factor with
\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}}
is by the same reasoning as in the proof
Lemma

4.23
.
The factor in the first line is from
(-N)^{-\mathbf{m}(\vec{r})}w(\mathcal{M}(\vec{s}))/w(\vec{s})-1
, which equals
(-N)^{-\mathbf{m}(\vec{r})}w(\mathcal{M}(\vec{r}))/w(\vec{r})-1
.
Namely, from (
37
),
w(\vec{r})
and
w(\mathcal{M}(\vec{r}))
differ at
\ell=a_{j}
for each
j
of type III, in the following ways:

•

in the first line of (
37
),
w(\mathcal{M}(\vec{r}))
has an extra factor of
-1
;

•

in the second line of (
37
),
w(\mathcal{M}(\vec{r}))
has an extra factor of
N_{a_{j}}
;

•

in the third line of (
37
),
r_{j}(t-1)=\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})_{j}(t-1)-\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})_{j}(Q_{a_{j}-1})
, for
t\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,Q_{a_{j}-1}+\vartheta_{j}\rrbracket
. (And
r_{j}(t-1)=\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})_{j}(t-1)
for any other
t
.)

These together imply that

|(-N)^{-\mathbf{m}(\vec{r})}w(\mathcal{M}(\vec{r}))/w(\vec{r})-1|<\left|\prod_{j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}}(1+CN^{-1}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))^{\vartheta_{j}}-1\right|+1-(N_{m}/N)^{\mathbf{m}(\vec{r})}.

This is further bounded by (using that
N^{-2/3}\sum_{j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}}\vartheta_{j}<C
)

CN^{-1/3}+\exp\left(CN^{-1}\sum_{j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}}\vartheta_{j}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)\right)-1<CN^{-1/3}+C\exp(CN^{-1/3}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))N^{-2/3}\sum_{j\in J_{\mathrm{III}}}\vartheta_{j}.

Thus we get (
57
). From there, the conclusion follows similarly to the proof of
Proposition

4.22
.
∎

We now take a operator
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}^{\prime}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
, and let
\mathscr{B}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})=\mathscr{B}[i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{m}^{\prime}]
denote the set of walks of it, and
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})=\mathscr{B}[i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{m}^{\prime}]
denote the set of those satisfying the conditions in
Definition

4.21
.
We assume that
\mathscr{B}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
contains
\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})
for some
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}
, and we use
\mathscr{O}=\mathscr{O}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}})
to denote the collection of all such operators
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
.

More precisely,
\mathscr{O}
contains all
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}^{\prime}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
, such that for each
j\in J
, either (1)
i_{\ell}^{\prime}=j
for each
\ell
with
i_{\ell}=j
, or (2) there is a
j^{\prime}\in J
, such that
i_{\ell}^{\prime}=j^{\prime}
for each
\ell
with
i_{\ell}=j
, and
\max\{\ell:i_{\ell}=j^{\prime}\}<\min\{\ell:i_{\ell}=j\}
.

Moreover, we let
J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
be the collection of all
j
that appears in
(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{m}^{\prime})
.
Then for
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}
with
\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})\in\mathscr{B}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
, we have
\mathbf{m}(\vec{r})=|J|-|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})|
.

Lemma 4.28
.

For
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}=(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}^{\prime}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}^{\prime}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}\in\mathscr{O}
, we have

\left|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon},\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})\in\mathscr{B}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})}w(\mathcal{M}(\vec{r}))-\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}),\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})\right|<C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})|-1/12}.

Proof.

We note that
\mathcal{M}
is injective, and that
\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
whenever
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}
.
However, there exist walks
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
with
\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}
, but which are not in the image of
\mathcal{M}
from
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}
; and the left-hand side is the sum of these walks.

We now describe these walks, by giving some necessary conditions any one of them must satisfy.
Any such walk
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
must not contain any type II index, and should satisfy one of the following (where
a_{j}
is in terms of
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
):

•

t\in\Delta
for some
t\in\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1},Q_{a_{j}-1}+N^{1/4}\rrbracket
and
j\in J\setminus J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
.

•

\mathscr{H}\geq\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell})
on
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1},Q_{a_{j}-1}+N^{1/4}\rrbracket
, for some
j\in J\setminus J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
.

This is because, if for each
j\in J\setminus J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
, none of these two hold, then one can construct a
\vec{r}^{*}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}
with
\mathcal{M}(\vec{r}_{*})=\vec{r}
.
(We note that here we take
N^{1/4}
to make the sum over
\vec{r}
satisfying either of the two conditions small, as will be evident at the end of this proof. )

More precisely, we let
r^{*}_{j}=r_{j}
for each
j\in U
; and for each
j\in J
, we take
r^{*}_{j}
as follows.
If
j\in J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
, let
r^{*}_{j}=r_{j}
on
\llbracket 0,Q_{b_{j}}\rrbracket
(where
b_{j}
is in terms of
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
).
If
j\not\in J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
, let
r^{*}_{j}=0
on
\llbracket 0,Q_{a_{j}-1}\rrbracket
,
r^{*}_{j}=r_{j^{\prime}}-r_{j^{\prime}}(Q_{a_{j}-1})
on
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1},t_{j}-1\rrbracket
, and
r^{*}_{j}=r_{j^{\prime}}
on
\llbracket t_{j},Q_{b_{j}}\rrbracket
. Here
j^{\prime}\in J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
is the index with
j^{\prime}=i^{\prime}_{a_{j}}
, and
t_{j}
is the smallest number in
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1},Q_{a_{j}}\rrbracket
with
\mathscr{H}(t_{j})<\mathscr{H}(Q_{a_{j}-1})
(which necessarily exists and is in
\llbracket Q_{a_{j}-1}+1,Q_{a_{j}-1}+N^{1/4}\rrbracket
, by the above conditions).
Now that we have defined
r^{*}_{j}
on
\llbracket 0,Q_{b_{j}}\rrbracket
for each
j\in J
, we let
r^{*}_{j}=0
on
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1},Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
for each
\ell\geq b_{j}+2
, and choose
r^{*}_{j}
on
\llbracket Q_{b_{j}},Q_{b_{j}+1}\rrbracket
(if
b_{j}<Q_{m}
) to ensure that the sum
\sum_{j\in J\cup U}r^{*}_{j}
remains the same as
\mathscr{H}
.

Therefore, the left-hand side in the statement is bounded by

\sum_{\vec{r}}\left|\sum_{\vec{s}:\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}w(\vec{s})\right|,

where the sum is over all
\vec{r}
satisfying at least one of the above two bulleted conditions.
Then the conclusion follows from bounding the sum using
Lemma

4.23
and
Corollary

4.24
with the additional conditions (i) and (ii), which are precisely
due to the two bulleted conditions above.
∎

We next take a (weighted) sum of
\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})}w(\vec{r})
over all
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{O}
.
From cancellations given in
Lemma

4.27
and
Lemma

4.28
, the main remaining terms would be
\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon},\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})
.

Lemma 4.29
.

We have

\left|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon},\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})-\sum_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{O}}N^{|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})|-|J|}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})}w(\vec{r})\right|<C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|J|-1/12}.

Proof.

For each
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{O}
, we let
\mathscr{O}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})\subset\mathscr{O}
be the collection of all operators
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}
, such that
\mathscr{B}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime})
contains
\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})
for some
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
.
Then by
Lemma

4.27
and
Lemma

4.28
we have

\left|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})}w(\vec{r})-\sum_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{O}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})}(-N)^{|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime})|-|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})|}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}),\mathcal{M}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}}w(\vec{r})\right|\\
<C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})|-1/12}.

(58)

Note that for each
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{O}
,
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}\neq(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
,
we have

\sum_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{O}:\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{O}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})}(-1)^{|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})|}=0.

This follows from Möbius inversion formula for partially ordered sets (see e.g.,
[
Rot64
,
KRY09
]
). If we make
\mathscr{O}
a partially ordered set, with a partial order
\prec_{\mathscr{O}}
given by
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}\prec_{\mathscr{O}}\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
for any
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime},\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{O}
with
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{O}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})
, then it is straight-forward to check that
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime},\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime})\mapsto(-1)^{|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})|-|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime\prime})|}
is the Möbius function.
This identity can also be understood as an inclusion-exclusion principle on the set of indices.

Then by multiplying
N^{|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime})|-|J|}
in both sides of (
58
) and summing over
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.25
.

As stated in
Lemma

4.9
, we expand
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}
as a sum of operators
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}=(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bar{i}_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bar{i}_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
, over all
(\bar{i}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}_{m})\in\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\llbracket 1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket
.
Then by
Proposition

4.20
and
N-CN^{2/3}<N_{m}\leq N_{1}\leq N
, we have that the sum of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bar{i}_{m}}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\bar{i}_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}
over those
(\bar{i}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}_{m})
with
\max\{\bar{i}_{\ell}:\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket\}>N_{m}
is bounded by
C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-1/3}
. Therefore it suffices to sum over
(\bar{i}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}_{m})\in\llbracket 1,N_{m}\rrbracket^{m}
.
By symmetry among the indices,
N-CN^{2/3}<N_{m}\leq N_{1}\leq N
, and further using
Proposition

4.20
, we have

\left|\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1}}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}-\sum_{\hat{\mathfrak{D}}}N^{|J(\hat{\mathfrak{D}})|}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}})}w(\vec{r})\right|<C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}N^{-1/3}.

From this,
Proposition

4.22
, and
Lemma

4.29
, the conclusion follows.
∎

4.8
Discrete blocks decomposition

The outcome of the previous six subsections is that the expressions of
Proposition

4.3
stay bounded as
N\to\infty
. The next step is to actually compute the limit and to identify it with
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
. This will be done in this and the next subsections: we will analyze
N^{m}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}w(\vec{r})
as
N\to\infty
and show that it converges towards the integral over
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
in
Definition

2.12
.

For this, in the present subsection we decompose a walk into ‘discrete blocks’ — which is a discrete analog of blocks defined in
Section

2
, and can be thought as the ‘skeleton’ of a walk — and a Bernoulli path.
Figure

11
gives an illustration and more precise definitions follow, culminating in
Proposition

4.38
, which shows that the discrete structures converge to their continuous counterparts as
N\to\infty
.

Again, we emphasize that we do not work with arbitrary walks in this subsection, but only those in
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
, which remain after all the regularization steps (i.e., cancellations and pair type eliminations) as being discussed in
Proposition

4.25
.
We recall the notation
Q_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}k_{\ell^{\prime}}
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
.

0
Q_{1}
Q_{2}
Q_{3}
r_{1}

0
Q_{1}
Q_{2}
Q_{3}
p_{2}
and
r_{2}

0
Q_{1}

and
r_{3}

0

Q_{2}+\upsilon_{3}
0
Q_{1}
