p^{0}=p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}+p_{4}
\mathcal{H}
\vec{\upsilon}
\mathscr{H}

Figure 11
:
An illustration of discrete blocks
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
, with
m=3
,
U=\{9\}
, and the path
\mathscr{H}
. These together encode the same information as
\vec{r}
. Discrete blocks are discrete analogs of blocks (from
Section

2
, and see
Figure

1
).

Definition 4.30
.

A
discrete block process
of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{m}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
is a family of functions
\vec{p}=\{p_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m+u}
, where
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and each
p_{j}:\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is a function satisfying the following:

•

for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
p_{j}(Q_{0})=0
, and
p_{\ell}=0
on
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{m}\rrbracket
;

•

for each
\ell\in\llbracket m+1,m+u\rrbracket
,
p_{j}(Q_{0})=p_{j}(Q_{m})=0
, and
p_{j}
is not identical zero;

•

for each
t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
, there is at most one
j
such that
p_{j}(t)\neq p_{j}(t-1)
; and we must have
j\in\llbracket\ell+1,N_{\ell}\rrbracket
;

•

\min\{t\in\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket:p_{j}(t)>0\}<\min\{t\in\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket:p_{j^{\prime}}(t)>0\}
for all
m+1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq m+u
.

We also denote
p^{\ell}=\sum_{j=\ell+1}^{m+u}p_{j}
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
.

Note that (compared to the continuous counterpart defined in
Definition

2.2
) we do not require the support of each
p_{j}
to be continuous. This is for the convenience of definitions in the discrete case, and does not affect the limiting objects: as
N\to\infty
the total mass of the blocks with discontinuous support tends to
0
.

Definition 4.31
.

For a discrete block process
\vec{p}
, a compatible
discrete virtual block process
is a vector
\vec{\upsilon}=\{\upsilon_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}\in\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(\llbracket 1,Q_{\ell}-Q_{\ell-1}\rrbracket\cup\{\varnothing\})
satisfying the following conditions: for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,

•

if
\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing
, then
p^{\ell}
is constant on
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1},Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell}\rrbracket
;

•

if
p_{\ell}(Q_{\ell-1})=0
, then
\upsilon_{\ell}=\varnothing
.

Definition 4.32
.

For a discrete block process
\vec{p}
and compatible discrete virtual block process
\vec{\upsilon}
, a compatible
discrete block height
is a function

\mathcal{H}:\{Q_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m}\cup\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\left(\bigcup_{t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket,p^{\ell}(t)\neq p^{\ell}(t-1)}\{t-1,t\}\right)\cup\bigcup_{\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing}\{Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell}-1,Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell}\}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

such that

•

\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell})=p^{0}(Q_{\ell})
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
\mathcal{H}(0)=0
;

•

\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell}-1)=p^{0}(Q_{\ell-1})
and
\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell})=p^{0}(Q_{\ell-1})-1
for each
\ell
with
\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing
17
17

17

It might happen that
p^{\ell}(Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell})\neq p^{\ell}(Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell}+1)
or
Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell}=Q_{\ell}
, so that there are more than one restrictions at
\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell})
, given by the other conditions in this definition.
;

•

for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
with
p_{j}(t)\neq p_{j}(t-1)
for some
j>\ell
, we have
\mathcal{H}(t-1)=\mathcal{H}(t)+1
,
\mathcal{H}(t)\geq p^{\ell}(t)
,
\mathcal{H}(t-1)\geq p^{\ell}(t-1)
;

•

if
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
for some
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
with
j>\ell
, such that
p_{j}(t)\neq p_{j}(t-1)
, then the jump
p_{j}(t)-p_{j}(t-1)
and the difference
\mathcal{H}(t)+1/2-p^{\ell}(t-1)-p_{j}(t)
have the same sign;

•

if
\upsilon_{\ell}=\varnothing
,
and
t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1},Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
is the maximum number with
p^{\ell}
being constant on
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1},t\rrbracket
, then
\mathcal{H}(t)\geq\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1})
.

In particular, if
p_{j}(t)=0
, we must have
\mathcal{H}(t)=p^{\ell}(t-1)-1
.

We also note that for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
p^{\ell}(t)=p^{0}(t)
for each
t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
,
while
p^{\ell}(Q_{\ell-1})=p^{0}(Q_{\ell-1})-p_{\ell}(Q_{\ell-1})
.
Therefore
p^{\ell}
in the third and fourth point in
Definition

4.32
is actually in correspondence with
\mathbf{p}^{0}
in (the third and fourth point in)
Definition

2.5
.

Definition 4.33
.

“Discrete blocks” is a triplet
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
: discrete block process, discrete virtual block process, and compatible discrete block height. It is
valid in parity
, if for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
Q_{\ell}+p^{\ell}(Q_{\ell})
is an even number, and
\upsilon_{\ell}
is an odd number unless
\upsilon_{\ell}=\varnothing
; and for each
t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
with
p^{\ell}(t)\neq p^{\ell}(t-1)
,
t+\mathcal{H}(t)
is an even number.

Connection to walks.

Below we take
\epsilon
to be any number with
N^{-1/10}<\epsilon<\hat{C}
, for some universal
\hat{C}>0
(and any
C,c
can depend on
\hat{C}
)
18
18

18

We note that starting from here the lower bound on
\epsilon
is taken to be larger than in the previous subsection (see e.g.
Definition

4.21
), where
\epsilon
is allowed to be of order
N^{-2/3}
. This is to simplify some computations later in this section.
.
Recall that
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
denotes the set of all walks
\vec{r}
of the particular operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{m}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
, satisfying certain constraints, as specified above
Proposition

4.25
.

For each walk
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
, we can construct discrete blocks
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
which are valid in parity, as follows.
For each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, we let
p_{\ell}=r_{\ell}
on
\llbracket 0,Q_{\ell-1}\rrbracket
, and
p_{\ell}=0
on
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{m}\rrbracket
.
We let
\upsilon_{\ell}=\min\{t\in\llbracket 1,Q_{\ell}-Q_{\ell-1}\rrbracket:\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1}+t)<\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1}),\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell-1}+t\rrbracket\cap\Delta=\emptyset\}
, and let
\upsilon_{\ell}=\varnothing
if the set is empty.

We let
u=|U|
(recall from
Section

4.4
that
U
is the set of
j\in\llbracket m+1,N\rrbracket
where
r_{j}
is not identical zero).
We define
p_{j}=r_{\sigma(j)}
for each
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+u\rrbracket
, where
\sigma:\llbracket m+1,m+u\rrbracket\to U
is a bijection, and is the unique one so that the last bullet point in
Definition

4.30
is satisfied.

We then let
\mathcal{H}=\mathscr{H}
wherever it should be defined.

Definition 4.34
.

We let
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}
denote the collection of all discrete blocks
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
, that are valid in parity and such that for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
p^{\ell}
is constant on
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1},Q_{\ell-1}+\epsilon N^{2/3}\rrbracket
and either
\upsilon_{\ell}>\epsilon N^{2/3}
, or
\upsilon_{\ell}=\varnothing
.

Lemma 4.35
.

The construction above defines a correspondence
\mathcal{L}:\vec{r}\mapsto(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
, which maps
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
to
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

Recall the definition of
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
from
Section

4.7
:

•

the requirement
J_{\mathrm{B}}=J_{\mathrm{C}}=\emptyset
(thereby
J_{\mathrm{III}}=\emptyset
) corresponds to
p_{\ell}=r_{\ell}=0
on the segment
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{m}\rrbracket
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
;

•

as for the requirement that
\vartheta_{\ell}\wedge\dot{\vartheta}_{\ell}>\epsilon N^{2/3}
for
\ell\in J_{\mathrm{I}}\cup J_{\mathrm{II}}\cup J_{\mathrm{IV}}
,
it is equivalent to that each
\dot{\vartheta}_{\ell}>\epsilon N^{2/3}
or
\dot{\vartheta}_{\ell}=\varnothing
, and
\vartheta_{\ell}>\epsilon N^{2/3}
or
\vartheta_{\ell}=\varnothing
;
the
\dot{\vartheta}_{\ell}
part corresponds to that
p^{\ell}
is constant on
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1},Q_{\ell-1}+\epsilon N^{2/3}\rrbracket
;
the
\vartheta_{\ell}
part corresponds to
\upsilon_{\ell}>\epsilon N^{2/3}
or
\upsilon_{\ell}=\varnothing
.

All the other properties of discrete blocks process
\vec{p}
and discrete block height
\mathcal{H}
are from properties of walks, in
Definition

4.7
, and the construction of
p_{j}
for
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+u\rrbracket
, which ensures that
p_{j}
is not identical zero, and the symmetry breaking in
Definition

4.30
.
All the other properties of discrete virtual block process
\vec{\upsilon}
are ensured by its construction above.
∎

The definition of
\mathcal{L}
also implies the following description of its inverse
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
.

Lemma 4.36
.

For
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})\in\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}
, the pre-image
\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
either is empty (due to the requirement of
J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}=\emptyset
for
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
), or consists of all the
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
obtained by specifying
\mathscr{H}
to be any function on
\llbracket 0,Q_{m}\rrbracket
, satisfying

•

\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell})=p^{0}(Q_{\ell})
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, and in particular
\mathscr{H}(0)=\mathscr{H}(Q_{m})=0
;

•

|\mathscr{H}(t)-\mathscr{H}(t-1)|=1
and
\mathscr{H}(t)\geq p^{0}(t)
for each
t\in\llbracket 1,Q_{m}\rrbracket
;

•

\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1=\mathcal{H}(t)
for each
t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
,
p^{\ell}(t)\neq p^{\ell}(t-1)
, or
t=Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell}
when
\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing
;

and specifying the set
U\subset\llbracket m+1,N\rrbracket
with
|U|=u
, as well as the bijection
\sigma:\llbracket m+1,m+u\rrbracket\to U
.

This follows by checking the definition of walks and
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
, and of blocks, as in the proof of
Lemma

4.35
. We omit the details.

We note that for any
\vec{r}\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
, the sets
\Delta
,
\Delta_{j,\ell}
, and the numbers
\delta
and
\delta_{j,\ell}
(for each
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
) are the same; therefore below we regard them as functions of
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
.

We next estimate the sum
\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})}w(\vec{r})
for any
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})\in\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}
.
For this, we recall the blocks defined in
Section

2
.

Closeness between blocks and discrete blocks.

We use the following notion for the correspondence between blocks and discrete blocks.

Definition 4.37
.

Take
D>0
.
For any discrete blocks
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{m}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}}
which are valid in parity, and any blocks
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
(with times
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
, and
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{k}_{\ell^{\prime}}
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
),
they are
D
-close
, or simply
close
if the following conditions hold.

•

u=\mathbf{u}
.

•

For each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, we have

|k_{\ell}-\mathbf{k}_{\ell}N^{2/3}|<D
.

•

For each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, either
\upsilon_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}=\varnothing
, or

|\upsilon_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}N^{2/3}|<D
with
\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing
.

•

For each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket\ell,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
, we have
\delta_{j,\ell}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}
.
Besides, if we denote the numbers in
\Delta_{j,\ell}
and
\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}
by
t_{1}<\cdots<t_{\delta_{j,\ell}}
and
x_{1}<\cdots<x_{\delta_{j,\ell}}
respectively, we have for each
i\in\llbracket 1,\delta_{j,\ell}\rrbracket
,

|t_{i}-N^{2/3}x_{i}|,\quad|p^{0}(t_{i})-N^{1/3}\mathbf{p}^{0}(x_{i})|,\quad|\mathcal{H}(t_{i})-N^{1/3}\mathbf{H}(x_{i})|<D,

and

(\mathbf{p}^{0}(x_{i})-\mathbf{p}^{0}({x_{i}}-))(p^{0}(t_{i})-p^{0}(t_{i}-1))>0,

and
p_{j}=0
on
\llbracket Q_{\ell},Q_{m}\rrbracket
if and only if
\mathbf{p}_{j}=0
on
[\mathbf{Q}_{\ell},\mathbf{Q}_{m}]
.

We note that when
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
and
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
are close, necessarily
\delta=\boldsymbol{\delta}
.

For the sum
\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})}w(\vec{r})
, we shall approximate it using blocks.

Recall the setup in
Definitions

2.9
and
2.11
.
The continuous counterpart of the sum
\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})}w(\vec{r})
is
\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]
, and we will explicitly bound the difference between them (with appropriate scaling), using
Lemma

4.40
.
We next define the notations in the error terms.
We let

\mathbf{F}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]=2^{-\boldsymbol{\delta}}\prod_{(x,y)\in\Xi}\mathbf{F}[x,y],

where

\mathbf{F}[x,y]=\begin{cases}\mathbf{F}_{0,0}(y-x),&(x,y)\in\Xi_{1},\\
\mathbf{F}_{0}(y-x;,\mathbf{H}(y)-\mathbf{H}(x)),&(x,y)\in\Xi_{2},\\
(-1)^{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{p}^{0}(x)<\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)]}\mathbf{F}_{0}(y-x;\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{H}(y)),&(x,y)\in\Xi_{3},\\
(-1)^{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{p}^{0}(x)<\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)]}\mathbf{F}(y-x;\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x),\mathbf{H}(y)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(y-)),&(x,y)\in\Xi_{4}.\end{cases}

And we further denote

\mathbf{Err}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]=\sum_{(x,y)\in\Xi}\mathbf{Err}[x,y],

where

\mathbf{Err}[x,y]=\begin{cases}N^{-1/40},&(x,y)\in\Xi_{1},\\
1\wedge\big(N^{-1/40}+N^{-1/3}/(\mathbf{H}(y)-\mathbf{H}(x))\big),&(x,y)\in\Xi_{2},\\
\!\begin{aligned} 1\wedge&\big(N^{-3/5}(y-x)^{-3}+N^{-1/40}+N^{-1/3}/(\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{H}(y))\big),\end{aligned}&(x,y)\in\Xi_{3},\\
\!\begin{aligned} 1\wedge&\big(N^{-3/5}(y-x)^{-3}+N^{-1/40}+N^{-1/3}/(\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x))\\
&+N^{-1/3}/(\mathbf{H}(y)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(y-))\big),\end{aligned}&(x,y)\in\Xi_{4}.\end{cases}

Finally, for
d>0
, we say that
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
is
d
-
well-spaced
, or simply
well-spaced
, if

•

y-x>dN^{-2/3}
for each
(x,y)\in\Xi
,

•

\mathbf{H}(y)-\mathbf{H}(x)>dN^{-1/3}
for each
(x,y)\in\Xi_{2}
,

•

\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{H}(y)>dN^{-1/3}
for each
(x,y)\in\Xi_{3}
,

•

\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x),\mathbf{H}(y)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(y-)>dN^{-1/3}
for each
(x,y)\in\Xi_{4}
.

The notion of being well-spaced is introduced for the asymptotic approximations: for such blocks
\mathbf{I}(x;h,g)
,
\mathbf{I}_{0}(x;h)
, and
\mathbf{I}_{0,0}(x)
are well-approximated by their discrete counterparts, as we later establish in
Lemma

4.40
.

We next state an asymptotic approximation for
\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})}w(\vec{r})
.

Proposition 4.38
.

Take any constants
C_{1},D,d>0
.
Take any limiting blocks
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
that is
d
-well-spaced, with
\boldsymbol{\delta},\mathbf{p}^{0},\mathbf{H}<C_{1}
.
Take any
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})\in\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}
, and assume that
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
and
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
are
D
-close, and that
\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
is non-empty.
Also take
\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}<C_{1}
, and
|N_{\ell}-N+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}N^{2/3}|<C_{1}
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.
Then there exists
C_{2}=C_{2}(C_{1},D,d)
such that

\Bigg|2^{-m-\boldsymbol{\delta}-|\{\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}|}N^{m-\frac{2}{3}\mathbf{u}+\frac{4}{3}\boldsymbol{\delta}+\frac{2}{3}|\{\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}|}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})}w(\vec{r})\\
-\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]\Bigg|<C_{2}\mathbf{F}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]\mathbf{Err}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}].

For the proof, we introduce discrete analogs of the functions
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{I}_{0}
,
\mathbf{I}_{0,0}
in
Definition

2.9
.

For
X,H,G\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
X+H+G
even, we let
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
be the collection of all
F:\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, satisfying
|F(t)-F(t-1)|=1
for each
t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket
, and
F(0)=H
,
F(X)=G
.
When
H\leq G
, we denote
\mathscr{F}^{+}(X;H,G)
to be the subset of
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
, consisting of
F
with
F\geq H
.

Definition 4.39
.

We denote

I(X;H,G)=2^{-X-1}\sum_{F\in\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
F(t)=F(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2F(t)}{\beta N}\right).

When
H\leq G
, we also denote
I^{+}(X;H,G)
to be the same, except for that the summation is over
F\in\mathscr{F}^{+}(X;H,G)
.

Proof of
Proposition

4.38
.

In this proof
C,c>0
may depend on
C_{1},D,d
, and we take
C_{2}
to be large enough depending on all
C,c
.

For the discrete block
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
, and each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, let
\Upsilon_{\ell}
be the set consisting of all maximal intervals
\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket\subset\llbracket Q_{\ell-1},Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
, such that
p^{\ell}
is constant on
\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket
, and the interval contains at most one of
Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell}
and
Q_{\ell-1}+\upsilon_{\ell}-1
.
Then there is an exact correspondence between discrete intervals in
\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\Upsilon_{\ell}
(constructed by
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
), and continuous intervals
\Xi
constructed by
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
.
This in particular implies that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}|\Upsilon_{\ell}|=|\Xi|=\delta+m+|\{\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}|
.

For each
\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket\in\Upsilon_{\ell}
, if
X=Q_{\ell-1}
, we let

\mathscr{F}[X,Y]=\mathscr{F}^{+}(Y-X;\mathcal{H}(X)-p^{\ell}(X),\mathcal{H}(Y)-p^{\ell}(X)),

and

I[X,Y]=I^{+}(Y-X;\mathcal{H}(X)-p^{\ell}(X),\mathcal{H}(Y)-p^{\ell}(X));

otherwise, we let

\mathscr{F}[X,Y]=\mathscr{F}(Y-X;\mathcal{H}(X)-p^{\ell}(X),\mathcal{H}(Y)-p^{\ell}(X)),

and

I[X,Y]=(-1)^{\mathds{1}[p^{\ell}(X)<p^{\ell}(X-1)]}I(Y-X;\mathcal{H}(X)-p^{\ell}(X),\mathcal{H}(Y)-p^{\ell}(X)).

Using the expression from
Definition

4.8
,
for each
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
with
\mathcal{L}(\vec{r})=(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
, we have

\Bigg|\frac{2^{-m}N^{\delta}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}}|w(\vec{r})|}{2^{-Q_{m}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}N_{\ell}^{(\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket\\
\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)}{\beta N_{\ell}}\right)}-1\Bigg|<CN^{-1/3}.

(59)

Indeed, plugging (
37
) into the ratio in the left-hand side, we would get

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\Biggl[(N_{\ell}/N)^{-\bigl|\{t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket:\,\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1,\,r_{i_{\ell}}(t)\neq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)-1\}\bigr|}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\\
\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)}{\beta N_{\ell}}-\frac{|\{j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:r_{j}(t-1)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\}|}{N_{\ell}}\right)\left(1+\frac{2r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)}{\beta N}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket\setminus\Delta\\
\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)}{\beta N_{\ell}}-\frac{|\{j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:r_{j}(t-1)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\}|}{N_{\ell}}\right)\Biggr].

Using that
\boldsymbol{\delta}=\delta<C_{1}
, each of the three lines above is between
1-CN^{-1/3}
and
1+CN^{-1/3}
, thus we get (
59
).

On the other hand, from the definition of
I[X,Y]
and
Lemma

4.36
, we have

\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}:\mathcal{L}(\vec{r})=(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})}2^{-Q_{m}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(-1)^{\bigl|\{t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket:\,\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1,\,r_{i_{\ell}}(t)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\}\bigr|}\\
\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket:\\
\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)}{\beta N}\right)=P[\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H}]\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\prod_{\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket\in\Upsilon_{\ell}}I[X,Y],

(60)

where
P[\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H}]
is the number of possible
U
and
\sigma
for
\vec{r}
with
\mathcal{L}(\vec{r})=(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
, and is between
N^{u}(1-CN^{-1/3})
and
N^{u}
.
Therefore from (
60
) and (
59
), we have that

\Bigg|2^{-m}N^{\delta}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}:\mathcal{L}(\vec{r})=(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})}w(\vec{r})-P[\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H}]\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}N_{\ell}^{(\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2}\prod_{\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket\in\Upsilon_{\ell}}I[X,Y]\Bigg|\\
<CN^{-1/3}2^{-Q_{m}}|\{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}:\mathcal{L}(\vec{r})=(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})\}|.

(61)

For the right-hand side, it equals

CN^{-1/3}2^{-Q_{m}}P[\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H}]\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\prod_{\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket\in\Upsilon_{\ell}}|\mathscr{F}[X,Y]|<CN^{-1/3}\cdot N^{u-|\Xi_{1}|-\frac{2}{3}(|\Xi_{2}|+|\Xi_{3}|)-\frac{1}{3}|\Xi_{4}|}\mathbf{F}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]\\
=CN^{-1/3}\cdot N^{-m+\frac{2}{3}\mathbf{u}-\frac{1}{3}\boldsymbol{\delta}-\frac{2}{3}|\{\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}|}\mathbf{F}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}].

Here the inequality is due to replacing each
|\mathscr{F}[X,Y]|
with
\mathbf{F}[x,y]
, for
(x,y)\in\Xi
being the interval in correspondence with
\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket
, using Lemmas
A.5
,
A.6
, and
A.7
.
The last equality is due to
u=\mathbf{u}
, and

|\Xi_{1}|+\frac{2}{3}(|\Xi_{2}|+|\Xi_{3}|)+\frac{1}{3}|\Xi_{4}|=m+\frac{1}{3}\mathbf{u}+\frac{1}{3}\boldsymbol{\delta}+\frac{2}{3}|\{\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}|,

(62)

which follows from
m=|\Xi_{1}|+|\Xi_{2}|
,
m+\mathbf{u}+|\{\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}|=|\Xi_{1}|+|\Xi_{3}|
, and
\boldsymbol{\delta}+m+|\{\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket:\upsilon_{\ell}\neq\varnothing\}|=|\Xi|
.

As for the left-hand side of (
61
),
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,

|\log(N_{\ell}/N)N^{1/3}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}|<CN^{-1/3},\quad|N^{-1/3}(\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell}))-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1})-\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}))|<CN^{-1/3},

using that
|N_{\ell}-N+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}N^{2/3}|<C_{1}
, and the fact
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
and
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
are
D
-close.
Thus

|\log(N_{\ell}/N)(\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1})-\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}))/2|<CN^{-1/3},

Then by summing over
\ell
and taking the exponential,
and using that
1-CN^{-1/3}<P[\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H}]N^{-u}\leq 1
, we get

\left|P[\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H}]N^{-u}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}N_{\ell}^{(\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2}-\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\right|<CN^{-1/3}.

(63)

And for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket\in\Upsilon_{\ell}
, there is a corresponding
(x,y)\in\Xi
, such that
|X-N^{2/3}x|,|Y-N^{2/3}y|<C
.
Moreover, we have
|\mathcal{H}(X)-p^{\ell}(X)-N^{1/3}(\mathbf{H}(x)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(x))|<C
. And if
(x,y)\in\Xi_{1}\cup\Xi_{3}
, we have
\mathcal{H}(Y)=p^{\ell}(Y)
; and for other
(x,y)
we have
|\mathcal{H}(Y)-p^{\ell}(Y)-N^{1/3}(\mathbf{H}(y)-\mathbf{p}^{0}(y-))|<C
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

4.40
below (where the conditions of
x>C_{1}N^{-2/3}
, and
h,g>C_{1}N^{-1/3}
or
h>C_{1}N^{-1/3}
or
g-h>C_{1}N^{-1/3}
, are satisfied by the well-spaced assumption) and
\epsilon>N^{-1/10}
,
we have

\displaystyle|NI[X,Y]-\mathbf{I}_{\beta}[x,y]|<C\mathbf{Err}[x,y]\mathbf{F}[x,y],

\displaystyle\quad(x,y)\in\Xi_{1},

\displaystyle|N^{2/3}I[X,Y]-\mathbf{I}_{\beta}[x,y]|<C\mathbf{Err}[x,y]\mathbf{F}[x,y],

\displaystyle\quad(x,y)\in\Xi_{2}\cup\Xi_{3},

\displaystyle|N^{1/3}I[X,Y]-\mathbf{I}_{\beta}[x,y]|<C\mathbf{Err}[x,y]\mathbf{F}[x,y],

\displaystyle\quad(x,y)\in\Xi_{4}.

The conclusion follows by combining the above estimates with (
9
) and (
61
), and using (
62
) again.
∎

Lemma 4.40
.

For any
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, there is a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{1},C_{2})>0
, such that for any
C_{1}N^{-2/3}<x<C_{2}
and
0\leq h,g<C_{2}
, and
X,H,G\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, such that
|X-xN^{2/3}|,|H-hN^{1/3}|,|G-gN^{1/3}|<C_{2}
, the following estimates hold:

(1)

When
X+H+G
is even and
h,g>C_{1}N^{-1/3}
, we have

\left|N^{1/3}I(X;H,G)-\mathbf{I}(x;h,g)\right|\\
<C_{3}\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)\left(1\wedge(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/40}+N^{-1/3}/h+N^{-1/3}/g)\right).

(2)

When
X+H
is even and
h>C_{1}N^{-1/3}
, we have

\left|N^{2/3}I(X;H,0)-\mathbf{I}_{0}(x;h)\right|<C_{3}\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)\left(1\wedge(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/40}+N^{-1/3}/g)\right).

(3)

When
X+H+G
is even,
H\leq G
, and
g-h>C_{1}N^{-1/3}
, we have

\left|N^{2/3}I^{+}(X;H,G)-\exp(-\beta^{-1}xh)\mathbf{I}_{0}(x;g-h)\right|\\
<C\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;g-h)\left(1\wedge(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/40}+N^{-1/3}/g)\right).

(4)

When
X
is even, we have

\left|NI^{+}(X;H,H)-\exp(-\beta^{-1}xh)\mathbf{I}_{0,0}(x)\right|<C_{3}\mathbf{F}_{0,0}(x)\left(1\wedge(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/40})\right).

The proof of this lemma is by coupling Bernoulli random walks with Brownian bridges. We postpone the details of the proof until
Section

A.3
.

Remark 4.41
.

We note that if
C_{2}>C_{1}
, then the required lower bounds on
x
,
h
,
g
,
g-h
, do not lead to any lower bounds on
X
,
H
,
G
,
G-H
. This is essential when
Lemma

4.40
is applied in the proof of
Proposition

4.38
, and then implicitly used in the next subsection (when upper-bounding (
69
) there).

4.9
Summation over discrete blocks and proof of Proposition
4.3

In the previous subsection we computed the asymptotics of the sum of the walks corresponding to a fixed discrete blocks structure
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
. In this subsection we sum over all discrete blocks in
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}
, thus arriving at the sum over all walks in
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
. We will show that the sum approximates an integral over the space of all blocks
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
as
N\to\infty
. As in the previous subsection, we shall use notations of blocks and their weights defined in
Section

2
.

Due to parity issues in counting discrete walks (which reflects in the necessity to add two terms of different parities in the product in Proposition
4.3
), we work under the following setup.
Take any
\vec{\mathfrak{b}}=\{\mathfrak{b}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
and let
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}[\vec{\mathfrak{b}}]
and
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathfrak{b}}]
(respectively) denote the corresponding set of walks and blocks of the operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{m}^{N_{m}})^{k_{m}+\mathfrak{b}_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}^{N_{1}})^{k_{1}+\mathfrak{b}_{1}}
.

We take
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
and denote
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{k}_{\ell^{\prime}}
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, and take
\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m}
.
The main results of this subsection are the following two statements, including justifying
Definition

2.12
, plus
Proposition

4.3
.

Proposition 4.42
.

Take
C_{1}>0
, and assume that
|k_{\ell}-N^{2/3}\mathbf{k}_{\ell}|<C_{1}
,
|N_{\ell}-N+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}N^{2/3}|<C_{1}
for each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.
Then

\lim_{N\to\infty}2^{-m}N^{m}\sum_{\vec{\mathfrak{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}-\mathfrak{b}_{\ell}}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}w(\vec{r})\\
=\lim_{C_{2}\to\infty}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]:\\
\mathbf{p}^{0},\mathbf{H},\boldsymbol{\delta}<C_{2}\end{subarray}}\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]\mathrm{d}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}),

(64)

and

\lim_{N\to\infty}2^{-m}N^{m}\sum_{\vec{\mathfrak{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}-\mathfrak{b}_{\ell}}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}|w(\vec{r})|\\
=\lim_{C_{2}\to\infty}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]:\\
\mathbf{p}^{0},\mathbf{H},\boldsymbol{\delta}<C_{2}\end{subarray}}\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)|\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]|\mathrm{d}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}),

(65)

and all the limits above exist and are finite.

In particular, the existence and finiteness of the limit in the right-hand side of (
65
) implies that
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\mapsto\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]
is absolutely integrable in
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
.

The next statement is on sending
\epsilon\to 0+
.

Proposition 4.43
.

\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
from
Definition

2.12
is well-defined, i.e.,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}\int_{(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]}\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]\mathrm{d}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})

(66)

exists and is finite.

Below we prove these two statements and
Proposition

4.3
. The key step is the following pre-limiting estimate.

Proposition 4.44
.

Take
C_{1},C_{2}>0
.
Assume that
|k_{\ell}-N^{2/3}\mathbf{k}_{\ell}|<C_{1}
and
|N_{\ell}-N+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}N^{2/3}|<C_{1}
for each
N
and
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.
Then there exists
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{1},C_{2})
such that

\Bigg|2^{-m}N^{m}\sum_{\vec{\mathfrak{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}-\mathfrak{b}_{\ell}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})\in\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathfrak{b}}]:\\
p^{0},\mathcal{H}<C_{2}N^{1/3},\delta<C_{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})}w(\vec{r})\\
-\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]:\\
\mathbf{p}^{0},\mathbf{H},\boldsymbol{\delta}<C_{2}\end{subarray}}\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]\mathrm{d}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\Bigg|<C_{3}\epsilon^{-m/2}N^{-1/40}.

(67)

The same bound holds in absolute values, i.e., if we simultaneously replace
w(r)
with
|w(r)|
and
\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]
with
|\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]|
.

Proof.

In this proof
C,c>0
may depend on
C_{1},C_{2}
, and we take
C_{3}
to be large enough depending on all
C,c
.

The general idea is to apply
Proposition

4.38
to blocks and discrete blocks that are close (in the sense of
Definition

4.37
), and sum over discrete blocks and integrate over blocks simultaneously.
To implement this, we need the following ‘correspondence relation’, which is more precise than ‘closeness’ in
Definition

4.37
.

Step 1: correspondence.

We say that any
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}=\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
and
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})\in\bigcup_{\vec{\mathfrak{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathfrak{b}}]
are
in correspondence
, if the following holds:

•

u=\mathbf{u}
.

•

For each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, either
\upsilon_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}=\varnothing
, or
\upsilon_{\ell}-\lfloor N^{2/3}\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\rfloor\in\{-2,-1\}
.

•

For each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket\ell,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
,
we have
\delta_{j,\ell}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{j,\ell}
.
Besides, if we denote the numbers in the sets
\Delta_{j,\ell}
and
\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}
by
t_{1}<\cdots<t_{\delta_{j,\ell}}
and
x_{1}<\cdots<x_{\delta_{j,\ell}}
respectively, we have for each
i\in\llbracket 1,\delta_{j,\ell}\rrbracket
,

t_{i}-\lfloor N^{2/3}x_{i}\rfloor\in\{0,1\},\quad p^{0}(t_{i})=\lfloor N^{1/3}\mathbf{p}^{0}(x_{i})\rfloor,\quad\mathcal{H}(t_{i})-\lfloor N^{1/3}\mathbf{H}(x_{i})\rfloor\in\{0,-1\}.

We note that for
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}
,
t_{i}
, and
\mathcal{H}(t_{i})
above, it seems that each is allowed to take one of two values. However, each of them can actually just take one of the two possible values, as the parity for each is already determined.

We next count the number of discrete blocks that are in correspondence to one blocks structure, and consider the measure of all blocks that are in correspondence to one discrete blocks.

Blocks to discrete blocks.

We let
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}^{*}=\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon,N}^{*}
be the subset of
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}
, consisting of all
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
such that

•

Any two (finite) numbers in
\mathbf{\Delta}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m}\cup\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1}+\boldsymbol{\upsilon}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
are at least
10N^{-2/3}
away.

•

For each
j\in\llbracket 1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
, the numbers
\mathbf{p}_{j}(x)
for
x\in\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}
are mutually different, and any two are at least
10N^{-1/3}
away.
In addition, when
j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, any one of these numbers is
\geq 10N^{-1/3}
.

•

For each
\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
j\in\llbracket 1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
, and
x\in\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}
, unless
\mathbf{p}_{j}=0
on
[x,\mathbf{Q}_{m}]
(i.e.,
j\in\llbracket m+1,m+\mathbf{u}\rrbracket
and
x=\max\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}_{j,\ell}
), we must have
\mathbf{H}(x)\geq\max\{\mathbf{p}^{0}(x),\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)\}+10N^{-1/3}
, and
|\mathbf{p}_{j}(x)-\mathbf{H}(x)+\mathbf{p}^{0}(x-)|\geq 10N^{-1/3}
.

Then any
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}^{*}
is necessarily

) by

C\sum_{a_{\ell}\in\llbracket 0,C_{2}\rrbracket,\ell\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,b_{\ell}\in\{0,1\}}\int\Bigg(N^{-1/40}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{a_{\ell}}1\wedge\left(N^{-3/5}x_{\ell,i}^{-3}+N^{-1/3}x_{\ell,i}^{-1/2}\right)\Bigg)\\
\times\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\prod_{i=1}^{b_{\ell}}y_{\ell,i}^{-3/2}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\prod_{i=1}^{a_{\ell}}x_{\ell,i}^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}(\vec{x},\vec{y}),

(76)

where
a_{\ell}
,
b_{\ell}
correspond to the numbers of intervals in
\Xi_{2}\cup\Xi_{3}\cup\Xi_{4}
contained in
(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
, and the numbers of intervals in
\Xi_{1}
contained in
(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell-1},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
, respectively. The integral in (
76
) is over all
\vec{x}=\{\{x_{\ell,i}\}_{i=1}^{a_{\ell}}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
and
\vec{y}=\{\{y_{\ell,i}\}_{i=1}^{b_{\ell}}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
, satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{a_{\ell}}x_{\ell,i}+\sum_{i=1}^{b_{\ell}}y_{\ell,i}=\mathbf{k}_{\ell}
, and each
x_{\ell,i}\geq 0
, and
y_{\ell,i}>\epsilon
.
Here the lower bound of
y_{\ell,i}
is due to that, for
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}
and
(x,y)\in\Xi_{1}
, necessarily
y-x>\epsilon
(which follows from the definition of
\Xi_{1}
in
Section

2.3
, and the definition of
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}
in
Definition

2.12
).

In (
76
), we upper bound each
x_{\ell,i}^{-1}
or
y_{\ell,i}^{-1}
by
C
, whenever
x_{\ell,i}<\mathbf{k}_{\ell}/C_{2}
or
y_{\ell,i}<\mathbf{k}_{\ell}/C_{2}
(which holds for at least one
x_{\ell,i}
or
y_{\ell,i}
for each
\ell
).
Then for the remaining
x_{\ell,i}
and
y_{\ell,i}
, we integrate each of them in
[0,\mathbf{k}_{\ell}/C_{2}]
.
The integrals over
y_{\ell,i}
would give a factor that is at most
C\epsilon^{-m/2}
.
For the integrals over
x_{\ell,i}
, we use that
\int_{0}^{\mathbf{Q}_{m}}N^{-1/40}x^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}x<CN^{-1/40}
, and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\mathbf{Q}_{m}}\left(1\wedge\big(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/3}x^{-1/2}\big)\right)x^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{N^{-1/5}}x^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}x+\int_{N^{-1/5}}^{\mathbf{Q}_{m}}\big(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/3}x^{-1/2}\big)x^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle CN^{-1/10}+CN^{-3/5}\cdot N^{1/2}+CN^{-1/3}\cdot\log(N)<CN^{-1/40}.

Step 6: bounding (
72
).

As for (
72
), here we integrate
\mathbf{F}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]
, over all
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}
, but either
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\not\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}^{*}
or
\max\mathbf{p}^{0}\geq C_{2}-10N^{-1/3}
, or
\max\mathbf{H}\geq C_{2}-10N^{-1/3}
.
Again we first integrate over
(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})
with fixed
\Xi_{1}
,
\Xi_{2}
,
\Xi_{3}
,
\Xi_{4}
.
We would get (
75
), except for that, for one
(x,y)\in\Xi_{2}\cup\Xi_{3}\cup\Xi_{4}
we would replace the domain of integration by an interval of length
<CN^{-1/3}
;
and then we would sum over all choices of this
(x,y)
.
Therefore (
72
) is bounded by

C\int\Bigg(\sum_{(x,y)\in\Xi_{2}\cup\Xi_{3}\cup\Xi_{4}}1\wedge(N^{-1/3}(y-x)^{-1/2})\Bigg)\\
\times\prod_{(x,y)\in\Xi_{1}}(y-x)^{-3/2}\prod_{(x,y)\in\Xi_{2}\cup\Xi_{3}}(y-x)^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}(\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2},\Xi_{3},\Xi_{4})<C\epsilon^{-m/2}N^{-1/40},

where we also use that, for any
x>0
and interval
I\subset(0,\infty)
of length
N^{-1/3}
, we have
\int_{I}\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)\mathrm{d}h<C(1\wedge N^{-1/3}x^{-1/2})x^{-1/2}
, and
\int_{I}\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)\mathrm{d}g<C(1\wedge N^{-1/3}x^{-1/2})
for any fixed
h
.
The inequality is derived similarly as in bounding (
73
). Therefore (
67
) follows.

Step 7: absolute values.

Finally, in terms of absolute values, we note that for any blocks
(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
, the weight
w(\vec{r})
for each
\vec{r}\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})
have the same sign. Therefore
Proposition

4.38
still holds if we replace
w(r)
with
|w(r)|
and
\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]
with
|\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]|
.
Then we can apply
Proposition

4.38
in the same way as above and deduce (
67
) in absolute values.
∎

Given the estimates established so far, we can now deduce the asymptotic results.

Proof of
Proposition

4.42
.

Note that
\epsilon>0
is fixed throughout this proof.
From
Proposition

4.44
we have for any
C_{2}>0
,

\Bigg|\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]:\\
\mathbf{p}^{0},\mathbf{H},\boldsymbol{\delta}<C_{2}\end{subarray}}\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]\mathrm{d}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\\
-2^{-m}N^{m}\sum_{\vec{\mathfrak{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}-\mathfrak{b}_{\ell}}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}w(\vec{r})\Bigg|<C_{3}\epsilon^{-m/2}N^{-1/40}\\
+2^{-m}N^{m}\sum_{\vec{\mathfrak{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}-\mathfrak{b}_{\ell}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})\in\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathfrak{b}}]:\\
\max\{N^{-1/3}p^{0},N^{-1/3}\mathcal{H},\delta\}\geq C_{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H})}|w(\vec{r})|,

(77)

for
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{1},C_{2})
. Let
A_{1}(C_{2})
denote the integral in the first line,
A_{2}(N)
denote the sum in the second line, and
A_{3}(C_{2},N)
denote the third line.
By sending
N\to\infty
with fixed
C_{2}
, we have

\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{2}(N)-\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{3}(C_{2},N)\leq A_{1}(C_{2})\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}A_{2}(N)+\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{3}(C_{2},N).

It now remains to show that
\lim_{C_{2}\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{3}(C_{2},N)=0
.
Since then,
\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{2}(N)<\infty
, and by sending
C_{2}\to\infty
we have

\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{2}(N)\leq\liminf_{C_{2}\to\infty}A_{1}(C_{2})\leq\limsup_{C_{2}\to\infty}A_{1}(C_{2})\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}A_{2}(N),

which implies that both
\lim_{N\to\infty}A_{2}(N)
and
\lim_{C_{2}\to\infty}A_{1}(C_{2})
exist and are finite, and the limits are equal, i.e., (
64
) holds.

For (
65
), by (
67
) in absolute values, (
77
) still holds if
w(\vec{r})
by
|w(\vec{r})|
in the first line and
\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]
by
|\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]|
in the second line, and the same argument as deriving (
64
) applies.

The rest of this proof is devoted to proving
\lim_{C_{2}\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{3}(C_{2},N)=0
.
We note that all
C,c
below are independent of
C_{2}
.

We apply
Corollary

4.18
to all
\vec{r}\in\bigcup_{\vec{\mathfrak{b}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}[\vec{\mathfrak{b}}]
, such that
\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)\geq C_{2}N^{1/3}
or
\delta\geq C_{2}-m
.
More precisely, from the upper bound in
Corollary

4.18
, we first multiply
N^{m}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}-\mathfrak{b}_{\ell}}
, sum over
U
(with fixed
|U|
), and lower bound
|J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}|
,
|J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}|
,
|J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}|
,
|J_{\mathrm{C}}|
by zero.
Note that
|J|=m
, and recall (from above
Proposition

4.25
)
\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}
consists of walks
\vec{r}
with
J_{\mathrm{B}}=J_{\mathrm{C}}=\emptyset
,
J_{\mathrm{III}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.1}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.2}}=J_{\mathrm{VI.3}}=\emptyset
, and
\vartheta_{\ell}\wedge\dot{\vartheta}_{\ell}>\epsilon N^{2/3}
for each
\ell\in J_{\mathrm{I}}\cup J_{\mathrm{II}}\cup J_{\mathrm{IV}}
.
Also note that the summation over
U
gives a factor of at most
{N\choose|U|}\leq\frac{N^{|U|}}{|U|!}
, so we get

N^{\frac{1}{3}|J_{\mathrm{I}}|+\frac{1}{3}|J_{\mathrm{II}}|-\frac{1}{3}\bigl(|J_{\mathrm{IV}}|+|\{j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}:\,\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing\}|\bigr)}C_{5}^{\delta+1}\frac{h^{\delta-2|U|}}{(\delta-2|U|)!}\exp(-C_{4}h^{2})\\
\times\frac{1}{|U|!}\big((|U|+1)\log(|U|+2)\big)^{|U|}\big(h/(|U|+1)\big)^{C_{4}|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}\prod_{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2},

where
C_{4},C_{5}
are constants depending only on
C_{1}
, and all
C,c
are allowed to depend on
C_{4},C_{5}
.
We next sum the product of
\prod_{j\in J:\vartheta_{j}\neq\varnothing}\vartheta_{j}^{-3/2}
, over
\vartheta_{j}\geq\epsilon N^{2/3}
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}
,
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}=\vartheta_{j}\geq\epsilon N^{2/3}
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{II}}
, and
\vartheta_{j}>\dot{\vartheta}_{j}\geq\epsilon N^{2/3}
or
\vartheta_{j}=\varnothing
,
\dot{\vartheta}_{j}\geq\epsilon N^{2/3}
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}
.
We get a factor of
C\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}N^{-\frac{1}{3}}
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{I}}\cup J_{\mathrm{II}}
, a factor of
N^{\frac{2}{3}}
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}
with
\varepsilon_{j}=\emptyset
, and a factor of
N^{\frac{1}{3}}
for each
j\in J_{\mathrm{IV}}
with
\varepsilon_{j}\neq\emptyset
.
Thus, the sum is at most

CC_{5}^{\delta+1}\frac{h^{\delta-2|U|}}{(\delta-2|U|)!}\exp(-C_{4}h^{2})\frac{1}{|U|!}\big((|U|+1)\log(|U|+2)\big)^{|U|}\big(h/(|U|+1)\big)^{C_{4}|U|+m\mathbf{1}_{h\geq|U|}}.

By further using that
\frac{(|U|+1)^{|U|}}{|U|!}<C\exp(|U|)
, denoting
a=\delta-2|U|
,
b=|U|
, and taking dyadic
h=2^{i}
, we have

A_{3}(C_{2},N)<C\epsilon^{-m/2}\sum_{a,b,i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:\max\{a,b,2^{i}\}>C_{2}/4}\frac{(C_{6}2^{i})^{a}}{a!}\exp(-C_{4}2^{2i})(C_{6}\log(b+2))^{b}(2^{i}/(b+1))^{C_{4}b},

where
C_{6}
is a constant depending only on
C_{1}
, and all
C,c
are allowed to depend on
C_{6}
.

In the rest of this proof we show that the above sum is at most
C\exp(-cC_{2})
, from which the conclusion follows.
We can assume that
C_{2}
is large enough (depending on
C_{4},C_{6}
), since otherwise the bound immediately follows, as the sum over all
a,b,i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is finite.

For any
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have
\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{(C_{6}2^{i})^{a}}{a!}=\exp(C_{6}2^{i})
, and

\sum_{b=0}^{\infty}(C_{6}\log(b+2))^{b}(2^{i}/(b+1))^{C_{4}b}\\
=\sum_{b=0}^{\lceil(i+1)^{2/C_{4}}\rceil 2^{i}-1}(C_{6}\log(b+2))^{b}(2^{i}/(b+1))^{C_{4}b}+\sum_{b=\lceil(i+1)^{2/C_{4}}\rceil 2^{i}}^{\infty}(C_{6}\log(b+2))^{b}(2^{i}/(b+1))^{C_{4}b}\\
<\lceil(i+1)^{2/C_{4}}\rceil 2^{i}\cdot(C_{6}\log((i+1)^{2/C_{4}+1}2^{i}))^{(i+1)^{2/C_{4}+1}2^{i}}\cdot 2^{C_{4}(i+1)^{2/C_{4}+2}2^{i}}+C<C\exp(Ci^{2/C_{4}+2}2^{i}).

Thus,

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:2^{2i}>C_{2}/4}\exp(-C_{4}2^{2i})\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{(C_{6}2^{i})^{a}}{a!}\sum_{b=0}^{\infty}(C_{6}\log(b+2))^{b}(2^{i}/(b+1))^{C_{4}b}\\
<\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:2^{2i}>C_{2}/4}C\exp(-C_{4}2^{2i}+C_{6}2^{i}+Ci^{2/C_{4}+2}2^{i})<C\exp(-cC_{2}).

(78)

When
2^{2i}\leq C_{2}/4
, we have
\sum_{a=\lceil C_{2}/4\rceil}^{\infty}\frac{(C_{6}2^{i})^{a}}{a!}<\frac{(C_{6}2^{i})^{\lceil C_{2}/4\rceil}}{\lceil C_{2}/4\rceil!}\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{(C_{6}2^{i})^{a}}{a!}<\frac{(CC_{2})^{C_{2}/8}}{\lceil C_{2}/4\rceil!}\exp(C_{6}2^{i})
, and

\sum_{b=\lceil C_{2}/4\rceil}^{\infty}(C_{6}\log(b+2))^{b}(2^{i}/(b+1))^{C_{4}b}<\sum_{b=\lceil C_{2}/4\rceil}^{\infty}(C_{6}\log(C_{2}))^{b}C_{2}^{-C_{4}b/2}<CC_{2}^{-cC_{2}}
.
Thus,

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:2^{2i}\leq C_{2}/4}\exp(-C_{4}2^{2i})\sum_{a=\lceil C_{2}/4\rceil}^{\infty}\frac{(C_{6}2^{i})^{a}}{a!}\sum_{b=0}^{\infty}(C_{6}\log(b+2))^{b}(2^{i}/(b+1))^{C_{4}b}\\
<\frac{(CC_{2})^{C_{2}/8}}{\lceil C_{2}/4\rceil!}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:2^{2i}\leq C_{2}/4}\exp(-C_{4}2^{2i}+C_{6}2^{i}+Ci^{2/C_{4}+2}2^{i})<C\exp(-cC_{2})

(79)

and

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:2^{2i}\leq C_{2}/4}\exp(-C_{4}2^{2i})\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{(C_{6}2^{i})^{a}}{a!}\sum_{b=\lceil C_{2}/4\rceil}^{\infty}(C_{6}\log(b+2))^{b}(2^{i}/(b+1))^{C_{4}b}\\
<CC_{2}^{-cC_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:2^{2i}\leq C_{2}/4}\exp(-C_{4}2^{2i}+C_{6}2^{i})<C\exp(-cC_{2}).

(80)

By adding up (
78
), (
79
), and (
80
), the proof concludes.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.43
and
Proposition

4.3
.

Denote

A_{4}(N)=2^{-m}\left(\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N_{m},2N/\beta}}{(2\sqrt{N_{m}N})^{k_{m}}}+\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}+1}^{N_{m},2N/\beta}}{(2\sqrt{N_{m}N})^{k_{m}+1}}\right)\cdots\left(\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N_{1},2N/\beta}}{(2\sqrt{N_{1}N})^{k_{1}}}+\frac{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}+1}^{N_{1},2N/\beta}}{(2\sqrt{N_{1}N})^{k_{1}+1}}\right)[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0},

A_{5}(\epsilon)=\int_{(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H})\in\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]}\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\mathbf{I}_{\beta,\vec{\mathbf{k}}}[\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}]\mathrm{d}(\vec{\mathbf{p}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\mathbf{H}).

We need to show that
\lim_{N\to\infty}A_{4}(N)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}A_{5}(\epsilon)
, and both limits exist and are finite.

From
Proposition

4.25
and (
64
), we have that
\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{4}(N)<A_{5}(\epsilon)+C\sqrt{\epsilon}<\infty
and
\liminf_{N\to\infty}A_{4}(N)>A_{5}(\epsilon)-C\sqrt{\epsilon}
.
Then by sending
\epsilon\to 0+
we have

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0+}A_{5}(\epsilon)\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}A_{4}(N)\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{4}(N)\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0+}A_{5}(\epsilon),

so the conclusion follows.
∎

4.10
Dyson Brownian Motion: proof of Proposition
4.4

The proof of
Proposition

4.4
is almost the same as the proof of
Proposition

4.3
and in this section we highlight the necessary modifications.

Instead of Theorem
3.2
, our starting point is now Theorem
3.4
. Arguing similarly to Section
4.3
, we expand the operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{m}}^{N,\tau_{m}})^{k_{m}}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{k_{1}}
in terms of walks. The difference is that now all
N_{1},\dots,N_{m}
are the same and equal to
N
, but
\tau
(which was fixed in Section
4.3
) is now varying. This leads to an adjustment in the weight of the walk, which we introduced in Definition
4.8
and transformed in (
37
). In the proof of Proposition
4.4
, the weight becomes

\begin{split}w(\vec{r})=&N^{-\delta}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(-1)^{|\{t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket:\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1,r_{i_{\ell}}(t)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)\}|}\\
&\times(\beta\tau_{\ell}N/2)^{k_{\ell}/2}(\beta\tau_{\ell}/2)^{(\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell})-\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1}))/2}\\
&\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket\setminus\Delta\\
\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)}{\beta N}-\frac{|\{j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:r_{j}(t-1)\geq r_{i_{\ell}}(t-1)>0\}|}{N}\right).\end{split}

(81)

Compared to (
37
), we replace each
N_{\ell}
in the third line, and in
N_{\ell}^{-\bigl|\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\bigr|}
in the second line, by
N
.
This is due to that, from the definition of the operators (
30
), in 2, 3(a), 3(b) of
Definition

4.5
,
j
is allowed to take any number in
\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
in the DBM setting.
Then the product
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}N^{-\bigl|\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\bigr|}
gives
N^{-\delta}
.
On the other hand, for
N_{\ell}
in
(\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}
and
N_{\ell}^{(\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2}
in the second line of (
37
), we replace each by
\beta\tau_{\ell}/2
, and get
(\beta\tau_{\ell}N/2)^{k_{\ell}/2}
and
(\beta\tau_{\ell}/2)^{(\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell})-\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1}))/2}
.
This is because these factors come from counting the number of down-steps versus the number of up-steps: in
Definition

4.5
each
-
or
\top
step in
\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket
corresponds to a factor of
N_{\ell}
, while in the DBM setting each
+
step gives a factor of
\beta\tau_{\ell}/2
.

The classification of walks developed in
Section

4.4
is still valid. The bound of Proposition
4.15
in Section
4.5
can be deduced verbatim, except for that the factor
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}
in the right-hand side of (
38
) is replaced by
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\beta\tau_{\ell}N)^{k_{\ell}/2}
.
In particular, for the factor
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}N_{\ell}^{(\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2}
, essentially it is upper bounded by
\exp(Ch)
, and the same holds for
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(\beta\tau_{\ell}/2)^{(\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell})-\mathscr{H}(Q_{\ell-1}))/2}
.
Also,
N^{-\delta}
is of the same order (up to a factor between
1
and
1+C\delta N^{-1/3}
) as
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}N_{\ell}^{-\bigl|\llbracket Q_{\ell-1}+1,Q_{\ell}\rrbracket\cap\Delta\bigr|}
. Thus these factors are bounded the same way as their counterparts in the proof of
Proposition

4.15
.

The bounds of Propositions
4.20
and
4.22
of Section
4.6
follow in the same way, with the same replacement of
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{k_{\ell}}
by
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\beta\tau_{\ell}N)^{k_{\ell}/2}
in the right-hand sides. From there, in parallel to Section
4.7
, we develop an analogue of Proposition
4.25
, which is a bound:

\left|N^{m}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}w(\vec{r})-\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{m}}^{N,\tau_{m}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k_{1}}^{N,\tau_{1}}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}\right|<C\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\beta\tau_{\ell}N)^{k_{\ell}/2}(\sqrt{\epsilon}+N^{-1/12}).

Further, in parallel with Sections
4.8
and
4.9
, we analyze
N^{m}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{\epsilon}^{*}}w(\vec{r})
as
N\to\infty
and show that it converges towards the integral over
\mathscr{K}_{\epsilon}[\vec{\mathbf{k}}]
in
Definition

2.12
. The same block decomposition as in
Section

4.8
applies and an analogue of the estimate
Proposition

4.38
holds, with
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{NN_{\ell}})^{-k_{\ell}}
replaced by
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\beta\tau_{\ell}N)^{-k_{\ell}/2}
;
and in its proof,
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}N_{\ell}^{(\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell}))/2}
would be replaced by
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(\beta\tau_{\ell}/2)^{(\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1}))/2}
.
In particular, (
63
) would be replaced by

\left|P[\vec{p},\vec{\upsilon},\mathcal{H}]N^{-u}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(\beta\tau_{\ell}/2)^{(\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell})-\mathcal{H}(Q_{\ell-1}))/2}-\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell+1})\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})/2\right)\right|<CN^{-1/3},

which can be derived using
|\log(\beta\tau_{\ell}/(2N))N^{1/3}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}|<CN^{-1/3}
(instead of
|\log(N_{\ell}/N)N^{1/3}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}|<CN^{-1/3}
), and the other estimates in obtaining (
63
).

Finally, everything in
Section

4.9
(
Proposition

4.44
and its proof, and the final proof of
Proposition

4.3
) goes through verbatim, with each appearance of
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\sqrt{N_{\ell}N})^{-k_{\ell}-\mathfrak{b}_{\ell}}
replaced by
\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}(2\beta\tau_{\ell}N)^{-(k_{\ell}+\mathfrak{b}_{\ell})/2}
.

5
Random process convergence

In this section we finish the proofs of Theorems
1.4
,
1.5
, and
1.6
. Our task is to upgrade the convergence of joint moments in Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
to distributional convergence in the space of continuous curves equipped with uniform topology (on compact intervals of time). The two main ingredients are:

•

A uniform bound of Section
5.1
on the moments of the time increments for the G
\beta
E corners process, the Dyson Brownian Motion, and the Airy
β
line ensemble. This is used both for proving tightness as
N\to\infty
in the space of continuous curves, and for the existence of continuous modification for the limiting Airy
β
line ensemble. The proof relies on an algebraic identity for Dunkl operators, which can be of independent interest.

•

An indirect argument of Section
5.3
deducing the distributional convergence from the convergence of the moments, taking into account that the moments problem for the individual random variables
\exp(\mathbf{k}\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})/2)
of Theorem
1.4
is not uniquely determined (the moments grow too fast as a function of
m
).

We introduce the notations for the quantities in Theorems
1.5
and
1.6
. Let
\{y_{i}^{n}\}_{0\leq i\leq n}
be the (infinite rank) G
\beta
E corners process with variance
1
from
Definition

1.3
.
For any
\boldsymbol{\tau}<N^{1/3}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,N-\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3}\rrbracket
, denote

\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})=\mathbf{y}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})=N^{2/3}\left(\frac{y_{i}^{\lfloor N-\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3}\rfloor}}{\sqrt{2\beta\lfloor N-\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3}\rfloor}}-1\right).

Let
\{Y_{i}(\tau)\}_{1\leq i\leq N,\tau>0}
be the
N
dimensional DBM started from zero initial condition from
Definition

1.1
.
For any
\boldsymbol{\tau}>-N^{1/3}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, denote

\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})=\mathbf{Y}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})=N^{2/3}\left(\frac{Y_{i}(2N/\beta+2N^{2/3}\boldsymbol{\tau}/\beta)}{2N\sqrt{1+\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{-1/3}}}-1\right).

5.1
Uniform bound on time increments

In order to upgrade
Theorem

4.1
and
Theorem

4.2
to the statements about convergence of continuous functions, we need to have control over time increments. In the spirit of the classical Kolmogorov Continuity Theorem, for this it would be sufficient to prove the following two estimates on the fourth moments.

Proposition 5.1
.

For any
C_{1}>0
, there exists
C=C(C_{1})>0
such that the following holds.
For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
N>C_{1}^{3}
,
0\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}<\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}\leq C_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\geq N^{-2/3}
,
C_{1}^{-1}N^{2/3}<k<C_{1}N^{2/3}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor N-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}N^{2/3}\rfloor}\left(1+N^{-2/3}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})/2\right)^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor N-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}N^{2/3}\rfloor}\left(1+N^{-2/3}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})/2\right)^{k}\right)^{4}\right]\\
<C(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}+C(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})N^{-1/3}.

Proposition 5.2
.

For any
C_{1}>0
, there exists
C=C(C_{1})>0
such that the following holds.
For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}<\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}\leq C_{1}
,
C_{1}^{-1}N^{2/3}<k<C_{1}N^{2/3}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(1+N^{-2/3}\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})/2\right)^{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(1+N^{-2/3}\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})/2\right)^{k}\right)^{4}\right]<C(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}.

The proofs of the above estimates are obtained by expressing the moments in terms of Dunkl operators (as in Theorems
3.2
and
3.4
), and bounding them using estimates in
Section

4
. In order to get the right-hand sides of the inequalities proportional to
(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}
, rather than
|\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}|
, we rely on an interesting purely algebraic identity for Dunkl operators, which we state next.

Recall the operators

\mathfrak{D}_{i}^{N}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,\\
j\neq i\end{smallmatrix}}\frac{1-\sigma_{ij}}{x_{i}-x_{j}},\quad\quad\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N,\tau}=\mathfrak{D}_{i}^{N}+\tau x_{i},\quad\quad\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N,\tau})^{k},

acting on polynomials of
x_{1},x_{2},\cdots
.
We further denote

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}=\sum_{w=0}^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N,\tau})^{w}x_{i}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N,\tau})^{k-1-w},

which can be thought of as the
\tau
derivative of
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}
.
For any two operators
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, we denote the commutator
[\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}]=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}-\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathcal{P}
.
Then we can also write
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}=[\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau},\mathcal{S}]
, where
\mathcal{S}
is the operator of multiplication by the function
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{x_{i}^{2}}{2}
.

Proposition 5.3
.

For any
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\geq 2
, and
\tau>0
, we have
[[\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau},\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}],\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}]=0
.

Proof.

In this proof we fix
N,\tau
.
We write
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}=\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N,\tau}
,
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}
, for each
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The identity to prove can be written as
[[[\mathcal{S},\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}],\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}],\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}]=0
.

Recall that Dukl operators commute, as stated in
Lemma

3.1
. Hence, using
[\mathfrak{D}_{i},x_{j}]=\frac{\beta}{2}\sigma_{ij}
, valid for
i\neq j
, we conclude that
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}
also commute. Therefore, by linearity and product rule
[A,BC]=[A,B]C+B[A,C]
, the expression
[[[\mathcal{S},\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}],\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}],\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}]
can be written as a linear combination of the terms of the kind
A[[[x_{i_{1}}^{2},\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{2}}],\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{3}}],\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{4}}]B
for various operators
A
,
B
(which are products of operators
\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}
) and various indices
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket^{4}
. Hence, it remains to show that
[[[x_{i_{1}}^{2},\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{2}}],\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{3}}],\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{4}}]=0
for each
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket^{4}
.

It is straightforward to check that, for each
i,j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
,
i\neq j
,

[\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},x_{j}]=\frac{\beta}{2}\sigma_{ij},\quad[\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},x_{j}^{2}]=\frac{\beta}{2}(x_{i}+x_{j})\sigma_{ij};

and for each
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
,

[\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},x_{i}]=1+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:j\neq i}\sigma_{ij},\quad[\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i},x_{i}^{2}]=2x_{i}+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:j\neq i}(x_{i}+x_{j})\sigma_{ij};

and for each
i,j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
,
i\neq j
, we have
[\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i^{\prime}},\sigma_{ij}]=0
for each
i^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
.
Using these we do get the desired identity: just note that
[x_{i_{1}}^{2},\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{2}}]
is a linear combination of
x_{i}\sigma_{i,j}
, then
[[x_{i_{1}}^{2},\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{2}}],\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{3}}]
is a linear combination of
\sigma_{i,j}
, and then
[[[x_{i_{1}}^{2},\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{2}}],\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{3}}],\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{4}}]=0
.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

For any
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\geq 2
, and
\tau>0
, we have

-\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau})^{3}+3\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau})^{2}-3(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau})^{2}\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}+(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau})^{3}\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}=0.

Proof.

The above identity can be written as
[[[\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau},\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}],\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}],\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}]=0
, which follows from
Proposition

5.3
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.2
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}
we denote
\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(1+N^{-2/3}\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})/2\right)^{k}
. Our goal is to bound
\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}))^{4}]
.
We will use (within this proof)
C
to denote a constant that is large enough (depending on
C_{1}
in the statement), and its value may change from line to line.

We also denote
\tau_{\iota}=2N/\beta+2N^{2/3}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\iota}/\beta
, for
\iota=1,2
;
then we have
1+N^{-2/3}\mathbf{Y}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\iota})/2=\frac{Y_{i}(\tau_{\iota})}{\sqrt{2\beta N\tau_{\iota}}}
.
We next show that

\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{3}-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{4}\right]-(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})\frac{\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}-k(2\tau_{1})^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}\right)(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{3}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}}{(2\beta N\tau_{1})^{2k}}\right|\\
<C(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}.

(82)

By
Theorem

3.4
, and as explained below (
31
), we can write the expectation as

\frac{\left((\tau_{1}/\tau_{2})^{k/2}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{2}}-\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}\right)(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{3}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}}{(2\beta N\tau_{1})^{2k}}.

Thus, it suffices to prove

\left|\left((\tau_{1}/\tau_{2})^{k/2}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{2}}-\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}-(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}-k(2\tau_{1})^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}\right)\right)(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{3}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}\right|\\
<CN^{-4/3}(2\beta N\tau_{1})^{2k}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})^{2}.

(83)

This can be viewed as a Taylor expansion of
(\tau_{1}/\tau_{2})^{k/2}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{2}}
in
\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}
: by taking the
\tau_{2}
derivative of
(\tau_{1}/\tau_{2})^{k/2}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{2}}
at
\tau_{2}=\tau_{1}
, we get
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}-k(2\tau_{1})^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}
.

By
Lemma

4.9
, we have

(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{4}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}=\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket^{4}}\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]}w(\vec{r}),

where
\mathscr{B}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]
is the set of all walks (of
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{4}}^{N})^{k}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N})^{k}
) from
Definition

4.7
, and
w(\vec{r})
is defined via (
81
) for the operator
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{4}}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{k}\cdots(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{k}
.
Similarly, (for the same
\mathscr{B}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]
and
w(\vec{r})
) we have

\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{2}}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{3}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket^{4}\\
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]\end{subarray}}(\tau_{2}/\tau_{1})^{|\{t\in\llbracket 3k+1,4k\rrbracket:\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)+1\}|}w(\vec{r}),

and

\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N,\tau})^{w}x_{i}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i}^{N,\tau})^{k-1-w}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{3}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}\\
=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket^{4}\\
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]\end{subarray}}\tau_{1}^{-1}\mathds{1}[\mathscr{H}(4k-w)=\mathscr{H}(4k-w-1)+1]w(\vec{r}),

for each
w\in\llbracket 0,k-1\rrbracket
. By summing over
w
we have

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{3}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket^{4}\\
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]\end{subarray}}\tau_{1}^{-1}|\{t\in\llbracket 3k+1,4k\rrbracket:\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)+1\}|w(\vec{r}).

Thus, the left-hand side of (
83
) can be written as

\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket^{4}\\
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]\end{subarray}}\left((\tau_{1}/\tau_{2})^{\mathscr{H}(3k)/2}-1+(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})(2\tau_{1})^{-1}\mathscr{H}(3k)\right)w(\vec{r})\right|,

using that
\mathscr{H}(3k)=\mathscr{H}(3k)-\mathscr{H}(4k)=k-2|\{t\in\llbracket 3k+1,4k\rrbracket:\mathscr{H}(t)=\mathscr{H}(t-1)+1\}|
.

We then handle blow-up terms via cancellations, as done in
Section

4.6
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the map in
Definition

4.19
. For any
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]
satisfying
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}
(i.e., without type II indices), we have

\left|(\tau_{1}/\tau_{2})^{\mathscr{H}(3k)/2}-1+(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})(2\tau_{1})^{-1}\mathscr{H}(3k)\right|<CN^{-2}(\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))^{2}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})^{2}.

Use this,
Lemma

4.23
, and
Corollary

4.24
, and sum over all
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket^{4}
and
\vec{r}
without type II indices, we get the bound
CN^{-4/3}(2\beta N\tau_{1})^{2k}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})^{2}
.
Therefore (
82
) follows.

We can similarly estimate

•

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{2}\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{3}\right]
,

•

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{3}\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{2}\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}\right]
,

•

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{4}-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{3}\right]

through the actions of the operators

•

\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}-k(2\tau_{1})^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}\right)(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{2}
,

•

(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{2}\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}-k(2\tau_{1})^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}\right)\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}
,

•

(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}})^{3}\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}-k(2\tau_{1})^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau_{1}}\right)
,

respectively.
Then since

\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}))^{4}]=-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{3}-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{4}\right]+3\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{2}\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{3}\right]\\
-3\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{3}\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{2}\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{4}-\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})\mathbf{X}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{3}\right],

via a linear combination of these estimates, and
Corollary

5.4
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.1
.

We follow the same strategy as the previous proof. However, estimates in this proof are more technical. The main reason is that in the setting of DBM, we need to consider
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N,\tau}
with different
\tau
, and the set of walks remain the same; while for corners we need to consider different
N
, which lead to different sets of walks for different terms in a version of (
83
).

Again we use
C
to denote a constant that is large enough (depending on
C_{1}
in the statement), whose value may change from line to line.
For any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}
we denote
\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(1+N^{-2/3}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})/2\right)^{k}
, and we want to show
\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})-\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}))^{4}]<C(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}+C(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})N^{-1/3}
.

Write
N_{\iota}=\lfloor N-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\iota}N^{2/3}\rfloor
for
\iota=1,2
; then we have
1+N^{-2/3}\mathbf{y}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\iota})/2=\frac{y_{i}^{N_{\iota}}}{\sqrt{2\beta N_{\iota}}}
.
Now we shall prove an analogue of (
82
):

\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{3}-\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{4}\right]-\frac{2(N_{1}-N_{2})}{\beta}\cdot\frac{\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}-k\beta(4N)^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}\right)(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}})^{3}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}}{(4NN_{1})^{2k}}\right|\\
<C(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}+C(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})N^{-1/3},

(84)

where we let
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{\iota}}=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{\iota},2N/\beta}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{\iota}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{\iota},2N/\beta}
for
N_{\iota}=1,2
.

By
Theorem

3.2
, and as explained below (
31
), the expectation equals (Note that
\{y_{i}^{n}\}_{0\leq i\leq n}
is the G
\beta
E corners process with variance
1
, while we used instead
2N/\beta
throughout Section
4
.)

\frac{\left(N_{1}^{k/2}N_{2}^{-k/2}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{2}}-\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}\right)(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}})^{3}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}}{(4NN_{1})^{2k}},

therefore (
84
) is implied by an analogue of (
83
) and our assumption
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\geq N^{-2/3}
:

\left|\left(2N_{1}^{k/2}N_{2}^{-k/2}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{2}}-2\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}-(N_{1}-N_{2})\left(4\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}/\beta-kN^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}\right)\right)(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}})^{3}[1]_{x_{1}=\dots=x_{N}=0}\right|\\
<C(4NN_{1})^{2k}\left(N^{-4/3}(N_{1}-N_{2})^{2}+N^{-1}(N_{1}-N_{2})\right).

(85)

For
\iota=1,2
, we let
\mathscr{B}_{\iota}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]
be the set of all walks from
Definition

4.7
, and
w_{\iota}(\vec{r})
be from
Definition

4.8
or (
37
), for the operators
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{4}}^{N_{1}})^{k}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{3}}^{N_{1}})^{k}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{2}}^{N_{1}})^{k}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k}
and
(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{4}}^{N_{2}})^{k}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{3}}^{N_{1}})^{k}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{2}}^{N_{1}})^{k}(\hat{\mathfrak{D}}_{i_{1}}^{N_{1}})^{k}
, respectively.
Besides, we further denote
\mathscr{B}_{+}=\bigcup_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N_{1}\rrbracket^{4}}\mathscr{B}_{1}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]
, and
\mathscr{B}_{-}=\bigcup_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\llbracket 1,N_{1}\rrbracket^{3}\times\llbracket 1,N_{2}\rrbracket}\mathscr{B}_{2}[i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}]
.
We also let
\mathscr{B}_{*}
denote the collection of all
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}
, such that
i_{4}
is of type II, and
i_{4}\neq i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
.

Now similar to (
83
), the left-hand side of (
85
) can be bounded by the sum of the following:

2(N_{1}/N_{2})^{k/2}\Bigg|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{*}}(N_{2}/N_{1})w_{2}(\vec{r})+\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{*}}w_{2}(\vec{r})-\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{-}}w_{2}(\vec{r})\Bigg|,

(86)

\Bigg|2(N_{1}/N_{2})^{k/2}\Bigg(\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{*}}(N_{2}/N_{1})w_{2}(\vec{r})+\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{*}}w_{2}(\vec{r})\Bigg)-\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}}2(N_{2}/N_{1})^{\mathscr{H}(3k)/2}w_{1}(\vec{r})\Bigg|,

(87)

and

\Bigg|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}}\big(2(N_{2}/N_{1})^{\mathscr{H}(3k)/2}-2+N^{-1}(N_{1}-N_{2})\mathscr{H}(3k)\big)w_{1}(\vec{r})\Bigg|.

(88)

We next bound them one-by-one. For (
86
) and (
87
), we need to exploit cancellations, as done in
Section

4.6
.

Bound (
86
).

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the map in
Definition

4.19
. Similarly to
Lemma

4.23
, for any
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{-}
such that
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}
,
i_{4}
is of type I, and
i_{4}\neq i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
, we have

\Bigg|\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{*}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{-},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}(N_{2}/N_{1})w_{2}(\vec{s})-\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{-}\cap\mathscr{B}_{*},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}\frac{N_{1}-N_{2}}{N_{1}}w_{2}(\vec{s})\Bigg|\\
<C(|U|+1)N^{-1}\prod_{j\in J_{I},j\neq i_{4}}\left(|U|N^{-1}+N^{-1}\vartheta_{j}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)\right)|w_{2}(\vec{r})|.

In addition, if
U\cap\llbracket N_{2}+1,N_{1}\rrbracket=\emptyset
and
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\leq N_{2}
, the upper bound can be improved to

C(|U|+1)(N_{1}-N_{2})N^{-2}\prod_{j\in J_{I},j\neq i_{4}}\left(|U|N^{-1}+N^{-1}\vartheta_{j}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)\right)|w_{2}(\vec{r})|.

From the above two bounds and using
Proposition

4.15
, we get

2(N_{1}/N_{2})^{k/2}\Bigg|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{*}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{-},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})\in\mathscr{B}_{-}}(N_{2}/N_{1})w_{2}(\vec{r})-\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{-}\cap\mathscr{B}_{*}}\frac{N_{1}-N_{2}}{N_{1}}w_{2}(\vec{r})\Bigg|\\
<C(4NN_{1})^{2k}N^{-5/3}(N_{1}-N_{2}).

(89)

Besides, as in
Lemma

4.23
, for any
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}
with
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}\not\in\mathscr{B}_{-}
, we have

\Bigg|\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{*},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}(N_{2}/N_{1})w_{2}(\vec{s})+\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{*},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}w_{2}(\vec{s})\Bigg|<C\prod_{j\in J_{I}}\left(|U|N^{-1}+N^{-1}\vartheta_{j}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t)\right)|w_{2}(\vec{r})|.

Then using
Corollary

4.24
, we get

2(N_{1}/N_{2})^{k/2}\Bigg|\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{*},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})\not\in\mathscr{B}_{-}}(N_{2}/N_{1})w_{2}(\vec{r})+\sum_{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{*},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})\not\in\mathscr{B}_{-}}w_{2}(\vec{r})\Bigg|\\
<C(4NN_{1})^{2k}N^{-1}(N_{1}-N_{2}).

(90)

We next add (
89
) and (
90
).
Note that for
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{*}
with
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})\not\in\mathscr{B}_{-}
, necessarily
\vec{r}\not\in\mathscr{B}_{-}
; and for
\vec{r}\not\in\mathscr{B}_{*}
,
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{-}
if and only if
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})\in\mathscr{B}_{-}
. Therefore,

\mathscr{B}_{*}=\{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{*}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{-}:\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})\in\mathscr{B}_{-}\}\cup(\mathscr{B}_{-}\cap\mathscr{B}_{*})\cup\{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{*}:\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})\not\in\mathscr{B}_{-}\},

\mathscr{B}_{-}=(\mathscr{B}_{-}\cap\mathscr{B}_{*})\cup\{\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{*}:\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})\in\mathscr{B}_{-}\}.

Thus, adding up (
89
) and (
90
) implies that (
86
) is bounded by
C(4NN_{1})^{2k}N^{-1}(N_{1}-N_{2})
.

Bound (
87
).

Take any
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}
satisfying
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{r})=\vec{r}
,
and
\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}
with
\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}
. If
\vec{s}\not\in\mathscr{B}_{*}
, then we have
w_{2}(\vec{s})-\frac{w_{2}(\vec{r})w_{1}(\vec{s})}{w_{1}(\vec{r})}=0
. If
\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{*}
, we have

\left|(N_{2}/N_{1})w_{2}(\vec{s})-\frac{w_{2}(\vec{r})w_{1}(\vec{s})}{w_{1}(\vec{r})}\right|<CN^{-3}(N_{1}-N_{2})\vartheta_{i_{4}}|w_{2}(r)|\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t).

Then by summing over
\vec{s}
, we have

2(N_{1}/N_{2})^{k/2}\Bigg|\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{*},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}(N_{2}/N_{1})w_{2}(\vec{s})+\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{*},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}w_{2}(\vec{s})\\
-\frac{w_{2}(\vec{r})}{w_{1}(\vec{r})}\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{+},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}w_{1}(\vec{s})\Bigg|<C\exp(CN^{-1/3}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))N^{-5/3}(N_{1}-N_{2})\vartheta_{i_{4}}|w_{1}(r)|.

Besides, we have

\Bigg|2(N_{1}/N_{2})^{k/2}w_{2}(\vec{r})-2(N_{2}/N_{1})^{\mathscr{H}(3k)/2}w_{1}(\vec{r})\Bigg|\\
<C(\exp(C(\delta N^{-1}+N^{-4/3}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))(N_{1}-N_{2}))-1)|w_{1}(\vec{r})|.

The above two together imply

\Bigg|2(N_{1}/N_{2})^{k/2}\Bigg(\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{*},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}(N_{2}/N_{1})w_{2}(\vec{s})+\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{*},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}w_{2}(\vec{s})\Bigg)\\
-\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{+},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}2(N_{2}/N_{1})^{\mathscr{H}(3k)/2}w_{1}(\vec{s})\Bigg|<C\exp(CN^{-1/3}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))N^{-5/3}(N_{1}-N_{2})\vartheta_{i_{4}}|w_{1}(\vec{r})|\\
+C(\exp(C(\delta N^{-1}+N^{-4/3}\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))(N_{1}-N_{2}))-1)\Bigg|\sum_{\vec{s}\in\mathscr{B}_{+},\mathcal{Q}(\vec{s})=\vec{r}}w_{1}(\vec{s})\Bigg|.

Then by
Lemma

4.23
, and taking a summation over
\vec{r}
using
Proposition

4.15
, we can bound (
88
) by
C(4NN_{1})^{2k}N^{-1}(N_{1}-N_{2})
.

Bound (
88
).

For any
\vec{r}\in\mathscr{B}_{+}
we have

\left|2(N_{2}/N_{1})^{\mathscr{H}(3k)/2}-2+N^{-1}(N_{1}-N_{2})\mathscr{H}(3k)\right|<CN^{-2}(N_{1}-N_{2})^{2}(\max_{t}\mathscr{H}(t))^{2}.

Then with
Lemma

4.23
and
Corollary

4.24
, we can bound (
88
) by
C(4NN_{1})^{2k}N^{-4/3}(N_{1}-N_{2})^{2}
.

So far we obtained (
85
), therefore (
84
