). As in the previous proof, we can similarly estimate

•

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{2}\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}-\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{3}\right]
,

•

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{3}\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})-\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{2}\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}\right]
,

•

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})^{4}-\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{3}\right]
,

respectively, through the actions of the operators

•

\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}-k\beta(4N)^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}\right)(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}})^{2}
,

•

(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}})^{2}\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}-k\beta(4N)^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}\right)\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}
,

•

(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}})^{3}\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}-k\beta(4N)^{-1}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{N_{1}}\right)
.

A linear combination of these estimates (as in the previous proof) and
Corollary

5.4
imply the conclusion.
∎

5.2
Topological statement

In this section we present a general topological statement, which connects (uniform in compacts) convergence of families of continuous functions with convergence of sums of large powers of the kind we have in Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
. All functions in this section are deterministic.
Throughout this and the next subsection we work with
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
, whose topology is generated by open intervals
(a,b)
and
[-\infty,a)
for all
a<b\in\mathbb{R}
.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote

M_{+}[x,\alpha,N]=\frac{1}{2}(1+N^{-2/3}x)^{2\lfloor\alpha N^{2/3}/2\rfloor},\quad M_{-}[x,\alpha,N]=\frac{1}{2}(1+N^{-2/3}x)^{2\lfloor\alpha N^{2/3}/2\rfloor+1},

and
M[x,\alpha,N]=M_{+}[x,\alpha,N]+M_{-}[x,\alpha,N]
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Take any compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
.
For integers
1\leq i\leq N
, let
f^{N}_{i}:I\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous functions, satisfying
f^{N}_{i}\geq f^{N}_{i+1}
for each
1\leq i\leq N-1
.
Suppose there exists an increasing sequence of positive integers
N_{1}<N_{2}<\cdots
, such that for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
or
\alpha^{-1}\in\mathbb{N}
, as
k\to\infty
,
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
converges uniformly towards a continuous function from
I
to
\mathbb{R}
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
is bounded by a constant independent of
k
(the constant allowed to depend on the sequence and
\alpha
).
Then for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, as
k\to\infty
,
we have uniform convergence of
f_{i}^{N_{k}}
, as continuous functions from
I
to
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
.
Moreover, if we denote the limit by
f_{i}
, then for any
\alpha>0
,

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]\to\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp(\alpha f_{i})
uniformly over
I
as
k\to\infty
.

Proof.

We also denote (for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
)

M_{+}^{\prime}[x,\alpha,N]=\left(\max\{1+N^{-2/3}x,0\}\right)^{2\lfloor\alpha N^{2/3}/2\rfloor}.

Then for
x>-N^{2/3}
,

|M_{+}^{\prime}[x,\alpha,N]-M[x,\alpha,N]|=N^{-2/3}|x|M_{+}[x,\alpha,N].

Take any
\delta_{-},\delta_{+}>0
.
Note that
|x|\leq\delta_{+}^{-1}(1+N^{-2/3}x)^{\delta_{+}N^{2/3}}
when
x>0
and
|x|\leq\delta_{-}^{-1}(1+N^{-2/3}x)^{-\delta_{-}N^{2/3}}
when
-N^{2/3}<x<0
. So for
x>-N^{2/3}
,
\alpha>\delta_{-}
, and
N
large enough depending on
\delta_{-},\delta_{+}
, we have

|M_{+}^{\prime}[x,\alpha,N]-M[x,\alpha,N]|\leq 2N^{-2/3}(\delta_{+}^{-1}M_{+}[x,\alpha+\delta_{+},N]+\delta_{-}^{-1}M_{+}[x,\alpha-\delta_{-},N]).

(91)

For
-\infty<x<-N^{2/3}
we have

|M_{+}^{\prime}[x,\alpha,N]-M[x,\alpha,N]|=|2+xN^{-2/3}|M_{+}[x,\alpha,N].

Let
x^{\prime}=-2N^{2/3}-x>-N^{2/3}
. Then
|2+xN^{-2/3}|M_{+}[x,\alpha,N]=|x^{\prime}|M_{+}[x^{\prime},\alpha,N]
,
M_{+}[x,\alpha+\delta_{+},N]=M_{+}[x^{\prime},\alpha+\delta_{+},N]
, and
M_{+}[x,\alpha-\delta_{-},N]=M_{+}[x^{\prime},\alpha-\delta_{-},N]
.
Thus by applying the
x>-N^{2/3}
case arguments with
x
replaced by
x^{\prime}
, we also get (
91
).
When
x=-N^{2/3}
, (
91
) also holds since
M_{+}^{\prime}[-N^{-2/3},\alpha,N]=M[-N^{-2/3},\alpha,N]=0

Next, we combine (
91
) with
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
being bounded for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\alpha^{-1}\in\mathbb{N}
. We conclude that for any
\alpha>0
, as
k\to\infty
,
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}}(s_{k}),\alpha,N_{k}]
converges
if and only if
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M[f_{i}^{N_{k}}(s_{k}),\alpha,N_{k}]
converges. The limit would be the same if both converge.

We now prove the convergence of
f_{1}^{N_{k}}
. The idea is that for a large
\alpha
only the first term in
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}}(s_{k}),\alpha,N_{k}]
significantly contributes to the
k\to\infty
limit. For the formal proof, we argue by contradiction, and suppose that the convergence does not hold.
Note that
\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sup_{I}f_{1}^{N_{k}}<\infty
, because
M_{+}[f_{1}^{N_{k}},1,N_{k}]
is uniformly bounded.
Then there exist two disjoint subsequences of
N_{1}<N_{2}<\cdots
, denoted by
P_{1}<P_{2}<\cdots
and
Q_{1}<Q_{2}<\cdots
, and some
t_{1},t_{2},\cdots\to t
and
s_{1},s_{2},\cdots\to t
in
I
, such that
f_{1}^{P_{k}}(t_{k})\to a
and
f_{1}^{Q_{k}}(s_{k})\to b
as
k\to\infty
, where
a,b\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
with
a<b
.

Note that for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
, since
\lim_{k\to\infty}f_{1}^{Q_{k}}(t_{k})=b
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{P_{k}}(t_{k}),\alpha,P_{k}]=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{Q_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{Q_{k}}(s_{k}),\alpha,Q_{k}]\geq\exp(\alpha b).

On the other hand, since
\lim_{k\to\infty}f_{1}^{P_{k}}(t_{k})=a
, and
f_{1}^{P_{k}}(t_{k})\geq f_{i}^{P_{k}}(t_{k})
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{P_{k}}(t_{k}),\alpha,P_{k}]\leq\exp((\alpha-1)a)\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{P_{k}}(t_{k}),1,P_{k}].

Combining the above two inequalities, we obtain

\exp(\alpha(b-a)+a)\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{P_{k}}(t_{k}),1,P_{k}]

Since
a<b
, and the right-hand side is finite, the above inequality can not hold for large enough
\alpha
, and we arrive at a contradiction. Therefore we get the uniform convergence of
f_{1}^{N_{k}}
as
k\to\infty
.

From the convergence of
f_{1}^{N_{k}}
,
\sum_{i=2}^{N_{k}}M[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
converges uniformly as
k\to\infty
, and
\sum_{i=2}^{N_{k}}M_{+}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
is bounded by a constant independent of
k
.
Then we can deduce the convergence of
f_{2}^{N_{k}}
using the same arguments; and further, we can inductively get the convergence of
f_{i}^{N_{k}}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

We next take any
\alpha>0
, and prove

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]\to\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp(\alpha f_{i}).

(92)

Denote
X=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]
.
Take
\alpha_{1}^{-1}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\alpha_{1}<\alpha<\alpha_{2}
.
From
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha_{1},N_{k}]+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha_{2},N_{k}]
we have that
X<\infty
.
Take any
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Note that for any
j^{\prime}\in\llbracket j,N_{k}\rrbracket
,

M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{j^{\prime}}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]\leq j^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}].

Therefore,

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=j}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]\leq(j^{-1}X)^{1-\alpha_{1}/\alpha}\lim_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha_{1},N_{k}].

This implies that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sum_{i=j}^{N_{k}}M_{+}^{\prime}[f_{i}^{N_{k}},\alpha,N_{k}]=0.

Then by the convergence of
f_{i}^{N_{k}}\to f_{i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we get (
92
).
∎

5.3
Distributional convergence and uniqueness of the limiting process

Finally we prove the uniqueness of Airy
β
line ensemble, as well as the convergence for both the DBM and the G
\beta
E corners processes, thereby finishing the proofs of our main results.

Proof of
Theorem

1.4
,
Theorem

1.5
, and
Theorem

1.6
.

Our proof consists of two steps.
First, we show that both
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
\{\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
are tight, and the moments of Laplace transform for any subsequential limit are given by
\mathbf{L}_{\beta}(\vec{\mathbf{k}},\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
from
Definition

2.12
.
This gives the existence part of
Theorem

1.4
.
Second, we prove the uniqueness part, with which we also get
Theorem

1.5
and
Theorem

1.6
.

Step 1: tightness and subsequential limit.

It is classical (by e.g.,
[
Bil99
, Theorem 7.3]
) that from
Theorem

4.2
and
Proposition

5.2
, for any fixed
\mathbf{k}>0
,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}M[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
is tight, as continuous functions from
[0,\infty)
to
\mathbb{R}
, under the uniform in compact topology.
Note that
\{Y_{i}(\tau)\}_{1\leq i\leq N,\tau>0}
and
\{-Y_{i}(\tau)\}_{1\leq i\leq N,\tau>0}
have the same law, therefore
\sum_{i=1}^{N}(M_{+}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]-M_{-}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N])
has the same law as
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
, and is also tight.
Taking the sum or the difference of the above two processes together, we have that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{+}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{-}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
are also tight. (See
[
EK09
, Chapter 3]
for general information on tightness in the spaces of continuous functions.)
20
20

20

We note that the use of symmetry (in deriving the tightness of
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{+}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{-}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
) is not essential.
Alternatively, one-point tightness of
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{+}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{-}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
can be obtained using
Proposition

4.20
,
and bounds on time increments are again from

Proposition

5.2
.

Thus, we can take a subsequence so that both
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{+}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}M_{-}[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k},N]
converge for each
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{k}^{-1}\in\mathbb{N}
; and by Skorokhod’s representation theorem (see e.g.,
[
Bil99
, Chapter 1, Theorem 6.7]
) we can make it almost sure convergence.
By
Proposition

5.5
,
\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}
for each
i
converges along this subsequence.

Take any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
. By
Theorem

4.2
,
\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}M[\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}/2,\mathbf{k}_{j},N])
is uniformly integrable in
N
.
Then from the second part of
Proposition

5.5
and
Theorem

4.2
, the Laplace transforms of the limit of
\{\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
have moments given by
Theorem

1.4
.

Using the same arguments, and
Theorem

4.1
,
Proposition

5.1
instead of
Theorem

4.2
,
Proposition

5.2
,
we also get the convergence of the rescaled Gaussian
\beta
corners process
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, and the Laplace transforms of the limit have moments given by
Theorem

1.4
.

21
21

21

For G
\beta
E corners process,
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})
is not continuous in
\boldsymbol{\tau}
due to discretization, and is also not defined when
i>N-\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3}
. Thus to apply
Proposition

5.5
, we need to consider a different
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})
, constructed as follows. For
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in N^{-2/3}\mathbb{Z}
,
0\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}<N^{1/3}
, we let
\{\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{N}
contain the same numbers as
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{N-\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3}}
and in addition
\boldsymbol{\tau}N^{2/3}
copies of
-N^{2/3}
(note that
M_{+}[-N^{-2/3},\mathbf{k},N]=M_{-}[-N^{-2/3},\mathbf{k},N]=0
). For generic
\boldsymbol{\tau}
we linearly interpolate between
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in N^{-2/3}\mathbb{Z}
. Then
Proposition

5.5
applies and the same arguments go through.

Note that so far we have established the uniform in compact subsequential convergence for
\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}
and
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}

and moments for the Laplace transform of the limit for
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in[0,\infty)
, and we need to extend this to
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}
.
For
\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}
, using that the DBM is invariant under diffusive scaling, the convergence can be extended to
[-C,\infty)
for any
C>0
, thereby to
\mathbb{R}
.
For
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(N)}
, note that for any
C>0
we also have the same convergence for
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{(\lfloor N+CN^{2/3}\rfloor)}
; thus we have the convergence of
\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}
on
[-C,\infty)
, thereby we get the extension to
\mathbb{R}
.

Step 2: uniqueness.

Take
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
to be a subsequential limit of
\{\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
constructed in Step 1. Suppose that there is another processes
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{2,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, such that both
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{2,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
have continuous sample paths in
\boldsymbol{\tau}
, and both have moments of their Laplace transforms given by
Theorem

1.4
and
Definition

2.12
.

Now for each
\iota=1,2
and any
\boldsymbol{\tau},h\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
f_{\iota}(\boldsymbol{\tau},h)=\max\{i\in\mathbb{N}:\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{\iota,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})>h\}
(with the convention
\max\emptyset=0
).
We would like to prove that
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{2,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
have the same distributions and for that it is sufficient to show that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
have the same finite dimensional distributions.

From the moments of Laplace transforms in (
5
), via an integration by parts, we have that for any
\vec{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m}
and
\vec{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathbf{k}_{\ell}h_{\ell}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell},h_{\ell})\right]\mathrm{d}\vec{h}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\exp\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\mathbf{k}_{\ell}h_{\ell}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=2}^{m}f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell},h_{\ell})\right]\mathrm{d}\vec{h}.

Then by the uniqueness theorem for Laplace transform (i.e. Lerch’s theorem
[
Ler03
]
), for any
\vec{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
except for a Lebesgue measure zero set,

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell},h_{\ell})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=2}^{m}f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell},h_{\ell})\right].

Since the expectations are right continuous in each
h_{\ell}
, this equality holds for any
\vec{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Namely, we have that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
equal in any finite mixed moments.

Next, we claim that for any fixed
\boldsymbol{\tau},h
, the tails of
f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau},h)
have exponential decay. With this in hand, we have that the expectation of
\exp(\epsilon f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau},h))
is finite for some
\epsilon>0
, hence the one-dimensional marginal of
f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau},h)
is uniquely determined by its one-dimensional moments, see e.g
[
Bil86
, Theorem 30.1]
. As for the finite dimensional distribution, Peterson’s theorem (see
[
Sch17
, Theorem 14.6]
) says that it is uniquely determined by its mixed moments as long as this is the case for all its marginals. Therefore we have that the mixed moments of
f_{1}
uniquely determine its finite dimensional distribution, which implies that for any
\vec{\tau}=(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},...,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\vec{h}=(h_{1},...,h_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
(f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},h_{1}),...,f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m},h_{m}))
and
(f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},h_{1}),...,f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{m},h_{m}))
, must be equal in distribution, because they share the same mixed moments. This further implies that
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
and
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{2,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
also have the same finite dimensional distribution.

To get the exponential tail probability of
f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau},h)
, note that for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{P}[f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau},h)\geq i]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})>h]
. Moreover, since we obtain
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
as a subsequential limit of
\{\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{(N)}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, for any fixed
\boldsymbol{\tau}
,
\{-\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{1,i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
is equal in distribution to
the eigenvalues of the Stochastic Airy Operator, by
[
RRV07
, Theorem 1.1]
.
Then the exponential decay of the tail of
f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\tau},h)
follows from
Lemma

5.6
below (deduced from
[
RRV07
, Theorem 1.2]
).
This finishes the proof of the uniqueness.
∎

Let us state the tail bound of the Stochastic Airy Operator, used in the previous proof.
Let
\Lambda_{0}\leq\Lambda_{1}\leq\cdots
denote the eigenvalues of the Stochastic Airy Operator, as in
[
RRV07
]
.
They are also the edge limit of the Gaussian
\beta
Ensemble from (
1
), see
[
RRV07
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
h\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists some
0<c<1
(depending on
h
), such that
\mathbb{P}[\Lambda_{i-1}<-h]<c^{i}
for any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

For
h\in\mathbb{R}
, let
p=p_{h}
be a one-dimensional diffusion on
t\in[h,\infty)
defined by the Itô equation:

dp(t)=\left(t-p^{2}(t)\right)dt+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\beta}}dB(t),

(93)

such that it starts from
+\infty
at time
h
; and whenever it arrives at
-\infty
, it starts again from
+\infty
.

[
RRV07
, Theorem 1.2]
states that
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{P}[\Lambda_{i-1}<-h]
equals the probability of the event that
p_{h}
comes through
-\infty
at least
i
times at times
t\geq h
.

From the diffusion
p_{h}
, it can also be derived that, if
q(h)
is the probability that
p_{h}
arrives at
-\infty
in finite time, then (A)
q(h)<1
for any
h
, and (B)
q(h)
is non-increasing in
h
.

To see (A), note that by
[
RRV07
, Theorem 1.2]
,

q(h)=1-\mathbb{P}[\Lambda_{0}\geq-h],

(94)

which is in
(0,1)
for all
h\in\mathbb{R}
(see
[
RRV07
, Theorem 1.3]
for a tail estimate of
\Lambda_{0}
). (B) also follows directly from (
94
).

From (B) we have
\mathbb{P}[\Lambda_{i-1}<-h]<(q(h))^{i}
, and by (A) the conclusion follows.
∎

The just presented proof can be also used to deduce the following continuity estimate for the Laplace transform of
\{\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
. This implies that the Laplace transform is Hölder
\frac{1}{4}-
function in
\boldsymbol{\tau}
; with additional efforts the estimate can be upgraded to Hölder
\frac{1}{2}-
class.

Corollary 5.7
.

For any
C_{1}>0
, there exists
C=C(C_{1})>0
such that the following holds.
For any
-C_{1}\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}<\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}\leq C_{1}
,
C_{1}^{-1}<\mathbf{k}<C_{1}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp(\mathbf{k}\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})/2)-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp(\mathbf{k}\mathcal{A}^{\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2})/2)\right)^{4}\right]<C(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1})^{2}.

Proof.

From
Theorem

1.4
and Theorems
4.1
,
4.2
, we have that the above expectation is precisely the
N\to\infty
limit of the expectations in
Proposition

5.1
or
Proposition

5.2
. Thus by
Proposition

5.1
or
Proposition

5.2
the conclusion follows.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

One can write the expectation in
Corollary

5.7
through
Theorem

1.4
, then Taylor expand in
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}
.
Corollary

5.7
says that the coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}
, which is a certain combination of integrals, miraculously vanishes. This is a non-trivial integral identity, which eventually follows from the Dunkl operator identity of
Corollary

5.4
.

Appendix A
Quantitative approximation of random walks

A.1
Upper bounds for numbers of walks

The following estimates are used in the proof of
Proposition

4.15
.

We let
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,G)
be the collection of all
F:\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
, satisfying
|F(t)-F(t-1)|=1
for each
t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket
, and
F(0)=H
,
F(X)=G
.
We also let
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
be the subset of
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,G)
, consisting of those
F
which stay non-negative.
And we use
C,c>0
to denote large and small universal constants, whose values may change from line to line.

Lemma A.1
.

Take any
X,H\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
M\geq 0
. There exist
C,c>0
such that:

(i)

\sum_{G\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},|G-H|\geq M}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|\leq C2^{X}\exp(-cM^{2}/X)
.

(ii)

|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|\leq CX^{-1/2}2^{X}\exp(-cM^{2}/X)
, for any
G\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, such that
|G-H|\geq M
.

(iii)

\sum_{G\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},G\geq M}|\mathscr{F}(X;0,G)|\leq CX^{-1/2}2^{X}\exp(-cM^{2}/X)
.

(iv)

|\mathscr{F}(X;H,0)|\leq CX^{-1}2^{X}\exp(-cM^{2}/X)
, whenever
H\geq M
.

(v)

|\mathscr{F}(X;0,0)|\leq CX^{-3/2}2^{X}
.

Proof.

For some choices of parity of
X
,
H
,
G
, the set
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
is empty and the inequalities are trivially true. Throughout the proof we silently assume that the parities are chosen so that
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
has at least one element.
Besides, without loss of generality, we assume that
M
is an even integer.

We repeatedly use the reflection principle throughout the proof (see, e.g.
[
Dur19
, Theorem 4.9.1]
). Namely, for any
X,H,G\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|=|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,G)|-|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,-G-2)|.

Also recall that

|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,G)|={X\choose(X+H-G)/2}.

We will also use asymptotic bounds on binomial coefficients: for any integers
0<A\leq B
, we have
{B\choose A}<2^{B}B^{-1/2}\exp(-(2A-B)^{2}/(2B))
.
This can be obtained by the Stirling’s formula.

The bound (i) follows from
\sum_{G\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},|G-M|\geq M}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|\leq\sum_{G\in\mathbb{Z},|G-H|\geq M}|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,G)|=\sum_{G\in\mathbb{Z},|G-H|\geq M}{X\choose(X+H-G)/2}\leq C2^{X}\exp(-cM^{2}/X)
.

For (ii), it follows from that
|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|\leq|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,G)|={X\choose(X+H-G)/2}
.

For (iii), by reflection principle, the sum equals
|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;0,M)|
or
|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;0,M+1)|
, depending on the parity of
X+M
. Therefore the estimate follows from the bound on the binomial coefficient.

For (iv), by reflection principle,
|\mathscr{F}(X;H,0)|
equals
|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,0)|-|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,-2)|={X\choose(X+H)/2}-{X\choose(X+H)/2+1}={X\choose(X+H)/2}\cdot\frac{H+1}{(X+H)/2+1}
, which is bounded as desired.

For (v), it is the Catalan number
{X\choose X/2}\cdot\frac{1}{X/2+1}
, and the bound holds.
∎

We will need the following statement on the monotonicity of random walk bridges.

Lemma A.2
.

Take any
X,H,G,R,L,H^{\prime},G^{\prime},R^{\prime},L^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, with
X+H+G
and
X+H^{\prime}+G^{\prime}
even, and
H\leq H^{\prime}
,
G\leq G^{\prime}
,
R\leq R^{\prime}
,
L\leq L^{\prime}
.
Sample
\mathfrak{f}:\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
uniformly from
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H,G)
, conditional on
R\leq\min\mathfrak{f}\leq\max\mathfrak{f}\leq L
; and
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}:\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
uniformly from
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X;H^{\prime},G;)
, conditional on
R^{\prime}\leq\min\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}\leq\max\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}\leq L^{\prime}
.
Then we can couple
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
in such a way that
\mathfrak{f}(t)\leq\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket
.

Lemma

A.2
can be proved using a coupling of Monte-Carlo Markov chains for sampling
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
.
See e.g. Lemmas 2.6 and 2.7 in
[
CH14
]
and their proofs.

Lemma A.3
.

Take any
X,H,G,L\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
X+H+G
even, and sample
\mathfrak{f}:\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
uniformly from
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
, conditional on
\max\mathfrak{f}\leq L
.
Take
k\in\mathbb{N}
and discrete intervals
\llbracket X_{1},Y_{1}\rrbracket,\ldots,\llbracket X_{k},Y_{k}\rrbracket
contained in
\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket
.
Assume that they are disjoint from each other, except for at endpoints and also assume that
Y_{i}-X_{i}\geq 1
for all
i
. Take
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, and denote
a=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}
. For each
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, denote
V_{i}=\max_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}-\min_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}+1
.
We have

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i}^{a_{i}}}{a_{i}!}\right]<C^{a}\log(a+1)^{a}(a^{-a/2}X^{a/2}+a^{-a}|H-G|^{a})

(95)

and for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i}^{a_{i}}}{a_{i}!}(\mathds{1}[\max\mathfrak{f}\geq\max\{H,G\}+K]+\mathds{1}[\min\mathfrak{f}\leq\min\{H,G\}-K])\right]\\
<C^{a}\log(a)^{a}(a^{-a/2}X^{a/2}+a^{-a}(|H-G|+K)^{a})\exp(-cK^{2}/X).

(96)

We emphasize that the constant
C
does not depend on
k
, i.e., the number of intervals.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we assume that
H\leq G
.

We start by deriving (
95
).
For each
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, we write
Z_{i}=\frac{V_{i}-(G-H)(Y_{i}-X_{i})/X}{(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}}
.

Claim A.4
.

We have that
\mathbb{P}[Z_{i}>x]<C\exp(-cx^{2})
for any
x>0
, where
C,c>0
are universal constants (i.e., independent of
X,H,G,L,X_{i},Y_{i}
).

We postpone the proof of this claim, and proceed assuming it.

We write
Z=\max\{0,Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{k}\}
.
Then
\mathbb{P}[Z>x]<Ck\exp(-cx^{2})
for any
x>0
, and
\mathbb{P}[Z>x\sqrt{\log(k+1)}]<Ck\exp(-c(k+1)x^{2})<C\exp(-cx^{2})
.
This implies that
\frac{Z}{C\sqrt{\log(k+1)}}
is stochastically dominated by the absolute value of a standard normal random variable.
Thus we have
\mathbb{E}[Z^{m}]<(C\log(k+1)m)^{m/2}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Now to prove (
95
), we note that for any
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,

\frac{V_{i}^{a_{i}}}{a_{i}!}=\sum_{p_{i}=0}^{a_{i}}\frac{Z_{i}^{p_{i}}(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{p_{i}/2}((G-H)(Y_{i}-X_{i})/X)^{q_{i}}}{p_{i}!q_{i}!},

(97)

where
q_{i}=a_{i}-p_{i}
.
Therefore we consider

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{Z^{p_{i}}(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{p_{i}/2}((G-H)(Y_{i}-X_{i})/X)^{q_{i}}}{p_{i}!q_{i}!}\right],

(98)

where
p_{i},q_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
p_{i}+q_{i}=a_{i}
for each
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
.
Denote
p=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}
and
q=\sum_{i=1}^{k}q_{i}
.
We have
\mathbb{E}[Z^{p}]<(C\log(k+1)p)^{p/2}
, and
\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}!q_{i}!>c^{a}\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}^{p_{i}}q_{i}^{q_{i}}
.
Also, since the logarithm function is concave, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\log\left(p_{i}^{-1}(Y_{i}-X_{i})\right)\leq p\log\left(p^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}Y_{i}-X_{i}\right)\right)\leq p\log(p^{-1}X),

\sum_{i=1}^{k}q_{i}\log\left(q_{i}^{-1}(Y_{i}-X_{i})\right)\leq q\log\left(q^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}Y_{i}-X_{i}\right)\right)\leq q\log(q^{-1}X),

so

\prod_{i=1}^{k}(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{p_{i}/2}\leq\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}^{p_{i}/2}}{p^{p/2}}X^{p/2},\quad\prod_{i=1}^{k}(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{q_{i}}\leq\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}q_{i}^{q_{i}}}{q^{q}}X^{q}.

Thus, we bound (
98
) by

C^{a}\log(a+1)^{a}\frac{X^{p/2}}{p^{p/2}}\cdot\frac{(G-H)^{q}}{q^{q}}.

The inequality
y<e^{y}
applied to
y=a/p,a/q
leads to
(a/p)^{p},(a/q)^{q}<e^{a}
, and, therefore, we have
\frac{1}{p^{p/2}q^{q}}<\frac{e^{3a/2}}{a^{p/2+q}}
.
Then we can further bound (
98
) by

C^{a}\log(a+1)^{a}\frac{X^{p/2}(G-H)^{q}}{a^{p/2+q}}<C^{a}\log(a+1)^{a}(a^{-a/2}X^{a/2}+a^{-a}(G-H)^{a}),

where the inequality is by
\frac{X^{p/2}(G-H)^{q}}{a^{p/2+q}}=\left(\frac{X^{1/2}}{a^{1/2}}\right)^{p}\left(\frac{G-H}{a}\right)^{q}\leq\max\left\{\frac{X^{1/2}}{a^{1/2}},\frac{G-H}{a}\right\}^{p+q}
, and
p+q=a
.
By summing over all (at most
2^{a}
) possible choices of
p_{i},q_{i}
, and using (
97
), we get (
95
).

For (
96
), note that
\mathbb{P}[\max\mathfrak{f}\geq G+K]<C\exp(-cK^{2}/X)
. (This follows from the same arguments as bounding the probability of
\mathcal{E}_{2}
in the proof of
Claim

A.4
below.)
For
\mathfrak{f}
conditional on
\max\mathfrak{f}\geq G+K
, it can be sampled as follows.
First take a
Y\in\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket
such that
Y+H+G+K
is even.
Then take
\mathfrak{f}_{-}
uniformly random from
\mathscr{F}(Y;H,G+K)
conditional on
\leq G+K
, and
\mathfrak{f}_{+}
uniformly random from
\mathscr{F}(X-Y;G+K,G)
conditional on
\leq L
.
Finally one gets
\mathfrak{f}
by concatenating
\mathfrak{f}_{-}
and
\mathfrak{f}_{+}
.
It now suffices to show that

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i,-}^{a_{i,-}}}{a_{i,-}!}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{V_{i,+}^{a_{i,+}}}{a_{i,+}!}\right]<C^{a}\log(a+1)^{a}(a^{-a/2}X^{a/2}+a^{-a}(G-H+K)^{a}),

(99)

where

•

V_{i,-}=\max_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}_{-}-\min_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}_{-}
and
a_{i,-}=a_{i}
if
Y_{i}\leq Y
;

V_{i,-}=1
and
a_{i,-}=0
if
X_{i}\geq Y
;

V_{i,-}=\max_{\llbracket X_{i},Y\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}_{-}-\min_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}_{-}
and
a_{i,-}
be any number in
\llbracket 0,a_{i}\rrbracket
if
X_{i}<Y<Y_{i}
.

•

V_{i,+}=\max_{\llbracket X_{i}-Y,Y_{i}-Y\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}_{+}-\min_{\llbracket X_{i}-Y,Y_{i}-Y\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}_{+}
and
a_{i,+}=a_{i}
if
X_{i}\geq Y
;

V_{i,+}=1
and
a_{i,+}=0
if
Y_{i}\leq Y
;

V_{i,+}=\max_{\llbracket 0,Y_{i}-Y\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}_{+}-\min_{\llbracket 0,Y_{i}-Y\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}_{-}
and
a_{i,+}=a_{i}-a_{i,-}
if
X_{i}<Y<Y_{i}
.

We have that (
99
) directly follows from applying (
95
) to
\mathfrak{f}_{-}
and
\mathfrak{f}_{+}
, respectively.
Then from (
99
), by summing over all
(at most
a+1
) possible choices of
a_{i,-}
(when there is one
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
with
X_{i}<Y<Y_{i}
), and averaging over all
Y
, the conclusion follows.
The estimate for
\mathfrak{f}
conditional on
\max\mathfrak{f}\leq H-K
follows similarly.
∎

Proof of
Claim

A.4
.

Under the event where
Z_{i}>x
, one of the following must happen

\mathcal{E}_{1}
:

|\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})-\mathfrak{f}(X_{i})|>(G-H)(Y_{i}-X_{i})/X+x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/3-1
; or

\mathcal{E}_{2}
:

\max_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}>\max\{\mathfrak{f}(X_{i}),\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})\}+x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/3
; or

\mathcal{E}_{3}
:

\min_{\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket}\mathfrak{f}<\min\{\mathfrak{f}(X_{i}),\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})\}-x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/3
.

We next show that the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is contained in the union of the following two events:

\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}
:

|\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})-\mathfrak{f}(X_{i})|>(\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})-H)(Y_{i}-X_{i})/Y_{i}+x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/6-1
; or

\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
:

|\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})-\mathfrak{f}(X_{i})|>(G-\mathfrak{f}(X_{i}))(Y_{i}-X_{i})/(X-X_{i})+x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/6-1
.

Indeed, if none of
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}
or
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
hold, while
\mathcal{E}_{1}
holds, then necessarily
(\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})-H)/Y_{i}>(G-H)/X
, thus,
\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})>H+(G-H)Y_{i}/X
, and similarly
\mathfrak{f}(X_{i})<G-(G-H)(X-X_{i})/X=H+(G-H)X_{i}/X
.
In particular
\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})>\mathfrak{f}(X_{i})
.
Then, by taking the reverse inequality in
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}
multiplied by
Y_{i}
, plus the reverse inequality in
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
multiplied by
X-X_{i}
, we obtain

X(\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})-\mathfrak{f}(X_{i}))\leq(G-H)(Y_{i}-X_{i})+(Y_{i}+X-X_{i})(x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/6-1).

Dividing by
X
, we get a contradiction with
\mathcal{E}_{1}
. This proves the desired
\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}_{1}
.

We next show that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}\mid\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})]<C\exp(-cx^{2})
. Note that while the left-hand side is a conditional probability, the bound is uniform and does not depend on
\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})
.

We first analyze the inequality in
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{1}
without absolute value; namely, we prove

\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{f}(X_{i})<\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})X_{i}/Y_{i}+H(Y_{i}-X_{i})/Y_{i}-x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/6+1\mid\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})]<C\exp(-cx^{2}).

(100)

By
Lemma

A.2
, it suffices to prove (
100
) with
\mathfrak{f}
replaced by
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
, uniformly sampled from
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i};H,\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i}))
conditional on
\leq\max\{\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i}),H\}
.
In order to prove the inequality involving
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
, we count walks in
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i};H,\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i}))
satisfying certain conditions.
First, the number of walks in
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i};H,\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i}))
that are
\leq\max\{\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i}),H\}
equals
|\mathscr{F}(Y_{i};|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|,0)|
, which (using the reflection principle) is the same as

\begin{split}&|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i};|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|,0)|-|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i};|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|,-2)|\\
&={Y_{i}\choose(Y_{i}+|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|)/2}-{Y_{i}\choose(Y_{i}+|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|+2)/2}\\
&=\frac{2|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|+2}{Y_{i}+|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|+2}|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i};|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|,0)|.\end{split}

(101)

On the other hand, for walks in
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i};H,\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i}))
that are
\leq\max\{\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i}),H\}
, and equal
\max\{\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i}),H\}-K
at
X_{i}
for some fixed
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the number of such walks equals
|\mathscr{F}(X_{i};|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|,K)|\cdot|\mathscr{F}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,K)|
or
|\mathscr{F}(X_{i};0,K)|\cdot|\mathscr{F}(Y_{i}-X_{i};|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|,K)|
, depending on whether
\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})\geq H
or not.
In the former case, using the reflection principle, the number of walks is bounded by

\frac{(|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|+1)(K+1)}{X_{i}+2}|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X_{i};|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|,K)|\cdot\frac{2K+2}{Y_{i}-X_{i}+2}|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,K)|.

(102)

Note that by Stirling’s formula, we have

\frac{|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(X_{i};|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|,K)|\cdot|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,K)|}{|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i};|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|,K)|}\\
<C\sqrt{\frac{Y_{i}+2}{(X_{i}+2)(Y_{i}-X_{i}+2)}}\exp\left(-c\frac{(K-|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|(Y_{i}-X_{i})/Y_{i})^{2}(Y_{i}+2)}{(X_{i}+2)(Y_{i}-X_{i}+2)}\right).

Using this, if we sum (
102
) over all
K>|H-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})|(Y_{i}-X_{i})/Y_{i}+x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/6-1
, and divide by (
101
), the result would be upper bounded by
C\exp(-cx^{2})
.
When
\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})<H
the same upper bound can be derived similarly.
These imply (
100
).

By the same argument we can analyze the inequality in
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}
with the left-hand side replaced by
\mathfrak{f}(X_{i})-\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})
without absolute value. Then we get
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}\mid\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})]<C\exp(-cx^{2})
, and similarly
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mid\mathfrak{f}(X_{i})]<C\exp(-cx^{2})
.
These together imply that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}]<C\exp(-cx^{2})
.

We then consider
\mathcal{E}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{1}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
Y_{i}-X_{i}
is even, and the odd case follows similarly.

Conditional on
\mathfrak{f}(X_{i})
and
\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})
, the function
\mathfrak{f}
on
\llbracket X_{i},Y_{i}\rrbracket
is a random Bernoulli random bridge, conditional on
\geq 0
and
\leq L
.
By
Lemma

A.2
,
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}\mid\mathfrak{f}(X_{i}),\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})]
is bounded by the probability of
\max\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}>x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/3
, where
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
is uniformly chosen from
\mathscr{F}(Y_{i}-X_{i};\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor,\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor)
.
It then suffices to consider the number of walks in
\mathscr{F}(Y_{i}-X_{i};\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor,\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor)
with maximum
>x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/3
, and
|\mathscr{F}(Y_{i}-X_{i};\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor,\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor)|
, and upper bound their ratio.
Using the reflection principle, we have

\displaystyle|\mathscr{F}(Y_{i}-X_{i};\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor,\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor)|

\displaystyle=|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,0)|-|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,2\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor+2)|>\frac{1}{10}|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,0)|.

On the other hand, for walks in
\mathscr{F}(Y_{i}-X_{i};\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor,\lfloor(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}\rfloor)
with maximum
>x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/3
,
their number is at most the number of walks in
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,0)
, with maximum
>(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}(x/3-1)
.
Using the reflection principle, this is at most

|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,\lfloor x(Y_{i}-X_{i})^{1/2}/3\rfloor)|
(when
x>6
) or
|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,0)|
(otherwise).
In either case, using Stirling’s formula, the ratio of the last expression and
|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(Y_{i}-X_{i};0,0)|
is
<C\exp(-cx^{2})
.
We then conclude that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}\mid\mathfrak{f}(X_{i}),\mathfrak{f}(Y_{i})]<C\exp(-cx^{2})
, thus
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]<C\exp(-cx^{2})
.
Similarly we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{3}]<C\exp(-cx^{2})
.

Putting all of the above bounds together, we get
\mathbb{P}[Z_{i}>x]<C\exp(-cx^{2})
.
∎

A.2
Exact asymptotic walk counts

Recall the notations from
Definition

2.9
and
Definition

4.39
(and also at the beginning of
Section

A.1
).
We next provide more precise estimates on the size
|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|
, which are used in
Section

4.8
.

Lemma A.5
.

For any
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, there exists a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{1},C_{2})>0
, such that for each
N=1,2,\dots
, and
x,h,g\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and
X,H,G\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, satisfying:
C_{1}N^{-2/3}<x<C_{2}
and
C_{1}N^{-1/3}<h,g<C_{2}N^{1/100}
, and
|X-xN^{2/3}|,|H-hN^{1/3}|,|G-gN^{1/3}|<C_{2}
, and
X+H+G
is even, we have

\left|2^{-X-1}N^{1/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|-\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)\right|\\
<C_{3}\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)\left(1\wedge(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/10}+N^{-1/3}/h+N^{-1/3}/g)\right).

(103)

Proof.

Recall that

\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\left(\exp\left(-\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{(g+h)^{2}}{2x}\right)\right).

In this proof we use
C,c>0
to denote large and small constants that can depend on
C_{1},C_{2}
.

Without loss of generality we assume that
G\leq H
.
When
X
is small (i.e., bounded by any fixed constant),
x
is of order at most
N^{-2/3}
, thus, the second factor in the right-hand side of (
103
) equals
1
.
Note that
2^{-X-1}N^{1/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|
is of order
N^{1/3}
.
If
|H-G|\leq X
, then
\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)>cN^{1/3}
, because
x
is of order
N^{-2/3}
,
g-h
is of order
N^{-1/3}
, and
h
is of order at least
N^{-1/3}
. Hence, the conclusion follows by taking
C_{3}
in the right-hand side of (
103
) to be large. On the other hand, if
|H-G|>X
, then
|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|=0
, and the conclusion follows by taking an arbitrary
C_{3}>1
in the right-hand side of (
103
).

Below we assume that
X
is large enough. Note that large
X
implies large
N
, because we assumed
x<C_{2}
, and
|X-xN^{2/3}|<C_{2}
.

When
|G-H|<X
we have via reflection principle:

|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|={X\choose(X+H-G)/2}\left(1-\frac{((X-H-G)/2)_{G+1}}{((X+H-G)/2+1)_{G+1}}\right).

Stirling’s approximation implies that when
|G-H|<X
, we have:

\Bigg|\log\left({X\choose(X+H-G)/2}\right)-\log\left(\frac{2X}{\pi(X+H-G)(X-H+G)}\right)/2\\
-X\log(X)+(X+H-G)\log((X+H-G)/2)/2\\
+(X-H+G)\log((X-H+G)/2)/2\Bigg|<\frac{C}{X+H-G}+\frac{C}{X-H+G}.

(104)

For
(X+H-G)\log(X+H-G)/2+(X-H+G)\log(X-H+G)/2-X\log(X)
, it can be written as

X\left((1+(H-G)/X)\log(1+(H-G)/X)/2+(1-(H-G)/X)\log(1-(H-G)/X)/2\right).

By taking a Taylor expansion in the variable
(H-G)/X
, this is between
\frac{(G-H)^{2}}{2X}+C_{4}^{-1}\frac{(G-H)^{4}}{X^{3}}
and
\frac{(G-H)^{2}}{2X}+C_{4}\frac{(G-H)^{4}}{X^{3}}
, for
C_{4}>0
being a large constant that depends only on
C_{1},C_{2}
.
Therefore, we have

|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|=2^{X+1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi X}}\exp\left(-\frac{(G-H)^{2}}{2X}\right)\left(1-\frac{((X-H-G)/2)_{G+1}}{((X+H-G)/2+1)_{G+1}}\right)\Err,

where

\sqrt{\frac{X^{2}}{X^{2}-(G-H)^{2}}}\exp\left(-C_{4}\frac{(G-H)^{4}}{X^{3}}-\frac{C}{X-|G-H|}\right)\\
<\Err<\sqrt{\frac{X^{2}}{X^{2}-(G-H)^{2}}}\exp\left(-C_{4}^{-1}\frac{(G-H)^{4}}{X^{3}}+\frac{C}{X-|G-H|}\right)

(105)

and the factor
\sqrt{\frac{X^{2}}{X^{2}-(G-H)^{2}}}
is from the second term in the first line of (
104
).
We further have

\left|\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}-\frac{(G-H)^{2}}{2X}\right|<CN^{-1/3}\frac{|g-h|}{x}+CN^{-2/3}\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{x^{2}}.

(106)

Case 1.
When
x<N^{-1/5}
,
we have

1\wedge(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/10}+N^{-1/3}/h+N^{-1/3}/g)=1.

(107)

Thus, in this case, it suffices to show that
2^{-X-1}N^{1/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|<C\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)
.
When
|G-H|>X
this is obvious, since then
|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|=0
.
When
|G-H|=X
, necessarily
|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|=1
, and
||g-h|-N^{1/3}x|<CN^{-1/3}
. Thus, since
h,g>C_{1}N^{-1/3}
, we have
gh/x>c
, and

\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\exp\left(-\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}\right)(1-\exp(-2gh/x))>cN^{1/3}X^{-1/2}\exp(-X/2),

and (
103
) follows.

Below we assume that
|G-H|<X
.
Note that since
X
is large enough, there is some
C_{5}>0
depending only on
C_{1},C_{2}
, such that
N^{-4/3}\frac{(g-h)^{4}}{x^{4}}<C_{5}\frac{(G-H)^{4}}{X^{3}}
.
Then we have that the right-hand side of (
106
) is bounded by

C+(2C_{4}C_{5})^{-1}N^{-4/3}\frac{(g-h)^{4}}{x^{4}}<C+(2C_{4})^{-1}\frac{(G-H)^{4}}{X^{3}},

by taking
C
large and using
C_{4}
from (
105
).
By combining this with the upper bound of
\Err
in (
105
), we have

\exp\left(-\frac{(G-H)^{2}}{2X}\right)\Err<C\exp\left(-\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}\right)\sqrt{\frac{X^{2}}{X^{2}-(G-H)^{2}}}\exp\left(-(2C_{4})^{-1}\frac{(G-H)^{4}}{X^{3}}\right).

Thus, we further have

\exp\left(-\frac{(G-H)^{2}}{2X}\right)\Err<C\exp\left(-\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}\right).

(108)

On the other hand, we have

1-\frac{((X-H-G)/2)_{G+1}}{((X+H-G)/2+1)_{G+1}}=\sum_{i=0}^{H}\frac{((X-H-G)/2+i+1)_{G+1}-((X-H-G)/2+i)_{G+1}}{((X+H-G)/2+1)_{G+1}}\\
=\sum_{i=0}^{H}\frac{(G+1)((X-H-G)/2+i)_{G}}{((X+H-G)/2+1)_{G+1}}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{H}\frac{G+1}{X}=\frac{(G+1)(H+1)}{X},

so

1-\frac{((X-H-G)/2)_{G+1}}{((X+H-G)/2+1)_{G+1}}\leq 1\wedge\frac{(G+1)(H+1)}{X}<C(1-\exp(-2gh/x)).

(109)

Therefore, by putting together (
108
) and (
109
), we have

2^{-X-1}N^{1/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|<C\exp\left(-\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}\right)(1-\exp(-2gh/x))=C\mathbf{F}(x;h,g),

and the conclusion holds due to (
107
) in this case.

Case 2.
When
x\geq N^{-1/5}
, necessarily
|G-H|\leq G+H<X
, since
h,g<C_{2}N^{1/100}
.
Now we have

\frac{2^{-X-1}N^{1/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|}{\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)}=\frac{\frac{N^{1/3}}{\sqrt{2\pi X}}\exp\left(-\frac{(G-H)^{2}}{2X}\right)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\exp\left(-\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}\right)}\cdot\frac{1-\frac{((X-H-G)/2)_{G+1}}{((X+H-G)/2+1)_{G+1}}}{1-\exp\left(-\frac{2gh}{x}\right)}\cdot\Err.

We are going to show that each factor in the right-hand side is close to
1
.

The right-hand side of (
106
) is smaller than
CN^{-1/10}
, and
|N^{1/3}\sqrt{x/X}-1|<CN^{-2/3+1/5}=CN^{-7/15}
.
Thus

\left|\frac{\frac{N^{1/3}}{\sqrt{2\pi X}}\exp\left(-\frac{(G-H)^{2}}{2X}\right)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi x}}\exp\left(-\frac{(g-h)^{2}}{2x}\right)}-1\right|<CN^{-1/10}.

(110)

On the other hand, we have

\left|\log\left(\frac{((X-H-G)/2)_{G+1}}{((X+H-G)/2+1)_{G+1}}\right)+\frac{2gh}{x}\right|=\left|\sum_{i=0}^{G}\log\left(1-\frac{2H+2}{X+H-G+2i+2}\right)+\frac{2gh}{x}\right|\\
<\left|\frac{2(H+1)(G+1)}{X}-\frac{2gh}{x}\right|+C(H+1)^{2}(G+1)/X^{2}<CN^{-1/3}(gh^{2}/x^{2}+gh/x^{2}+g/x+h/x),

where in the first inequality we used that
\left|\log\left(1-\frac{2H+2}{X+H-G+2i+2}\right)+\frac{2H+2}{X}\right|<\frac{C(H+1)^{2}}{X^{2}}
for each
i\in\llbracket 0,G\rrbracket
.
As a result,

\left|\frac{1-\frac{((X-H-G)/2)_{G+1}}{((X+H-G)/2+1)_{G+1}}}{1-\exp\left(-\frac{2gh}{x}\right)}-1\right|<\frac{\left(\exp(CN^{-1/3}(gh^{2}/x^{2}+gh/x^{2}+g/x+h/x))-1\right)\exp\left(-\frac{2gh}{x}\right)}{1-\exp\left(-\frac{2gh}{x}\right)}\\
<C(N^{-1/10}+N^{-1/3}/h+N^{-1/3}/g).

(111)

Here the last inequality is due to the following reason. Since
x\geq N^{-1/5}
and
h,g<C_{2}N^{1/100}
, we have
N^{-1/3}(g/x+h/x)<N^{-1/100}
.
If
gh/x<N^{1/100}
, we can upper bound the second expression by
\frac{CN^{-1/3}(gh^{2}/x^{2}+gh/x^{2}+g/x+h/x)}{gh/x}
; otherwise, we get the upper bound
C\exp(-cN^{1/50})
.

Besides, using that
x\geq N^{-1/5}
and
h,g<C_{2}N^{1/100}
, we have

1\leq\frac{(G-H)^{4}}{X^{3}}<CN^{-2/3}\frac{(g-h)^{4}}{x^{3}}<CN^{-3/5}x^{-3},\quad\frac{1}{X-|G-H|}<N^{-1/10},

\sqrt{\frac{X^{2}}{X^{2}-(G-H)^{2}}}<1+C\frac{(G-H)^{2}}{X^{2}}<1+CN^{-1/10}.

Therefore, by (
105
), we obtain
|\Err-1|<C(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/10})
.

Finally, the bound on
|\Err-1|
with (
110
) and (
111
) imply that

\left|\frac{2^{-X-1}N^{1/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|}{\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)}-1\right|<C(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/10}+N^{-1/3}/h+N^{-1/3}/g).\qed

Lemma A.6
.

For any
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, there exists a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{1},C_{2})>0
, such that for each
N=1,2,\dots
, and
x,h\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and
X,H\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, satisfying:
C_{1}N^{-2/3}<x<C_{2}
and
C_{1}N^{-1/3}<h<C_{2}N^{1/100}
, and
|X-xN^{2/3}|,|H-hN^{1/3}|<C_{2}
, and
X+H
is even, we have

\left|2^{-X-1}N^{2/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,0)|-\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)\right|\\
<C_{3}\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)\left(1\wedge(N^{-3/5}x^{-3}+N^{-1/10}+N^{-1/3}/h)\right).

Proof.

Recall that

\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)=\frac{2h}{\sqrt{2\pi x^{3}}}\exp\left(-\frac{h^{2}}{2x}\right).

Similarly to the previous proof, we assume that
X
is large enough, and when
H<X
we have

|\mathscr{F}(X;H,0)|=2^{X+1}\frac{2H+2}{X+H+2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi X}}\exp\left(-\frac{H^{2}}{2X}\right)\Theta_{0},

where

\sqrt{\frac{X^{2}}{X^{2}-H^{2}}}\exp\left(-C_{4}\frac{H^{4}}{X^{3}}-\frac{C}{X-H}\right)\\
<\Theta_{0}<\sqrt{\frac{X^{2}}{X^{2}-H^{2}}}\exp\left(-C_{4}^{-1}\frac{H^{4}}{X^{3}}+\frac{C}{X-H}\right),

for some large
C_{4}>0
, depending only on
C_{1},C_{2}
.
We further have

\left|\frac{h^{2}}{2x}-\frac{H^{2}}{2X}\right|<CN^{-1/3}\frac{h}{x}+CN^{-2/3}\frac{h^{2}}{x^{2}}.

(112)

Case 1.
When
x<N^{-1/5}
, it suffices to bound
2^{-X-1}N^{2/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,0)|
by
\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)
, up to a constant factor.
When
H>X
this is obvious, since then
|\mathscr{F}(X;H,0)|=0
. When
H=X
, necessarily
|\mathscr{F}(X;H,0)|=1
, and
|h-N^{1/3}x|<CN^{-1/3}
. Thus, since
h>C_{1}N^{-1/3}
, we have
gh/x>c
,

\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)>cN^{2/3}HX^{-3/2}\exp(-X/2),

and the above stated bound also follows.

Below we assume that
H<X
.
The right-hand side of (
112
) is smaller than

C+(2C_{4})^{-1}\frac{H^{4}}{X^{3}}
,
by taking
C
large.
By plugging this into the upper bound for
\Theta_{0}
, we get that
2^{-X-1}N^{1/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,0)|<C\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)
,
and the conclusion holds.

Case 2.
When
x\geq N^{-1/5}
, necessarily
H<X
since
h<C_{2}N^{1/100}
.
The right-hand side of (
112
) is bounded by
CN^{-1/10}
.
We have
|\Theta_{0}-1|<C(N^{-2/3}x^{-3}+N^{-1/10})
in this case, and
\left|\frac{2H}{X+H+2}\cdot\frac{xN^{1/3}}{2h}-1\right|<C(N^{-1/10}+N^{-1/3}/h)
.
Thus, the conclusion also holds.
∎

We also record without proof the following asymptotics of Calatan numbers.

Lemma A.7
.

For any
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, there exists a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{1},C_{2})>0
, such that for each
N=1,2,\dots
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and
X\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, satisfying:
C_{1}N^{-2/3}<x<C_{2}
and
|X-xN^{2/3}|<C_{2}
, and
X
is even, we have

\left|2^{-X-1}N|\mathscr{F}(X;0,0)|-\mathbf{F}_{0,0}(x)\right|<C_{3}N^{-2/3}x^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{0,0}(x).

A.3
Coupling between walks and Brownian bridges

We next establish that a uniformly random element from
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
is close to a corresponding Brownian bridge.
For this, we need the following statement.

Lemma A.8
.

Take any
X,H,G,H^{\prime},G^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, with both
X+H+G
and
X+H^{\prime}+G^{\prime}
even, and
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}:\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, uniformly random from
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
and
\mathscr{F}(X;H^{\prime},G^{\prime})
respectively. We can couple them so that almost surely,
|\mathfrak{f}-\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}|\leq\max\{|G-G^{\prime}|,|H-H^{\prime}|\}
on
\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket
.

Take any
x,h,g,h^{\prime},g^{\prime}>0
, and Brownian bridges
B
and
B^{\prime}
on
[0,x]
with
B(0)=h
,
B(x)=g
, conditional on
B>0
; and
B^{\prime}(0)=h^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime}(x)=g^{\prime}
, conditional on
B^{\prime}>0
. We can couple them so that almost surely,
|B-B^{\prime}|\leq\max\{|g-g^{\prime}|,|h-h^{\prime}|\}
on
[0,x]
.

This statement is in a similar spirit as
Lemma

A.2
, and can also be proved using Monte-Carlo Markov chains.
See Lemmas 2.6 and 2.7 in
[
CH14
]
and their proofs.

Our main coupling statement is as follows.

Lemma A.9
.

For any
C_{1}>0
and
C_{2}>1
, there is a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(C_{1},C_{2})>0
, such that the following is true for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Take any
C_{1}N^{-2/3}<x<(C_{2}/2)^{10}
and
0\leq h,g<(C_{2}-x^{1/10})N^{1/100}
, and
X,H,G\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, such that
|X-xN^{2/3}|,|H-hN^{1/3}|,|G-gN^{1/3}|<C_{2}
, and
X+H+G
is even.
Take
\mathfrak{f}:\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
uniformly random from
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
, and a Brownian bridge
B
on
[0,x]
with
B(0)=h
,
B(x)=g
, conditional on
B\geq 0
. Then we can couple
\mathfrak{f}
and
B
together, such that with probability
>1-C_{3}\log(X)XN^{-7/10}
, we have
|\mathfrak{f}(Y)-N^{1/3}B(y)|<C_{3}\log(N)N^{3/10}+C_{3}\log(X)N^{1/10}
for all
Y\in\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket
and
y\in[0,x]
with
|Y-yN^{2/3}|<1
.

Proof.

In this proof we use
C,c>0
to denote constants that may depend on
C_{1},C_{2}
.
We shall take
C_{3}
to be large enough depending on
C_{1},C_{2}
and all
C,c
, and we assume without loss of generality that
N
is large enough depending on
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
and all
C,c
.

We prove by an induction in
X
.

For the base case, we consider
X<N^{3/5}
.
Take any
U,W\in\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket
,
U<W
, and let
V=\lfloor(U+W)/2\rfloor
or
V=\lceil(U+W)/2\rceil
.
Similarly to the proof of
Claim

A.4
, we have that

\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathfrak{f}(U)+\mathfrak{f}(W)-2\mathfrak{f}(V)|>\frac{1}{10}(W-U)^{1/2}\log(N)\right)<C\exp(-c(\log(N))^{2}).

Now we take
U,W
to be
\lfloor a2^{-b}X\rfloor,\lfloor(a+1)2^{-b}X\rfloor
, for any
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
a\in\llbracket 0,2^{b}-1\rrbracket
, such that
U<W
.
By a union bound, we have that with probability
>1-C\exp(-c(\log(N))^{2})
, for all such
U,V,W
we have
|\mathfrak{f}(U)+\mathfrak{f}(W)-2\mathfrak{f}(V)|\leq 0.1(W-U)^{1/2}\log(N)
.
This, in particular, implies that

\mathbb{P}[|\mathfrak{f}(Y)-H-(G-H)Y/X|<X^{1/2}\log(N),\;\forall Y\in\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket]>1-C\exp(-c(\log(N))^{2}).

(113)

Similarly we have that

\mathbb{P}[|B(y)-h-(g-h)y/x|<x^{1/2}\log(N),\;\forall y\in\llbracket 0,x\rrbracket]>1-C\exp(-c(\log(N))^{2}).

(114)

Therefore the statement holds.

For the induction step, now we assume that
X\geq N^{3/5}
, and that the conclusion holds for any smaller
X
.
The idea is to first couple the random variables
\mathfrak{f}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)
and
N^{1/3}B(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})
and then couple distributions conditional on the values of these variables.

We first deduce that

\mathbb{P}\left(N^{-1/3}\mathfrak{f}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)\geq\left(C_{2}-\frac{99}{100}x^{1/10}\right)N^{1/100}\right)<CN^{-1/10},

(115)

when both
h,g<(C_{2}-x^{1/10})N^{1/100}
.
By
Lemma

A.2
it suffices to consider the case where
H=\lceil(C_{2}-x^{1/10})N^{1/3+1/100}+C_{2}\rceil
, and
G=H
or
H+1
(depending on the parity of
X
).
In this case we can estimate the number of walks in
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
that is
>\big(C_{2}-\frac{99}{100}x^{1/10}\big)N^{1/3+1/100}
at
\lfloor X/2\rfloor
, using
Lemma

A.1
(i), and get an upper bound of
C2^{X}\exp(-cx^{1/5}N^{1/50})<C2^{X}\exp(-cN^{1/150})
;
and by reflection principle we can lower bound
|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|
by
cX^{-1/2}2^{X}
.
Then their ratio is
<CN^{-1/10}
.

We now couple
\mathfrak{f}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)
and
N^{1/3}B(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})
so that with probability
>1-CN^{-1/10}
, they differ by
\leq N^{1/10}
.

We take
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
uniformly sampled from
\mathscr{F}(X;H+\lfloor N^{1/10}/3\rfloor,G+\lfloor N^{1/10}/3\rfloor)
, and a Brownian bridge
B^{\prime}
on
[0,x]
with
B^{\prime}(0)=h+N^{-7/30}/3
,
B^{\prime}(x)=g+N^{-7/30}/3
, conditional on
B^{\prime}>0
.
Take any
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
X+H+\lfloor N^{1/10}/3\rfloor+\lfloor X/2\rfloor+i
is even, and
i<C_{2}N^{1/3+1/100}
.
The probability
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)=i]
equals

\frac{|\mathscr{F}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor;H+\lfloor N^{1/10}/3\rfloor,i)|\cdot|\mathscr{F}(\lceil X/2\rceil;i,G+\lfloor N^{1/10}/3\rfloor)|}{|\mathscr{F}(X;H+\lfloor N^{1/10}/3\rfloor,G+\lfloor N^{1/10}/3\rfloor)|}.

By
Lemma

A.5
, this equals

2N^{-1/3}\frac{\mathbf{F}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3};h+N^{-7/30},\tau)\mathbf{F}(x-\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3};\tau,g+N^{-7/30})}{\mathbf{F}(x;h+N^{-7/30},g+N^{-7/30})}\Phi,

(116)

for any
\tau>0
with
|i-\tau N^{1/3}|\leq 2
,
where
\Phi
is a number satisfying

|\Phi-1|<C(1\wedge(N^{-1/10}+N^{-1/3}/\tau)).

(117)

On the other hand, the probability density of
B(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})
at
\tau
is precisely the ratio in (
116
).
Thus, if we consider
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)
and
2\lfloor N^{1/3}B^{\prime}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})/2\rfloor
or
2\lfloor N^{1/3}B^{\prime}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})/2\rfloor+1
(depending on the parity), the total variation distance between their distributions is bounded by

\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}\lfloor X/2\rfloor\geq C_{2}N^{1/3+1/100}]\\
+\int_{0}^{C_{2}N^{1/100}+N^{-1/3}}\frac{\mathbf{F}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3};h+N^{-7/30},\tau)\mathbf{F}(x-\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3};\tau,g+N^{-7/30})}{\mathbf{F}(x;h+N^{-7/30},g+N^{-7/30})}|\Phi-1|\mathrm{d}\tau.

(118)

By (
115
), the first term is bounded by
CN^{-1/10}
.
As for the integral, for
\tau<x^{1/2}N^{-1/10}
we can bound the ratio by
Cx^{-1/2}
, and
|\Phi-1|
by
C
; so the integral for such
\tau
is bounded by
CN^{-1/10}
. For
\tau>x^{1/2}N^{-1/10}
, we have
\tau>cN^{-2/15}
, and therefore,
|\Phi-1|<CN^{-1/10}
, so we get an upper bound of
CN^{-1/10}
times the integral of the ratio.
Note that since the ratio is precisely the probability density of
B(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})
at
\tau
, its integral for
\tau
from
0
to
\infty
equals
1
.
Thus, the integral in (
118
) is bounded by
CN^{-1/10}
.

In other words, we can couple
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
so that with probability
>1-CN^{-1/10}
, we have

|\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)-N^{1/3}B^{\prime}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})|\leq 2.

We then couple
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
using
Lemma

A.8
so that almost surely they differ by
\leq N^{1/10}/3
; similarly we couple
B
and
B^{\prime}
using
Lemma

A.8
so that almost surely they differ by
\leq N^{-7/30}/3
.
Therefore, we have
|\mathfrak{f}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)-N^{1/3}B(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})|\leq N^{1/10}
with probability
>1-CN^{-1/10}
.

We then use the induction hypothesis for
\mathfrak{f}
on
\llbracket 0,\lfloor X/2\rfloor\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\lfloor X/2\rfloor,X\rrbracket
respectively, conditional on
\mathfrak{f}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)
and
B(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})
.
Note that here we only couple them so that (with probability
>1-CN^{-1/10}
) they differ by
\leq N^{1/10}
, rather than
\leq C_{2}
.
Therefore to use the induction hypothesis, we need one more procedure.

We consider Brownian bridges
B_{-}
and
B_{+}
on
[0,\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3}]
and
[\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3},x]
respectively, with boundary conditions
B_{-}(0)=h
,
B_{-}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})=N^{-1/3}\mathfrak{f}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)
, and
B_{+}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})=N^{-1/3}\mathfrak{f}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)
,
B_{+}(x)=g
, conditional on
B_{-},B_{+}>0
.
From the bound (
115
) and that
(C_{2}-\frac{99}{100}x^{1/10})N^{1/100}
smaller than either
(C_{2}-(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})^{1/10})N^{1/100}
or
(C_{2}-(x-\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})^{1/10})N^{1/100}
, we have that
with probability
>1-CN^{-1/10}
,
we can couple
B_{-}
and
B_{+}
with
\mathfrak{f}
on the two intervals respectively, using the induction hypothesis.
We then couple
B_{-}
and
B_{+}
with
B
on
[0,\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3}]
and
[\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3},x]
respectively, conditional on
B(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})
, using
Lemma

A.8
.
Under the event that
|\mathfrak{f}(\lfloor X/2\rfloor)-N^{1/3}B(\lfloor X/2\rfloor N^{-2/3})|\leq N^{1/10}
, we have that
B
and
B_{-}
,
B_{+}
differ by
\leq N^{-7/30}
almost surely.

Finally, using that
CN^{-1/10}+C_{3}\log(\lceil X/2\rceil)XN^{-7/10}<C_{3}\log(X)XN^{-7/10}
, and that
C_{3}\log(\lceil X/2\rceil)N^{1/10}+N^{1/10}<C_{3}\log(X)N^{1/10}
, we see that the coupling satisfies the requirements.
Thus the conclusion follows by the principle of induction.
∎

Remark A.10
.

The bound in
Lemma

A.9
is stated in this way for the convenience of the proof by induction. It is straightforward to see that, under the same coupling, with probability
>1-N^{-1/31}
we have
|N^{-1/3}\mathfrak{f}(Y)-B(y)|<N^{-1/31}
for all
Y\in\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket
and
y\in[0,x]
with
|Y-yN^{2/3}|<1
.

Finally, we use this coupling to prove
Lemma

4.40
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.40
.

In this proof we use
C,c>0
to denote constants that may depend on
C_{1},C_{2}
, and we take
C_{3}
to be large enough depending on all
C,c

We first prove (1).
Take
\mathfrak{f}:\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
uniformly random from
\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)
, and a Brownian bridge
B
on
[0,x]
with
B(0)=h
,
B(x)=g
, conditional on
B>0
; and couple them using
Lemma

A.9
.

Note that when
H\geq G
, each up-step of the path has a matching down-step, except for
H-G
up-steps. Thus, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)}{\beta N}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)+1\end{subarray}}\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)-2}{\beta N}\right)+\sum_{i=G}^{H-1}\log\left(1+\frac{2i}{\beta N}\right);

and when
H<G
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)}{\beta N}\right)+\sum_{i=H}^{G-1}\log\left(1+\frac{2i}{\beta N}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)+1\end{subarray}}\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)-2}{\beta N}\right).

Thus (since
G,H<CN^{1/3}
) we have

\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)}{\beta N}\right)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)+1\end{subarray}}\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)-2}{\beta N}\right)\right|<CN^{-1/3}.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event where
\max\mathfrak{f},\max B<N^{1/2}
, and
|N^{-1/3}\mathfrak{f}(t)-B(y)|<N^{-1/31}
for all
t\in\llbracket 0,X\rrbracket
and
y\in[0,x]
with
|t-yN^{2/3}|<1
.
Then under
\mathcal{E}
, for each
t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket
with
t<xN^{2/3}
, we have

\left|\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)+\iota}{\beta N}\right)-2\beta^{-1}\int_{(t-1)N^{-2/3}}^{tN^{-2/3}}B(y)\mathrm{d}y\right|<CN^{-2/3-1/31},

where
\iota=0
or
-2
.
Thus, under
\mathcal{E}
, we have

\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\log\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)}{\beta N}\right)-\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{x}B(y)\mathrm{d}y\right|<CN^{-1/31},

which implies that

\left|\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)}{\beta N}\right)-\exp\left(\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{x}B(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)\right|<CN^{-1/31}\exp\left(\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{x}B(y)\mathrm{d}y\right).

(119)

On the other hand, using the same arguments as deriving (
113
) and (
114
) in the proof of
Lemma

A.9
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\max\mathfrak{f}>mN^{1/3})<C\exp(-cm^{2})
and
\mathbb{P}(\max B>m)<C\exp(-cm^{2})
, for any
m>0
.
Thus,

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{x}B(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)\right]<\int_{0}^{\infty}C\exp(-cm^{2})\exp(Cm)\mathrm{d}m<C,

(120)

and
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]<CN^{-1/40}
(where
\mathcal{E}^{c}
denotes the complement of
\mathcal{E}
), and

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)}{\beta N}\right)\right]<\int_{0}^{\infty}C(\exp(-cm^{2})\wedge\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{c}])\exp(Cm)\mathrm{d}m<C\log(N)N^{-1/31},

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]\exp\left(\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{x}B(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)\right]<\int_{0}^{\infty}C(\exp(-cm^{2})\wedge\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{c}])\exp(Cm)\mathrm{d}m<C\log(N)N^{-1/31}.

These two bounds together with (
119
) and (
120
) imply that

\left|\mathbb{E}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\llbracket 1,X\rrbracket:\\
\mathfrak{f}(t)=\mathfrak{f}(t-1)-1\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{2\mathfrak{f}(t)}{\beta N}\right)-\mathbb{E}\exp\left(\beta^{-1}\int_{0}^{x}B(y)\mathrm{d}y\right)\right|<CN^{-1/40}.

This leads to

\left|N^{1/3}I(X;H,G)-\frac{\mathbf{I}(x;h,g)\cdot 2^{-X-1}N^{1/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|}{\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)}\right|<CN^{-1/40}\mathbf{F}(x;h,g),

using that
2^{-X-1}N^{1/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,G)|<C\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)
, which can be deduced from
Lemma

A.5
.
Applying
Lemma

A.5
again to the left-hand side, and using that
\mathbf{I}(x;h,g)<C\mathbf{F}(x;h,g)
, the estimate (1) follows.

For (2), (3), and (4), we similarly apply
Lemma

A.9
and
Lemmas

A.6
and
A.7
to deduce that

\left|N^{2/3}I(X;H,0)-\frac{\mathbf{I}_{0}(x;h)\cdot 2^{-X-1}N^{2/3}|\mathscr{F}(X;H,0)|}{\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h)}\right|<CN^{-1/40}\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;h),

and

\left|N^{2/3}I^{+}(X;H,G)-\frac{\exp(-\beta^{-1}xh)\mathbf{I}_{0}(x;g-h)\cdot 2^{-X-1}N^{2/3}|\mathscr{F}^{+}(X;H,G)|}{\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;g-h)}\right|\\
<CN^{-1/40}\mathbf{F}_{0}(x;g-h),

and

\left|NI^{+}(X;H,H)-\frac{\exp(-\beta^{-1}xh)\mathbf{I}^{\epsilon}_{0}(x;h)\cdot 2^{-X-1}N|\mathscr{F}^{+}(X;H,H)|}{\mathbf{F}_{0,0}(x)}\right|<CN^{-1/40}\mathbf{F}_{0,0}(x).

These together with
Lemmas

A.6
and
A.7
imply (2)–(4) of
Lemma

4.40
.
∎
